**Allotment rule:** A membership change triggers a complete reallotment. No Justice inherits a predecessor's circuits. Under the Composition register, Stone reassesses the entire map for institutional effectiveness; an express administrative order may also change an allotment.

### Standing Practices

**Referral of applications, effective October 7, 1991.** By the Court's internal practice adopted at the opening of October Term 1991, an application for a stay, injunction, or other interim relief in a matter on the Term's inventory is referred by the Circuit Justice to the full Court and decided by the participating Justices under the participating-majority rule. The order issues in the Court's name and recites the presenting Circuit Justice ("presented to Justice ___ and by him or her referred to the Court"). It ordinarily issues without opinion; a brief per curiam and noted dissents are available. A Justice may participate in a referred application from the date of the judicial oath. An application not on the Term's inventory remains subject to ordinary Circuit Justice practice. Source: opening Standing State, section 3. The practice continues unchanged into OT1994.

## Current law

### United States v. Robertson, No. 94-251 — 1995-05-01

Authority: [Record](../records/United_States_v_Robertson_merits_1995-05-01.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### The mine’s own interstate operations satisfy the enacted enterprise-commerce condition

**Controlling proposition:** An enterprise is engaged in interstate commerce under §1962(a) when it directly participates in interstate acquisition, production or distribution of goods or services. This mine’s actual cross-border procurement, repeated interstate recruitment and mine-work movement, together with its proprietor’s carriage out of Alaska of approximately $30,000 of its own output, establish that connection without a separate showing that this individual mine substantially affected interstate commerce.

**Authority:** Justice Breyer’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: nine direct joins in the rule, cumulative application and bounded remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that this mine participated in interstate commerce itself. American Building Maintenance distinguishes direct acquisition, production or distribution across state lines from local purchases of goods whose earlier interstate journey has ended; Gulf Oil likewise keeps direct engagement distinct from local activity with interstate effects. The mine obtained some equipment in California for use in Alaska, repeatedly brought workers across state lines for mining, and its sole proprietor carried a meaningful amount of its gold outside Alaska. Calling the enterprise local mining and local sales does not erase these transactions. The output’s mining origin and its carriage by the proprietor, considered with the continuing interstate operations, answer the attribution objection here without an out-of-state sale or a rule attributing every private owner act to the enterprise. Lopez rejects the unbounded chain from local wrongdoing to national economic consequences; this prosecution needs no such chain because the enacted connection is proved. The Court does not decide the separate affecting-commerce alternative, whether it requires substantial effects, or the sufficiency of retained-output carriage standing alone.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. American Building Maintenance Industries, 422 U.S. 271, 283–286 (1975): applies its distinction between direct interstate goods or services transactions and local purchases after interstate movement has ended; its Clayton Act setting does not erase RICO’s separate affecting-commerce alternative.
- Gulf Oil Corp. v. Copp Paving Co., 419 U.S. 186, 195 (1974): applies its direct-engagement distinction; local economic effects and direct participation remain different inquiries.
- United States v. Lopez, April 26, 1995: distinguishes the stand-alone school-possession offense and generalized social-cost chain from this proved interstate enterprise connection; its bounded holding remains intact.
- Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, January 18, 1995: preserves attention to actual multistate commercial operation and its reserved constitutional ceiling; the FAA’s broader involving-commerce wording is not substituted for §1962(a).
- United States v. Harris, April 27, 1995: preserves the economic-class vehicle-taking holding and refusal to make remote vehicle history alone conclusive; this decision instead concerns the enterprise’s own present interstate operations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The cumulative ground makes an independently sufficient procurement-only holding unnecessary. The Court does not decide whether a purely intrastate enterprise with only remote economic consequences satisfies §1962(a), establish a universal owner-activity attribution rule, or make a jurisdictional phrase substitute for proof. Predicate drug commerce alone is not enterprise commerce. Neither an out-of-state nugget sale, a precise output percentage, productive use of the wrecked Cadillac, mine use of the airplane, nor traced downstream refinery shipment is necessary or found. The Court decides no other RICO appellate claim, Guidelines issue or claim arising under a different RICO subsection.

#### Precedent Treatment

American Building Maintenance and Gulf Oil supply the direct-engagement distinction; their narrower statutory settings remain identified. Actual interstate operation controls this application. Allied-Bruce, Lopez and Harris retain their stated scopes. The commerce condition remains independent under National Organization for Women v. Scheidler; Reves’s direction requirement for §1962(c) is not imported into §1962(a). Holmes’s direct-injury requirement for civil RICO damages does not determine this criminal enterprise-commerce question. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Section 1962(a)’s direct-engagement alternative is satisfied by the proved cumulative interstate operation of this mine. A separate substantial-effects showing for this individual enterprise is unnecessary under that alternative. The separate affecting-commerce route and its constitutional reach remain undecided. The decision adds no independently sufficient procurement-only rule and no rule making every proprietor’s private conduct enterprise activity. Lopez’s negative school-possession holding and Harris’s distinct economic-class holding remain unchanged; the Chief Justice’s complete six-route account in Lopez does not become a Court test through this decision.

Section 1962(a)’s separate income, participation and investment conditions remain intact. Its securities qualification continues to exclude an open-market investment purchase made without intent to control or participate in control of the issuer or assist another in doing so, when the purchaser’s, immediate family’s and racketeering or unlawful-debt accomplices’ aggregate holdings after purchase are less than one percent of any outstanding class and confer no power, in law or fact, to elect a director. That qualification is not implicated by ownership and operation of this mine.



The public holdings, precedent treatment and present force below are copied from completed Records. Records control; no provisional commitment or separate writing changes law. Their effective dates and exact coalition limits remain operative.

### United States v. Shabani; No. 93-981; 513 U.S. 10 — 1994-11-01

Authority: [Record](../records/United_States_v_Shabani_merits_1994-11-01.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### Section 846 requires the unlawful agreement, without a further overt act

**Controlling proposition:** Section 846 of Title 21 requires neither an allegation nor proof of an overt act beyond the unlawful narcotics agreement. The Government must still prove the unlawful agreement and the defendant’s knowing participation beyond a reasonable doubt; neither mere association nor awareness of another person’s plan is enough.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine directly adopt this construction and its application; no fractured-opinion analysis is necessary.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress made the unlawful agreement the completed conspiracy offense. Section 846 contains no additional overt-act language, while 18 U.S.C. §371 expressly requires an act to effect the conspiracy’s object. Nash supplies the common-law understanding of conspiracy, and Singer shows why Congress’s omission of an extra act requirement matters when it uses that settled term. This reading does not punish a thought: the agreement itself is conduct, and the Government must prove knowing participation in it. The Ninth Circuit’s contrary element cannot rest on circuit precedent when the governing statute supplies none. Felix’s treatment of successive prosecutions did not decide this statutory question and cannot add the omitted element. Nor does lenity supply it; ordinary construction resolves the question without the residual ambiguity addressed in Ratzlaf. The refused overt-act instruction therefore supplies no ground for reversal of the conviction. The Court leaves the appeal’s reserved sufficiency and other claims to the court of appeals.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *Nash v. United States*, 229 U.S. 373 (1913), applies the common-law meaning under which the agreement completes conspiracy without a further overt act.
- *Singer v. United States*, 323 U.S. 338 (1945), applies the significance of Congress’s omission of an additional overt-act requirement from a statutory conspiracy offense.
- *United States v. Felix*, 503 U.S. 378 (1992), remains a double-jeopardy decision distinguishing agreement from substantive conduct; it did not decide §846’s overt-act element. Its narrower separate position likewise supplies no contrary statutory element.
- *Ratzlaf v. United States*, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), preserves lenity for genuine ambiguity remaining after ordinary construction; that condition is absent here. Its distinct willfulness rule is unchanged.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal rule for every conspiracy statute is announced. The Court does not decide credibility, evidentiary sufficiency, the remaining appellate objections or the ultimate validity of the conviction. Current double-jeopardy law is unchanged.

- *Nash v. United States*, 229 U.S. 373 (1913), applies the common-law meaning under which the agreement completes conspiracy without a further overt act.
- *Singer v. United States*, 323 U.S. 338 (1945), applies the significance of Congress’s omission of an additional overt-act requirement from a statutory conspiracy offense.
- *United States v. Felix*, 503 U.S. 378 (1992), remains a double-jeopardy decision distinguishing agreement from substantive conduct; it did not decide §846’s overt-act element. Its narrower separate position likewise supplies no contrary statutory element.
- *Ratzlaf v. United States*, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), preserves lenity for genuine ambiguity remaining after ordinary construction; that condition is absent here. Its distinct willfulness rule is unchanged.

Effective November 1, 1994, §846 conspiracy requires the unlawful agreement and knowing participation, but no further overt act in allegation or proof. This adds an offense-specific statutory construction. It does not alter the knowledge requirements of other crimes, create a universal rule about omitted statutory language, or change Felix, Dixon, Grady or Ratzlaf beyond the distinctions stated above.

### U.S. Bancorp Mortgage Co. v. Bonner Mall Partnership; No. 93-714; 513 U.S. 18 — 1994-11-08

Authority: [Record](../records/US_Bancorp_Mortgage_Co_v_Bonner_Mall_Partnership_mootness_vacatur_1994-11-08.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### Mootness ends merits review but leaves authority to dispose of the judgment

**Controlling proposition:** When a judgment has been lawfully brought before the Court for review, subsequent mootness bars adjudication of the underlying merits but does not eliminate the Court’s authority under 28 U.S.C. §2106 to make a just disposition of that judgment and the pending proceeding. Section 2106 permits appropriate relief; it does not require vacatur merely because review has become moot.

**Authority:** Justice Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine directly adopt this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that ending a controversy and disposing of the pending review are distinct judicial acts. Section 2106 authorizes a reviewing court to affirm, modify, vacate, set aside or reverse a judgment lawfully before it, and to direct further proceedings as justice requires. Walling recognizes the authority to make an appropriate disposition even when merits review cannot continue. Munsingwear likewise rests on power to address the consequences of a judgment that has become unreviewable. A categorical rule stripping all dispositional authority after mootness would contradict that practice. Article III still forbids an advisory ruling on the bankruptcy dispute; the Court therefore does not decide whether the new-value doctrine survives or whether a former proposed plan could be confirmed. Kokkonen concerns jurisdiction over a separate contract-enforcement controversy after dismissal, which no party obtains here. The existing review supplies the occasion for considering vacatur; the parties’ consent does not create jurisdiction or dictate the equitable result.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *Walling v. James V. Reuter, Inc.*, 321 U.S. 671 (1944), applies the distinction between inability to complete merits review and authority to make an appropriate disposition of the judgments before the Court.
- *United States v. Munsingwear, Inc.*, 340 U.S. 36 (1950), confirms §2106 dispositional authority when appellate merits review becomes unavailable; it does not make every available disposition mandatory.
- *Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America*, 511 U.S. 375 (1994), is distinguished: jurisdiction over a new settlement-enforcement controversy is different from disposition of a judgment already lawfully under review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court neither enforces the settlement nor supplies a ruling on bankruptcy priority, plan confirmation or the former automatic-stay dispute.

### Voluntary settlement ordinarily does not justify vacatur

**Controlling proposition:** A party that voluntarily settles while pursuing review does not ordinarily establish equitable entitlement to vacatur of the judgment it sought to overturn; the movant must show a further sufficient equitable reason. Exceptional settlement circumstances remain possible, but a settlement term calling for vacatur is not itself such a circumstance; Bancorp’s showing establishes none, so vacatur is denied.

**Authority:** Justice Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine directly adopt this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a losing party’s voluntary surrender of review ordinarily leaves the existing judgment in place. Munsingwear protects against an unreviewable judgment when review fails through happenstance; Karcher shows why a party’s own decision to end review receives different treatment. Bancorp accepted a consensual resolution while review remained available and has identified no further equitable reason to erase the appellate judgment. Settlement is lawful and valuable, but that value does not turn a completed judicial act into a term the litigants can remove by agreement. The judgment also has a public significance beyond their immediate bargain. Exceptional circumstances may justify relief in another settlement case; a clause requesting vacatur alone merely repeats the private preference and cannot supply the missing equity. This is no heightened evidentiary test or finding of settlement misconduct. The Court denies the requested alteration and dismisses this proceeding as moot, without affirming the Ninth Circuit’s bankruptcy reasoning or determining the judgment’s effect on every outsider.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *United States v. Munsingwear, Inc.*, 340 U.S. 36 (1950), preserves equitable relief when review is frustrated by happenstance; its actual refusal to erase an unchallenged judgment does not create automatic vacatur after settlement.
- *Karcher v. May*, 484 U.S. 72 (1987), extends its reason for denying vacatur after voluntary abandonment of review to the voluntary settlement here, while preserving its distinct party-capacity setting.
- *Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp.*, 510 U.S. 27 (1993), remains a threshold decision that left settlement vacatur undecided. Its separate vacatur discussion supplies no earlier controlling rule.
- *Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc.*, 511 U.S. 863 (1994), remains a collateral-order decision; it does not establish a private right to erase a judgment following settlement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Mootness caused by circumstances beyond the movant’s choice, including unilateral action of the prevailing party, remains distinct. No categorical ban on exceptional settlement relief or universal rule of collateral estoppel is adopted. No Rule 60(b) relief or remand to seek it is ordered.

- *Walling v. James V. Reuter, Inc.*, 321 U.S. 671 (1944), applies the distinction between inability to complete merits review and authority to make an appropriate disposition of the judgments before the Court.
- *United States v. Munsingwear, Inc.*, 340 U.S. 36 (1950), confirms §2106 dispositional authority when appellate merits review becomes unavailable; it does not make every available disposition mandatory.
- *Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America*, 511 U.S. 375 (1994), is distinguished: jurisdiction over a new settlement-enforcement controversy is different from disposition of a judgment already lawfully under review.
- *United States v. Munsingwear, Inc.*, 340 U.S. 36 (1950), preserves equitable relief when review is frustrated by happenstance; its actual refusal to erase an unchallenged judgment does not create automatic vacatur after settlement.
- *Karcher v. May*, 484 U.S. 72 (1987), extends its reason for denying vacatur after voluntary abandonment of review to the voluntary settlement here, while preserving its distinct party-capacity setting.
- *Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp.*, 510 U.S. 27 (1993), remains a threshold decision that left settlement vacatur undecided. Its separate vacatur discussion supplies no earlier controlling rule.
- *Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc.*, 511 U.S. 863 (1994), remains a collateral-order decision; it does not establish a private right to erase a judgment following settlement.

Effective November 8, 1994, §2106 continues to authorize a proper disposition after mootness, and voluntary settlement ordinarily weighs against vacatur absent a further sufficient equitable reason. The Court adds this settlement-vacatur rule to the prior mootness framework; Izumi had left it unresolved. Exceptional relief is reserved, without a heightened proof standard. Bankruptcy new-value doctrine, settlement enforcement, collateral-order review and the merits of the judgment below remain unchanged by this disposition.

### Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., including Walsh; No. 93-1197; 513 U.S. 30 — 1994-11-14

Authority: [Record](../records/Hess_v_Port_Authority_Trans_Hudson_Corp_merits_1994-11-14.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### PATH’s actual compact and financial structure does not confer the States’ immunity

**Controlling proposition:** A bistate compact entity does not acquire the States’ Eleventh Amendment immunity merely by exercising governmental power: its actual legal, financial and institutional relationship must supply good reason to conclude that the States structured it as their arm and Congress concurred, with state-treasury exposure highly probative but not categorically indispensable. PATH is a distinct, financially self-supporting enterprise responsible for its obligations; these employee suits impose no judgment obligation on either State and do not treat either State itself as a defendant, so PATH lacks that immunity.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Six of nine participants directly support this entity-specific rule and its application to both employee actions. O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas dissent; no fragmented-rationale analysis is needed.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that PATH’s actual relationship to New York and New Jersey does not make these suits actions against the States. Lake Country Estates requires examining the compact structure, and Mt. Healthy distinguishes separate public entities from the State itself. Gubernatorial appointment and veto, legislative restrictions and public transportation responsibilities favor PATH’s position, but do not overcome this enterprise’s separate identity, revenues, assets and obligations. Article XV requires equal state appropriations only while operating revenues are inadequate, only for salaries, office and other administrative expenses, in amounts recommended by the Authority and approved by both governors, with each State obligated only up to $100,000 annually. It is no promise to pay judgments. Financial independence therefore carries great weight in this mixed setting, without making treasury liability immunity’s sole condition. Feeney assumed status when deciding consent; it did not resolve the antecedent question now presented. Because PATH is not an immune arm, its conditional consent is unnecessary to overcome an Eleventh Amendment bar. The Court leaves ordinary defenses and liability for the alleged injuries to proceedings on remand.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *Lake Country Estates, Inc. v. Tahoe Regional Planning Agency*, 440 U.S. 391 (1979), applies its entity-specific compact inquiry: exercising state power does not itself make a bistate corporation the States, and the compact’s actual structure matters.
- *Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*, 429 U.S. 274 (1977), preserves the distinction between a separate public entity and an arm of a State; a governmental function alone supplies no immunity.
- *Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp. v. Feeney*, 495 U.S. 299 (1990), is distinguished as deciding federal-forum consent while assuming, rather than deciding, PATH’s arm status. Its consent ruling is not an earlier holding that PATH is immune.
- *Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission*, 502 U.S. 197 (1991), preserves state-court FELA coverage without constructive waiver of federal-forum immunity; no abrogation or waiver rule is enlarged here.
- *Puerto Rico Aqueduct & Sewer Authority v. Metcalf & Eddy, Inc.*, 506 U.S. 139 (1993), preserves immediate review of a qualifying immunity denial without establishing that a particular entity is an immune arm. Its appealability holding does not decide PATH’s status.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not declare every compact entity nonimmune, make a debt disclaimer alone dispositive, decide a materially different entity’s practical dependence, abolish state sovereign immunity or hold that FELA abrogates federal-forum immunity. Negligence, causation, damages, the Boiler Inspection Act merits, every independently applicable nonimmunity limitations defense and hypothetical consent exceptions remain undecided.

- *Lake Country Estates, Inc. v. Tahoe Regional Planning Agency*, 440 U.S. 391 (1979), applies its entity-specific compact inquiry: exercising state power does not itself make a bistate corporation the States, and the compact’s actual structure matters.
- *Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*, 429 U.S. 274 (1977), preserves the distinction between a separate public entity and an arm of a State; a governmental function alone supplies no immunity.
- *Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp. v. Feeney*, 495 U.S. 299 (1990), is distinguished as deciding federal-forum consent while assuming, rather than deciding, PATH’s arm status. Its consent ruling is not an earlier holding that PATH is immune.
- *Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission*, 502 U.S. 197 (1991), preserves state-court FELA coverage without constructive waiver of federal-forum immunity; no abrogation or waiver rule is enlarged here.
- *Puerto Rico Aqueduct & Sewer Authority v. Metcalf & Eddy, Inc.*, 506 U.S. 139 (1993), preserves immediate review of a qualifying immunity denial without establishing that a particular entity is an immune arm. Its appealability holding does not decide PATH’s status.

Effective November 14, 1994, PATH is not an arm entitled to the States’ Eleventh Amendment immunity from these employee actions. Lake Country’s compact inquiry is clarified through the actual separate legal and financial structure, with treasury exposure carrying great weight but no universal sole-treasury test. The Third Circuit’s contrary PATH-status rule cannot sustain these dismissals. FELA coverage, consent of an entity that actually is immune, immunity abrogation and collateral-order appealability remain separate and unchanged.

### United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc.; No. 93-723; 513 U.S. 64 — 1994-11-29

Authority: [Record](../records/United_States_v_X_Citement_Video_Inc_merits_1994-11-29.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### The enacted knowledge term reaches minority and sexually explicit character

**Controlling proposition:** To convict under 18 U.S.C. §§2252(a)(1) or (2), the Government must prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the defendant knew the depiction’s sexually explicit character and knew the performer was a minor, as well as the applicable knowing handling act. It need not prove knowledge of the statute, illegality or the precise legal age definition; the actual statutory production, depiction and commerce-or-mail conditions remain distinct, and the Court does not decide whether knowledge of the jurisdictional nexus is required.

**Authority:** Part I of Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer. Seven Justices directly adopt the factual-knowledge construction and its reasoning. Scalia and Thomas dissent.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that knowingly reaches the factual features that make these depictions criminal. Morissette supplies the presumption of culpability, applied to the whole statutory setting rather than confined by the distance between the adverb and the later conditions. Staples supports requiring awareness of the characteristics that distinguish a prohibited object from an otherwise familiar one, while Posters preserves the difference between factual knowledge and legal classification. Neither decision mechanically resolves this statute. Ratzlaf’s distinct willfulness language required knowledge of unlawfulness there; no such general requirement follows here. Reading §2252 to punish a distributor who lacks awareness of the material’s incriminating character would also raise the speech concerns identified in Smith and Ferber. The fair contextual reading avoids that unnecessary constitutional ruling without rewriting a federal default of recklessness from Osborne’s Ohio statute. The reported finding that respondents knew the performer was underage remains relevant, but does not resolve every count, the indictment objection or whether these tapes contain the conduct the statute defines.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *Morissette v. United States*, 342 U.S. 246 (1952), applies the presumption of culpability to the statutory setting rather than treating grammatical distance as conclusive.
- *Staples v. United States*, 511 U.S. 600 (1994), applies its distinction between knowledge of facts making an object regulated and knowledge of legal duties; its weapon-specific holding does not automatically construe §2252.
- *Posters ’N’ Things, Ltd. v. United States*, 511 U.S. 513 (1994), preserves actual factual knowledge as distinct from legal classification and from a desire to cause the forbidden use; its different coverage dispute remains unchanged.
- *Ratzlaf v. United States*, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), is distinguished as a text-specific willfulness requirement concerning unlawful structuring; it creates no general requirement to know that conduct is illegal.
- *Smith v. California*, 361 U.S. 147 (1959), and *New York v. Ferber*, 458 U.S. 747 (1982), inform the constitutional concern about penalizing dissemination without adequate awareness of incriminating content; the Court does not decide an unavoidable constitutional requirement of actual knowledge of minority in every enactment.
- *Osborne v. Ohio*, 495 U.S. 103 (1990), remains distinct: its Ohio default-culpability statute supplied recklessness and supplies no federal default here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This is a statutory construction of §§2252(a)(1)–(2), not a universal constitutional actual-knowledge minimum, a construction of every other offense or a decision concerning §2251 producers. No new digital-image category, knowledge-of-illegality element, knowledge-of-nexus rule or automatic trial-error remedy is supplied.

### The two renewed alternatives may defend the existing favorable judgment

**Controlling proposition:** Respondents may defend the existing appellate reversal on the renewed age-eighteen and lascivious-wording grounds that appear in the record, although the court of appeals rejected those grounds, because they seek no enlargement of their rights or reduction of the Government’s rights under that judgment. The Court reaches those two alternatives; the separate actual-or-simulated bestiality and sadistic-or-masochistic-abuse objection is not established as renewed and is not decided.

**Authority:** Part II of Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer, supplies six direct joins to this reach ruling. Stone-Zsela joins only the dispositions on the two alternatives and adds no reach ground. Scalia and Thomas accept the legal availability of such defenses but do not decide either alternative because their independent ground would sustain the appellate judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that respondents may offer these two grounds to preserve the judgment they already obtained. American Railway Express distinguishes defending a judgment from asking the Court to enlarge it or overturn a separate adverse judgment. Respondents seek the same appellate reversal, not greater relief, and the two renewed objections were considered in the record below. Izumi therefore does not require treating each rejected reason as a separate adverse judgment that respondents must overturn. The limited question rules preserved in Caspari and Madsen remain intact; briefing alone still does not expand a request for different relief. Availability is not compulsion: Dandridge leaves room for a proper remand where the issues warrant one. Here the Court resolves the two renewed facial grounds on their stated limited terms while reserving the unresolved indictment and tape-content claims. The other definition challenge is not established as renewed, so its treatment below does not become a new holding of this Court.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *United States v. American Railway Express Co.*, 265 U.S. 425 (1924), applies the prevailing party’s ability to defend the existing judgment on a ground appearing in the record, even one rejected below, without seeking greater relief.
- *Dandridge v. Williams*, 397 U.S. 471 (1970), preserves a lawful remand option rather than imposing a duty to decide every available alternative.
- *Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp.*, 510 U.S. 27 (1993), remains applicable to an attempt to overturn a distinct adverse judgment; defending this already favorable judgment does not undo its presented-question rule.
- *Caspari v. Bohlen*, 510 U.S. 383 (1994), and *Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc.*, 512 U.S. 753 (1994), retain their limited subsidiary-question and exceptional-review boundaries; the Court grants no general enlargement of review based on briefing alone.

### The asserted age-eighteen line does not establish substantial facial overbreadth

**Controlling proposition:** The renewed objection that §2256 defines a minor as a person under eighteen, rather than under sixteen, does not establish substantial First Amendment overbreadth in §§2252(a)(1)–(2) as construed here. Difficulty estimating age near eighteen, together with decisions permitting adult treatment of some older adolescents in other settings, does not require facial invalidation of this protective category.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, Part III, joined by Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer. Six Justices directly adopt the stated ground. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the disposition of Part III and adds no ground; Scalia and Thomas do not reach its merits.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the asserted difficulty of distinguishing older adolescents from adults does not establish the substantial overbreadth needed to invalidate this statute on its face. Ferber supplies that demanding facial standard and recognizes the governmental interest in preventing the sexual exploitation of children. Its particular statute protected children under sixteen, but the decision did not constitutionalize that age as an upper boundary. Permission to treat some sixteen- or seventeen-year-olds as adults in other settings likewise does not require adult treatment for sexual exploitation. The concern that distributors may avoid protected adult expression deserves consideration; the Court does not equate it with proof of a substantial unconstitutional sweep. The required actual knowledge of minority further narrows that concern, although the age-line ruling does not rest on scienter alone. No empirical finding about the amount of chilled material is made. The Court rejects this facial ground without deciding every application or the disputed content of respondents’ tapes.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *New York v. Ferber*, 458 U.S. 747 (1982), applies the requirement of substantial overbreadth for facial invalidation and its protection of children from sexual exploitation. Its approval of a statute protecting children under sixteen does not establish sixteen as the highest permissible protective age.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No general constitutional age-of-majority rule is announced, and no as-applied challenge is foreclosed merely by this facial disposition.

### The complete lascivious-exhibition category defeats the word-substitution facial attack

**Controlling proposition:** Replacing lewd with lascivious in §2256’s category of lascivious exhibition of the genitals or pubic area does not itself make §§2252(a)(1)–(2) facially vague or substantially overbroad. The complete category retains a sexual-conduct limit and does not cover nudity merely because a person’s body is visible; whether these particular tapes satisfy the definition remains open.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, Part IV, joined by Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer. Six Justices directly adopt the stated ground. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the disposition of Part IV and adds no ground; Scalia and Thomas do not reach its merits.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the challenged word must be read with the conduct it describes. The provision concerns lascivious exhibition of the genitals or pubic area, not nudity as such. Ferber’s treatment of lewd exhibition supplies a meaningful comparison, and the ordinary terminology considered in Miller does not become constitutionally indeterminate merely through the substitution of lascivious. Wiegand persuasively recognizes the words’ equivalent meaning in this context. Respondents therefore have not shown that the substitution itself creates the uncertainty or substantial unconstitutional breadth they assert. This conclusion keeps content classification distinct from culpability: knowledge does not automatically make every nude depiction covered, and the Court does not hold that scienter cures every vague definition. The inquiry must still concern the actual statutory conduct. The Court adopts no new checklist of factors and makes no final classification of the tapes here. Their reserved contention that the tapes are not child pornography remains for appellate consideration.

**Precedent treatment:**

- *New York v. Ferber*, 458 U.S. 747 (1982), applies the meaningful limit supplied by lewd exhibition of the genitals and the substantial-overbreadth standard; it does not equate ordinary nudity with the prohibited sexual conduct.
- *Miller v. California*, 413 U.S. 15 (1973), supplies the ordinary lewd-exhibition terminology considered in this comparison; the Court does not add Miller’s complete obscenity test as a requirement for the child-pornography category.
- *United States v. Wiegand*, 812 F.2d 1239 (9th Cir. 1987), provides persuasive contextual support for the equivalence of lascivious and lewd exhibition. It supplies no new mandatory multifactor definition of the federal category.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The other objection concerning actual or simulated bestiality and sadistic or masochistic abuse is not reached. No unofficial-code punctuation is adopted and no definition of every possible application is supplied.

- *Morissette v. United States*, 342 U.S. 246 (1952), applies the presumption of culpability to the statutory setting rather than treating grammatical distance as conclusive.
- *Staples v. United States*, 511 U.S. 600 (1994), applies its distinction between knowledge of facts making an object regulated and knowledge of legal duties; its weapon-specific holding does not automatically construe §2252.
- *Posters ’N’ Things, Ltd. v. United States*, 511 U.S. 513 (1994), preserves actual factual knowledge as distinct from legal classification and from a desire to cause the forbidden use; its different coverage dispute remains unchanged.
- *Ratzlaf v. United States*, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), is distinguished as a text-specific willfulness requirement concerning unlawful structuring; it creates no general requirement to know that conduct is illegal.
- *Smith v. California*, 361 U.S. 147 (1959), and *New York v. Ferber*, 458 U.S. 747 (1982), inform the constitutional concern about penalizing dissemination without adequate awareness of incriminating content; the Court does not decide an unavoidable constitutional requirement of actual knowledge of minority in every enactment.
- *Osborne v. Ohio*, 495 U.S. 103 (1990), remains distinct: its Ohio default-culpability statute supplied recklessness and supplies no federal default here.
- *United States v. American Railway Express Co.*, 265 U.S. 425 (1924), applies the prevailing party’s ability to defend the existing judgment on a ground appearing in the record, even one rejected below, without seeking greater relief.
- *Dandridge v. Williams*, 397 U.S. 471 (1970), preserves a lawful remand option rather than imposing a duty to decide every available alternative.
- *Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp.*, 510 U.S. 27 (1993), remains applicable to an attempt to overturn a distinct adverse judgment; defending this already favorable judgment does not undo its presented-question rule.
- *Caspari v. Bohlen*, 510 U.S. 383 (1994), and *Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc.*, 512 U.S. 753 (1994), retain their limited subsidiary-question and exceptional-review boundaries; the Court grants no general enlargement of review based on briefing alone.
- *New York v. Ferber*, 458 U.S. 747 (1982), applies the requirement of substantial overbreadth for facial invalidation and its protection of children from sexual exploitation. Its approval of a statute protecting children under sixteen does not establish sixteen as the highest permissible protective age.
- *New York v. Ferber*, 458 U.S. 747 (1982), applies the meaningful limit supplied by lewd exhibition of the genitals and the substantial-overbreadth standard; it does not equate ordinary nudity with the prohibited sexual conduct.
- *Miller v. California*, 413 U.S. 15 (1973), supplies the ordinary lewd-exhibition terminology considered in this comparison; the Court does not add Miller’s complete obscenity test as a requirement for the child-pornography category.
- *United States v. Wiegand*, 812 F.2d 1239 (9th Cir. 1987), provides persuasive contextual support for the equivalence of lascivious and lewd exhibition. It supplies no new mandatory multifactor definition of the federal category.

Effective November 29, 1994, §§2252(a)(1)–(2) require the stated actual factual knowledge of sexual character and minority, without a general knowledge-of-illegality requirement. The Court rejects the two renewed facial-definition grounds on the limited six-Justice rationales stated above. The prior offense-specific holdings of Ratzlaf, Staples and Posters remain intact. The Ninth Circuit’s facial-scienter invalidation is set aside; no acquittal, count-specific affirmance, automatic harmlessness result or mandatory retrial follows. The unresolved indictment and actual-tape claims remain open, as does the independently unrenewed definition objection.

### Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, No. 93-1062. — 1994-12-05

Authority: [Record](../records/Church_of_Scientology_Flag_Service_Organization_Inc_v_City_of_Clearwater_merits_1994-12-05.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### RFRA governs future enforcement and requires application-specific adjudication

**Holding and operative rule:** RFRA applies to the threatened future implementation of Clearwater's preexisting ordinance: the Church must establish a substantial burden on its religious exercise, after which Clearwater must produce evidence and persuade that applying that burden to this person furthers a compelling governmental interest by the least restrictive means. The adverse prospective determinations concerning the identifying disclosures designated below as section 100.03(1)(a), (b), (c), and (j), and the written-refund-policy requirement in section 100.05(1)(g), must therefore be vacated for that inquiry, with ordinary Article III standing and appropriate-relief limits preserved.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: eight direct rationale votes. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela agrees that RFRA governs, but does not join the opinion or its disposition of these adverse determinations.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress's protection of religious exercise governs the proposed enforcement. Section 6 expressly covers implementation of laws adopted before RFRA, and the act to be restrained is future enforcement, not the completed enactment of the ordinance. Landgraf supplies the distinction between that prospective operation and new liability for completed conduct. The Court of Appeals decided before RFRA became law; its constitutional disposition cannot substitute for the statutory inquiry. Limited identifying information and disclosure of a voluntarily offered refund policy may serve neutral fraud prevention, but that description does not establish either the absence of a substantial burden or the least restrictive means of addressing a demonstrated burden. The Church must prove the predicate burden; the City must then justify the application to this claimant. Further proceedings must undertake those distinct inquiries. The existing favorable relief and purpose remand remain intact. RFRA leaves the Establishment Clause untouched, so statutory reconsideration neither resolves the disputed purpose nor approves the lower court's constitutional reasoning.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244 (1994): applies the distinction between future enforcement and new legal consequences for completed conduct, consistently with RFRA's express temporal direction; neither Landgraf's general framework nor its distinct relevant-activity rationale is enlarged.
- Church of the Lukumi Babalu Aye, Inc. v. City of Hialeah, 508 U.S. 520 (1993): preserves its objective religious-targeting, substantial secular-underinclusion, and strict-scrutiny holdings; the statutory remand neither supplies a purpose finding nor converts the enactment-history discussion into a controlling test.
- Board of Education of Kiryas Joel Village School District v. Grumet, 512 U.S. 687 (1994): preserves the distinct denominational-allocation and unequal particular-grant rules; accommodation does not cure an establishment violation, and RFRA does not decide that constitutional question.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No final substantial-burden or justification finding is made for the surviving identifying or written-policy requirements. The Court does not decide RFRA's constitutionality, retrospective monetary liability, a general exemption from civil fraud law, the disputed sectarian purpose, the precise Florida severability boundary, the unreviewed general anti-fraud clause, or unpreserved hardship-discretion and procedural objections. Section 7 leaves the Establishment Clause unaffected; section 6(c) supplies no authority to burden religious belief.

- Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244 (1994): applies the distinction between future enforcement and new legal consequences for completed conduct, consistently with RFRA's express temporal direction; neither Landgraf's general framework nor its distinct relevant-activity rationale is enlarged.
- Church of the Lukumi Babalu Aye, Inc. v. City of Hialeah, 508 U.S. 520 (1993): preserves its objective religious-targeting, substantial secular-underinclusion, and strict-scrutiny holdings; the statutory remand neither supplies a purpose finding nor converts the enactment-history discussion into a controlling test.
- Board of Education of Kiryas Joel Village School District v. Grumet, 512 U.S. 687 (1994): preserves the distinct denominational-allocation and unequal particular-grant rules; accommodation does not cure an establishment violation, and RFRA does not decide that constitutional question.

Effective December 5, 1994, RFRA's original burden-and-justification inquiry governs the specified prospective municipal enforcement claims despite the ordinance's earlier enactment. This adds a controlling statutory application and remand rule; it does not replace the constitutional free-exercise or establishment standards. The favorable lower relief and factual purpose/severability remand survive without adoption of the lower constitutional rationales. No final exemption or constitutional invalidity of the surviving provisions is established.

### Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund, No. 93-1151. — 1994-12-06

Authority: [Record](../records/Federal_Election_Commission_v_NRA_Political_Victory_Fund_jurisdictional_dismissal_1994-12-06.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### The Commission lacks independent certiorari authority for this FECA enforcement action

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 437d(a)(6)'s authority to litigate and appeal civil enforcement actions through the Commission's general counsel does not displace 28 U.S.C. §518(a)'s allocation of Supreme Court representation in this FECA corporate-contribution action. The Attorney General's particular-case direction exception remains available, and the express certiorari powers in 26 U.S.C. §§9010(d) and 9040(d) remain confined to actions in which the Commission appears under those respective funding sections.

**Authority:** Part I of Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: six of eight participating Justices directly adopt the rule. Stone-Zsela and Stevens dissent; Ginsburg does not participate.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress did not authorize the Commission to petition independently in this enforcement action. Providence Journal establishes that authority to conduct litigation below does not necessarily include authority to represent the Government here. Congress may create an exception, and ordinary construction determines whether it has done so. Section 437d(a)(6) gives the Commission substantial enforcement autonomy, but its general appeal language must be read with section 518(a)'s specific allocation and the express certiorari grants in the presidential-funding statutes. Those grants apply only to actions brought within their respective sections; this corporate-contribution action is not one of them. Enforcement exclusivity and the separate power to defend actions do not settle who may affirmatively invoke this Court's review. Germain permits the grants to coexist with their distinct conditions. The Court adopts neither a rule that appeal invariably excludes certiorari nor a required formula for statutory exceptions. Lawful particular-case direction remains possible; no timely direction is established here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Providence Journal Co., 485 U.S. 693 (1988): applies its separation of lower-court litigation authority from Supreme Court representation while retaining ordinary statutory construction of congressional exceptions; no special verbal formula is imposed.
- Connecticut National Bank v. Germain, 503 U.S. 249 (1992): applies its preservation of coexisting statutory routes and their conditions; enforcement authority and Supreme Court representation can operate together.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding concerns this affirmative FECA enforcement petition. It does not deny the Commission the express powers supplied for funding litigation, construe every agency's litigation statute, or eliminate section 518(a)'s particular-case direction exception.

### Authorization after the statutory petition period does not ratify this filing

**Holding and operative rule:** Even assuming that the Solicitor General's May 26, 1994 authorization fully adopted the Commission's January 18 petition, it cannot relate back to validate this unauthorized invocation after the period in which an authorized petition could be filed had expired. Section 2101(c) allows ninety days and permits a Justice, for good cause, to extend the application period by no more than sixty days; the asserted authorization falls outside even that maximum under either supplied judgment date.

**Authority:** Part II of Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: six direct votes. Together with Part I, this ground requires dismissal for want of jurisdiction; it is not a constitutional merits disposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that subsequent authorization cannot cure this petition after lawful filing became impossible. The Commission filed on January 18, within ninety days of the October 22 judgment, but lacked authority to invoke review for the Government. Cook permits ratification to relate back only when the principal could perform the act both when first attempted and when adopted. By May 26 the ninety-day period and even the maximum possible sixty-day extension had elapsed. Treating the October 25 amendment as the starting point would not change that conclusion; the Court finds neither a restart nor an extension. The fullest claimed authorization therefore fails without an inquiry into finer wording of the letter. Dodge concerned adoption of a seizure in a forfeiture proceeding, not this expired review period. Smith v. Barry preserves functional compliance by a timely notice document, not missing governmental authority. Ordinary timely corrections and lawful delegation are not foreclosed by this bounded decision.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Cook v. Tullis, 85 U.S. (18 Wall.) 332 (1873): applies the requirement that a ratifying principal have capacity both at the original act and at ratification, with intervening rights preserved; relation back does not supply capacity after this statutory petition period.
- Dodge v. United States, 272 U.S. 530 (1926): distinguishes adoption of an unauthorized seizure where the forfeiture prerequisites were satisfied from authorization of appellate review after the statutory filing period.
- Smith v. Barry, 502 U.S. 244 (1992): distinguishes a timely document that functionally supplies required appellate notice from a filing lacking governmental representation authority; the functional-notice holding remains intact.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no universal rule that all representation defects are incurable. It makes no finding that a Justice granted an extension or that the amended lower opinion restarted time. The Solicitor General's authorization is distinct from any Commission resolution addressing its composition; neither constitutional validity nor structural ratification is reached.

- United States v. Providence Journal Co., 485 U.S. 693 (1988): applies its separation of lower-court litigation authority from Supreme Court representation while retaining ordinary statutory construction of congressional exceptions; no special verbal formula is imposed.
- Connecticut National Bank v. Germain, 503 U.S. 249 (1992): applies its preservation of coexisting statutory routes and their conditions; enforcement authority and Supreme Court representation can operate together.
- Cook v. Tullis, 85 U.S. (18 Wall.) 332 (1873): applies the requirement that a ratifying principal have capacity both at the original act and at ratification, with intervening rights preserved; relation back does not supply capacity after this statutory petition period.
- Dodge v. United States, 272 U.S. 530 (1926): distinguishes adoption of an unauthorized seizure where the forfeiture prerequisites were satisfied from authorization of appellate review after the statutory filing period.
- Smith v. Barry, 502 U.S. 244 (1992): distinguishes a timely document that functionally supplies required appellate notice from a filing lacking governmental representation authority; the functional-notice holding remains intact.

Effective December 6, 1994, the Commission's general FECA enforcement appeal power does not independently authorize this Supreme Court petition, and the assumed fullest later authorization cannot cure the filing after the lawful application period. Those threshold rules control; the D.C. Circuit's constitutional judgment remains in force for the parties without Supreme Court endorsement. The Court adds no rule about nonvoting congressional members, structural severability, agency ratification, the de facto officer doctrine, contribution liability, or constitutional relief.

### Reich v. Collins, No. 93-908. — 1994-12-06

Authority: [Record](../records/Reich_v_Collins_merits_1994-12-06.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### A State may not withdraw its apparent postpayment remedy after payment

**Holding and operative rule:** When a State holds out a clear postpayment remedy for illegally assessed and collected taxes, including voluntary payments, due process forbids withdrawing that remedy after payment for the constitutional invalidity asserted and then relying on the taxpayer's failure to pursue a different prepayment route. Georgia must afford Reich a clear and certain opportunity for the refund or constitutionally adequate equivalent relief due under federal law, subject to ordinary proof of payment and amount; it may prospectively establish an adequate prepayment system with fair notice.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for the bounded procedural rule and remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a State's remedial choice must be clear when a taxpayer must act. McKesson permits meaningful review before payment, after payment, or both; it does not permit a State to offer one route and withdraw it after the taxpayer has paid. Georgia's statute appeared to promise refunds of all taxes illegally assessed and collected, whether paid voluntarily or involuntarily. The later construction excluded taxes imposed under an unconstitutional law. The state court controls its statute's meaning, but the resulting removal of the represented remedy presents a federal due-process question. Even assuming Georgia's prepayment remedies were adequate, their existence does not cure this change. Harper keeps the applicable federal rule distinct from the remedy: lawful refunds, actual nondiscriminatory retroactive assessments, or a constitutionally adequate combination remain possible. The State must provide an actual lawful correction, not a hypothetical alternative tax. The Court fixes no amount and decides neither disputed protest requirements nor the adequacy of each prepayment mechanism.

**Precedent treatment:**

- McKesson Corp. v. Division of Alcoholic Beverages and Tobacco, 496 U.S. 18 (1990): applies its clear-and-certain-remedy requirement to withdrawal of an apparently available postpayment route; adequate prepayment systems and lawful remedial choices remain permissible.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993): applies the distinction between the federal rule governing open direct review and the lawful remedy; no automatic refund or reopening of final judgments follows.
- Barker v. Kansas, 503 U.S. 594 (1992): preserves its separate reservation of refund questions; substantive pension-tax invalidity does not fix the amount or form of relief.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The substantive tax invalidity is already accepted. The Court neither declares every Georgia prepayment avenue inadequate nor converts discretionary stays into guaranteed stays. It creates no general government-estoppel doctrine, exemption from clearly announced reasonable requirements, fixed refund, interest award, or reopening of final judgments. Prospective procedural changes remain permissible with adequate review and fair notice.

- McKesson Corp. v. Division of Alcoholic Beverages and Tobacco, 496 U.S. 18 (1990): applies its clear-and-certain-remedy requirement to withdrawal of an apparently available postpayment route; adequate prepayment systems and lawful remedial choices remain permissible.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993): applies the distinction between the federal rule governing open direct review and the lawful remedy; no automatic refund or reopening of final judgments follows.
- Barker v. Kansas, 503 U.S. 594 (1992): preserves its separate reservation of refund questions; substantive pension-tax invalidity does not fix the amount or form of relief.

Effective December 6, 1994, due process bars this after-payment withdrawal of a clear apparent postpayment remedy even assuming another adequate prepayment avenue existed. The rule adds a fair-notice constraint to remedial choice; it preserves McKesson's options and Harper's separation of governing law from remedy. It changes no substantive tax-immunity rule and supplies no refund amount or final-case reopening.

### Brown v. Gardner, No. 93-1128. — 1994-12-12

Authority: [Record](../records/Brown_v_Gardner_merits_1994-12-12.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

### Section 1151 requires causation, not provider fault or accident

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1151 requires an injury or aggravation resulting from covered VA hospitalization, medical or surgical treatment, chapter 31 vocational rehabilitation, or examination, producing additional disability or death and not resulting from the veteran's own willful misconduct; it does not additionally require negligence, other provider fault, or an unforeseen untoward accident. Section 3.358(c)(3)'s added fault-or-accident prerequisite is invalid, and Gardner's claim must be redetermined under the surviving statutory conditions without an automatic benefits award.

**Authority:** Justice Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct rationale votes on the causal rule and invalidity of the additional prerequisite. Stone-Zsela also concurs separately on the rule's limits.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that causal connection and fault are different requirements. Section 1151 speaks in terms of injury or aggravation resulting from the listed services and expressly excludes the veteran's own willful misconduct. Congress did not impose the regulation's additional demand for deficient care or an unforeseen accident. King supplies contextual interpretation that respects enacted limits, with protective construction as confirmation. Chevron requires the agency first to respect Congress's answer, and MCI confirms that administrative expertise cannot enlarge a statutory delegation. The Secretary's reliance on duration of the regulation, costs, hearing examples, reenactment, and legislative silence does not overcome that text. Removing fault does not make every later disability compensable: actual causation and the other statutory conditions remain. The existing natural-progress provision is undisturbed, and a necessary consequence of consented treatment is not automatically compensable. The Court neither finds that surgery caused Gardner's claimed disability nor resolves a comprehensive consent or necessary-consequences doctrine. The existing administrative redetermination must proceed under the lawful standard.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837 (1984): applies the statutory-meaning inquiry before deference; the clear causal standard leaves no authority to add provider fault, without abolishing deference in genuine interpretive gaps.
- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): applies contextual reading of protective veterans legislation and respect for express rather than invented limits; the veterans-protective canon confirms the text instead of replacing it.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994): applies the limit that administrative expertise cannot supply statutory power Congress did not grant; the distinct tariff-modification rule is not extended into a general rejection of agency discretion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The natural-progress provision is left undisturbed. No complete rule governing consent or necessary consequences is adopted. The separate FTCA coordination sentence remains: for a qualifying judgment under 28 U.S.C. §1346(b), or settlement or compromise under §§2672 or 2677, on or after December 1, 1962, for a disability, aggravation, or death treated as service connected under §1151, benefits for months after finality are withheld until the aggregate otherwise payable equals the total recovery. That is a coordination rule, not a provider-fault requirement; no qualifying recovery is found here.

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837 (1984): applies the statutory-meaning inquiry before deference; the clear causal standard leaves no authority to add provider fault, without abolishing deference in genuine interpretive gaps.
- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): applies contextual reading of protective veterans legislation and respect for express rather than invented limits; the veterans-protective canon confirms the text instead of replacing it.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994): applies the limit that administrative expertise cannot supply statutory power Congress did not grant; the distinct tariff-modification rule is not extended into a general rejection of agency discretion.

Effective December 12, 1994, the VA may not impose provider fault or accident as an additional §1151 eligibility condition. Actual causation and the other enacted conditions remain; the natural-progress provision is undisturbed. The Court adds this statutory rule without changing Chevron generally, deciding a comprehensive consent doctrine, or awarding Gardner benefits. The separate FTCA coordination remains independent of fault.

### In re Baby K; No. 93-1673. — 1994-12-12

Authority: [Record](../records/In_re_Baby_K_merits_1994-12-12.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

#### The acute respiratory emergency triggers stabilization or lawful transfer

**Holding and operative rule:** When a participating hospital determines that an individual has an emergency respiratory condition, §1395dd(b)(1) requires the examination and treatment within its available staff and facilities necessary to prevent likely material deterioration, within reasonable medical probability, resulting from or during transfer, or transfer complying with subsection (c). Inability to cure the patient's underlying anencephaly, an ethical or medical objection to continued life support, uniform withholding from comparable anencephalic patients, and the hospital's lack of an intention to transfer do not excuse this duty where the acute emergency and effective stabilizing capability are established.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly adopt this proposition and its stated qualifications. These 9 rationale votes control without combining separate rationales.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the immediate respiratory emergency is the condition to be stabilized. Section 1395dd(e)(1)(A) addresses acute symptoms sufficiently severe that absence of immediate attention could reasonably cause serious jeopardy to health, serious impairment of bodily functions, or serious dysfunction of an organ or part. The hospital concedes that Baby K's episodes meet that standard and that ventilation prevents deterioration. Incurable anencephaly does not negate those concessions. King requires reading the whole statute without adding an unexpressed restriction; Suter keeps the enacted duty distinct from an inferred remedy. Uniform screening under subsection (a) therefore cannot replace the separate stabilization obligation after an emergency is found. Nor may the hospital erase that obligation by retaining the patient without intending transfer. Ethical objection does not establish that otherwise available staff or facilities are unavailable. The holding concerns these acute presentations, not all inpatient, nonemergency or end-of-life care, and creates no entitlement to every preferred treatment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): applies contextual reading and respect for expressed statutory coverage and limits; its military-leave rule does not decide a medical question.
- Suter v. Artist M., 503 U.S. 347 (1992): preserves separate inquiries into enacted duty, cause of action and remedy; its rejection of particular child-welfare claims supplies no categorical exemption from express hospital duties.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Screening under subsection (a) is requested examination of an individual coming to the emergency department within that department's capability, including routinely available ancillary services. The diagnosed-emergency duty under subsection (b) is distinct. Transfer includes hospital-directed movement and discharge, but excludes movement after declaration of death or departure without hospital-affiliated permission. The separate labor provisions are not decided; Congress remains free to enact a defined futility exception.

#### Unstabilized transfer remains a fully conditional alternative

**Holding and operative rule:** An unstabilized transfer requires either a written request by the individual or legally responsible person after explanation of the hospital's obligations and transfer risks, or a physician-signed certification, based on then-available information, that expected medical benefits at the other facility outweigh increased transfer risks; if no physician is physically present in the emergency department, a qualified medical person may sign only after consulting a physician who makes that determination and later countersigns, and every certification must summarize risks and benefits. Every route also requires available treatment minimizing risk; receiving space, qualified personnel and acceptance of appropriate care; transmission of all available emergency-related records, the request or certification, and the name and address of a required on-call physician who refused or failed timely to appear; qualified transportation personnel and equipment with necessary appropriate life support; and the Secretary's applicable additional health-and-safety requirements.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly adopt this proposition and its stated qualifications. These 9 rationale votes control without combining separate rationales.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress provided a lawful transfer route and specified what makes it lawful. The treatment-or-transfer structure cannot be restated as treatment at this hospital under all future circumstances. King supports respecting those expressed alternatives rather than constructing an absolute obligation from the statute's protective purpose. The present record separately lacks a benefits-over-risks certification and a qualified receiving facility that has agreed to accept Baby K. The mother's opposition is not a substitute for either missing condition. Conversely, opposition alone does not defeat a future transfer supported by the statutory physician certification and every appropriate-transfer safeguard. Available records include the emergency observations, preliminary diagnosis, treatment and test results; the hospital must send what is available rather than invent missing records. The alternative is permission for a compliant transfer, not a command to transfer or a finding that future space, personnel and acceptance exist. The prospective declaration remains confined by those statutory terms.

**Precedent treatment:**

- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): preserves the force of express statutory alternatives and conditions; protective purpose does not erase an enacted transfer route.

#### Refusal compliance requires an actual offer and informed refusal

**Holding and operative rule:** Subsection (b)(2) deems the treatment requirement met when the hospital offers the required examination and treatment, explains its risks and benefits, the individual or representative refuses, and the hospital takes all reasonable steps to obtain written informed refusal. Subsection (b)(3) separately deems compliance when the hospital offers a transfer satisfying subsection (c), explains its risks and benefits, the individual or representative refuses, and the hospital takes all reasonable steps to obtain written informed refusal; the mother's opposition alone establishes neither route here.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court; Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly adopt this proposition and its stated qualifications. These 8 rationale votes control without combining separate rationales.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that refusal provisions are conditional compliance routes, not exceptions triggered by disagreement alone. A treatment offer and a transfer offer perform different statutory work. The latter must itself satisfy subsection (c), including appropriate receiving capability and acceptance, before refusal can have the effect stated in subsection (b)(3). Here the record supplies neither a compliant transfer offer nor a finding that the mother refused the stabilizing treatment the hospital must offer. Her opposition to the requested declaration therefore does not release the hospital from its emergency obligation. King supports reading the alternatives within the coordinated enactment, and Suter cautions against confusing a defined duty with an assumed remedial result. The duty to take all reasonable steps to secure writing does not become an assertion that written refusal already exists. The Court preserves either route if its actual predicates arise, while making no general decision about parental or guardian authority outside this statutory controversy.

**Precedent treatment:**

- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): applies the coordinated statutory structure, including the separate conditions attached to separate alternatives.
- Suter v. Artist M., 503 U.S. 347 (1992): preserves the distinction between a substantive duty and a claimed consequence; no broader family-authority or remedial rule is inferred.

#### The receiving-hospital route depends on appropriate transfer, required specialized capability and capacity

**Holding and operative rule:** If a nursing-home movement is an appropriate transfer requiring the participating hospital's specialized capabilities or facilities, §1395dd(g) prohibits the hospital from refusing acceptance when it has capacity to treat the individual. The Court does not settle every movement's classification or presume future capacity; the conditional receiving-hospital route preserves those requirements independently of the ordinary arrival-and-diagnosed-emergency route.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court; Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly adopt this proposition and its stated qualifications. These 8 rationale votes control without combining separate rationales.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the uncertainty about the description of a nursing-home movement does not justify deleting the receiving-hospital provision. Subsection (g) addresses an appropriate transfer requiring specialized capabilities and expressly conditions the duty on receiving capacity. The record identifies pediatric intensive-care and ventilation capabilities, but that evidence does not establish every future transfer's appropriateness or the hospital's capacity whenever one may be proposed. King requires attention to the distinct function of each part of the enactment. The hospital's broader reading would allow the unresolved label to defeat either statutory route without examining its conditions; an unconditional acceptance rule would make the opposite error by erasing capacity. The declaration instead applies the route whose actual predicates are met. The Court neither finds that every nursing-home movement is a new emergency presentation nor decides that every such movement is a transfer requiring acceptance. Those factual predicates remain necessary when the prospective duty is applied.

**Precedent treatment:**

- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): applies reading of related provisions together while retaining each provision's expressed conditions.

#### State medical law yields only to an applicable direct conflict

**Holding and operative rule:** Under §1395dd(f), a state or local requirement remains effective except to the extent it directly conflicts with an applicable EMTALA requirement; state law cannot authorize withholding the stabilizing treatment required here. Virginia §54.1-2990, a limitation within an article concerning adult directives and surrogates, does not itself establish an infant-care exemption, and the Court invalidates no wider field of state medical law.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly adopt this proposition and its stated qualifications. These 9 rationale votes control without combining separate rationales.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that federal displacement extends only as far as the conflict Congress identified. The hospital invokes Virginia's provision stating that its Health Care Decisions Act does not require treatment a physician considers medically or ethically inappropriate. The article's described coverage concerns adult directives and surrogate decisions, so its language does not itself establish authority governing this infant. Even if an applicable state requirement permitted withholding the stabilizing care required by EMTALA, subsection (f) resolves that direct conflict in favor of the federal duty. King supports reading this limiting clause with the enacted emergency obligation rather than replacing either with a general medical-policy judgment. The hospital remains governed by compatible state medical law, including questions this case does not decide. The Court does not derive a general federal right to demand any treatment, adjudicate parental or guardian power, or decide the district court's independent disability-law grounds. The declaration is affirmed at the scope of the federal emergency duty.

**Precedent treatment:**

- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): applies the whole-enactment approach; the limited preemption clause and affirmative duty both retain effect.

- King v. St. Vincent's Hospital, 502 U.S. 215 (1991): applies contextual reading and respect for expressed statutory coverage and limits; its military-leave rule does not decide a medical question.
