**Holding and operative rule:** A sales tax is fairly related to state services when broad governmental services support the locally taxed activity; the Constitution requires no dollar-for-dollar or mile-for-mile reimbursement calculation. Oklahoma's services supporting ticket sales, passenger loading, and local operations establish that relation for these transactions.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: five direct rationale votes. Justices Scalia and Thomas concur only in the judgment and do not join this complete explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that fair relation concerns the connection between the taxed activity and governmental benefits, not a bill for each service the passenger receives. Commonwealth Edison rejects a requirement that the taxpayer's liability match the cost of particular benefits. Goldberg likewise recognizes the significance of broad protections supporting a taxable transaction. The stipulation and bankruptcy findings establish local ticketing and loading operations, governmental protection at those locations, and use of Oklahoma roads. Jefferson answers that Oklahoma supplies no services along the out-of-state part of the route. That objection would require the Court to price each mile's governmental support, although the occasion of this tax is the completed local sale. The transaction's price measures its value rather than the State's exact expenditure. The Court does not permit taxation of an unrelated event or hold that this factor replaces apportionment. It finds the required relation for the local activity shown and leaves remote or wholly out-of-state transactions undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Commonwealth Edison Co. v. Montana, 453 U.S. 609: applies the connection to broad governmental benefits without dollar-for-dollar accounting.
- Goldberg v. Sweet, 488 U.S. 252: applies the fair-relation inquiry to general protections supporting the taxable activity.
- Complete Auto Transit, Inc. v. Brady, 430 U.S. 274: keeps fair relation independent of nexus, apportionment, and nondiscrimination.

#### Precedent Treatment

Complete Auto remains the governing four-part framework. Quill's remote-collection boundary is preserved; National Geographic supplies the actual-local-operations comparison. Goldberg keeps internal and external consistency distinct. Central Greyhound continues to require apportionment of the carrier-business receipts it addressed, but does not govern this distinct local retail sale. Itel Containers supplies the distinct-transaction principle without deciding this case through its conceded Complete Auto compliance. Lohman preserves actual-burden nondiscrimination analysis. Commonwealth Edison preserves fair relation without a service-cost accounting. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 3, 1995, the full price of the stipulated local-sale/local-origin interstate tickets may be taxed under the five component conclusions stated above. This is an application of Complete Auto, including a controlling distinction of Central Greyhound, rather than immunity for all transportation levies. Remote purchases, tickets for wholly out-of-state trips, actual competing assessments, and materially different discriminatory burdens remain undecided. There is a five-Justice opinion for each controlling proposition; no Marks synthesis of the separate writings is necessary.

### Plant Variety Protection Act Amendments of 1994; Pub. L. 103-349, 108 Stat. 3136; no Court docket. — 1995-04-04

Authority: [Record](../records/Plant_Variety_Protection_Act_Amendments_statutory_effectiveness_1995-04-04.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

This source creates no judicial holding.

#### Precedent Treatment

The January 18 Asgrow adjudication of former law remains undisturbed.

#### Law After Decision

The amendments operate subject to section 14's former-law preservation for earlier certificates and pending applications, permitted refiling with its original-date rule, and amended-protection labeling and sanctions requirements. The complete enacted instrument supplies all qualifications. Section 9's contract-producer clause has its own enumerated crop and contractual conditions; section 10 removes the former farmer-to-farmer sale proviso for the amended regime.

### Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, No. 94-286. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Freightliner_Corp_v_Myrick_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### No in-effect federal standard supplies the express-preemption predicate here

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1392(d)'s express restriction requires an in-effect federal motor-vehicle safety standard governing the same aspect of performance as the asserted state duty. The asserted antilock-equipment defect claims are not expressly displaced on this record because that federal predicate is absent; the Court does not decide whether common-law duties themselves are safety standards within that provision.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer: eight direct rationale votes. Justice Scalia concurs only in the judgment and gives no separate explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the express clause must be applied to the particular aspect of performance actually regulated. Easterwood requires attention to federal coverage of the duty at issue, and Cipollone requires examination of the enacted preemption language and predicate duty. Regulation of vehicle braking generally does not establish an operative antilock requirement for these claims. Paccar judicially invalidated inadequately supported stopping-distance requirements; that invalidation does not itself supply a federal safety standard forbidding state liability for omitted antilock equipment. Section 1392(d)'s condition is therefore unsatisfied without deciding whether common-law duties fall within its category of state standards. Section 1397(k) states that compliance with a federal safety standard does not exempt a person from common-law liability. The Court preserves that text but need not determine its every effect where an operative standard exists. The common-law claims return for ordinary adjudication; this statutory conclusion establishes neither a product defect nor causation and does not disturb the independent Georgia RICO judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- CSX Transportation, Inc. v. Easterwood, April 21, 1993: applies its attention to the aspect of performance actually covered, without importing the separate railroad speed holding or treating every braking rule as coverage of antilock omission.
- Cipollone v. Liggett Group, Inc., June 24, 1992: applies clause- and duty-specific analysis; its particular cigarette holdings do not answer all Safety Act questions.
- Paccar, Inc. v. National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, 573 F.2d 632: treats its judicial invalidation as the regulatory-history predicate, not as a new federal right to avoid common-law liability.

### Judicial invalidation alone establishes no actual federal conflict

**Holding and operative rule:** An express preemption clause does not categorically foreclose inquiry into an independently established actual conflict with operative federal law or a demonstrated federal objective. Here neither impossibility nor interference with such an objective is established by judicial invalidation and the absence of an operative antilock requirement, so the asserted conflict does not bar these common-law claims.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer: eight direct rationale votes. Scalia's judgment-only agreement supplies no additional rationale vote.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the failure of express preemption does not end every possible conflict inquiry. The Eleventh Circuit read Cipollone too broadly when it treated Congress's express clause as invariably excluding implied preemption. Gade rejects automatic displacement from regulatory overlap but supplies no physical-impossibility-only rule. The manufacturers nevertheless must identify the federal obligation or policy that their asserted state duty would frustrate. Their strongest claim treats the ability to sell trucks without antilock equipment as a protected federal choice. Paccar does not establish that premise: the court rejected the agency's evidentiary support while allowing adequately supported future standards. It did not adopt a policy insulating manufacturers from state liability. Compliance with the asserted duty is not shown to violate federal law, and regulatory silence alone does not demonstrate the claimed obstacle. A valid operative standard or demonstrated affirmative policy requiring a uniform absence of regulation could present a different case. The Court leaves that case, and the ultimate tort merits here, undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Cipollone v. Liggett Group, Inc., June 24, 1992: rejects the lower court's universal inference that any express provision exhausts every possible actual-conflict inquiry; its enacted-clause holdings remain intact.
- Gade v. National Solid Wastes Management Association, June 18, 1992: preserves the shared rule against automatic displacement from overlap; no physical-impossibility-only theory or broader separate theory is made Court law.
- Paccar, Inc. v. National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, 573 F.2d 632: distinguishes inadequate support for a mandatory standard from an affirmative policy protecting noninstallation against state duties.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 1397(k)'s compliance saving clause is not held to immunize every common-law action from an actual conflict. The Court does not decide whether tort duties are standards under §1392(d), every effect of the saving clause when a standard exists, or a federal field-wide ceiling. It does not find all air-brake regulation absent, invent an agency policy from an unproduced revision, or decide defect, accident causation, consortium entitlement, wrongful-death recovery, or damages.

#### Precedent Treatment

Easterwood and Cipollone require attention to actual coverage and the enacted clause. Cipollone is not read as universally foreclosing an independent actual-conflict inquiry. Gade supplies no automatic displacement from overlap and no Court-wide physical-impossibility-only rule. Paccar's judicial invalidation does not establish a protected federal freedom from the asserted tort duties.

#### Law After Decision

The presented claims survive the asserted express and conflict-preemption grounds because no applicable in-effect antilock standard or demonstrated conflicting federal command or objective establishes displacement. A saving clause preserves its actual terms without supplying immunity from every actual conflict. Valid operative standards and proved affirmative no-regulation policies remain distinct possibilities; no product-liability merits finding follows.

### Heintz v. Jenkins, No. 94-367. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Heintz_v_Jenkins_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Regular attorney debt collectors remain covered when they use litigation

**Holding and operative rule:** The FDCPA applies to an attorney who regularly collects or attempts to collect covered consumer debts owed or asserted to be owed another, including through litigation, subject to the statute's actual definition, exceptions, elements and defenses. There is no categorical attorney-litigation exemption; the decision does not cover lawyers merely because they practice law or establish that this settlement letter violated the Act.

**Authority:** Justice Souter's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct rationale and judgment votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that collection litigation is a means of collecting a debt within the enacted definition. The statute covers regular direct or indirect collection for another, and Congress deleted the former attorney exemption in 1986. Gardner supports enforcing those retained conditions without restoring an exclusion Congress removed. The strongest contrary argument is that rules regulating collection threats and communications can interact awkwardly with ordinary judicial proceedings. That concern does not justify excluding every litigating attorney from the definition. Congress retained an activity-specific exclusion for serving or attempting to serve legal process in connection with judicial debt enforcement, not a general occupational exclusion. Questions about a particular threat, communication or defense must be decided under the provision actually presented. An unsuccessful collection action is not automatically unlawful, and coverage does not prove the alleged insurance demand false or unauthorized. The complaint therefore returns for the ordinary inquiries into regularity, contractual authorization, alleged misrepresentation and defenses, without a liability or damages finding.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brown v. Gardner, December 12, 1994: applies respect for enacted conditions and rejection of an added category restriction; it does not create a new FDCPA proof rule or resolve conduct liability.
- Green v. Hocking, 9 F.3d 18: rejects the lower-court categorical litigation-exemption approach as inconsistent with the enacted definition and attorney-exemption repeal; it supplies no continuing exemption in this Court's law.

**Operative statutory terms and questions not reached:** A covered debt is an obligation or alleged obligation of a consumer to pay money arising from a transaction whose money, property, insurance or services are primarily for personal, family or household purposes, whether or not reduced to judgment. The retained §1692a(6)(D) exception concerns a person while serving or attempting to serve legal process on another in connection with judicial enforcement of a debt; it does not create a whole-attorney exemption. No factual statutory exception is established here.

Section 1692e prohibits false, deceptive or misleading representations in debt collection; §1692f(1) addresses collection of an amount not expressly authorized by the agreement creating the debt or permitted by law. The alleged $4,173 charge remains to be tested under those provisions. Section 1692e(5) concerns threatened action that cannot legally be taken or is not intended; failure to prevail alone does not necessarily satisfy it. No authoritative resolution of those conduct applications is made.

The communication and defense provisions invoked in the objection retain their exact conditions. Under §1692c(c), a consumer's written refusal to pay or written request to cease communication bars further communications except to advise that collection efforts are terminated, to notify that the collector or creditor may invoke specified remedies ordinarily invoked by that collector or creditor, or, where applicable, to notify that the collector or creditor intends to invoke a specified remedy. A mailed notice is complete on receipt. These are alternative permissions, not mandatory notices. Under §1692k(c), the collector must prove by a preponderance that a violation was unintentional and resulted from bona fide error despite procedures reasonably adapted to avoid it; no general immunity for legal error is decided. Under §1692k(e), good-faith conformity with an FTC advisory opinion protects an act or omission even if the opinion is later amended, rescinded or held invalid; staff commentary is not automatically such an opinion. Section 814(d) denies the FTC and the other agencies assigned enforcement responsibility under §814(b) authority to promulgate trade-regulation rules or other regulations concerning collection of debts by debt collectors as defined in the Act. No defense is established on these allegations, and no comprehensive litigation exception or fee entitlement is adopted.

#### Precedent Treatment

Gardner supports applying the statutory conditions without a judicial category exemption. Green v. Hocking's contrary lower-court exemption is rejected. The decision settles regular-attorney/litigation coverage, not authoritative interpretations of every conduct-regulating provision or defense.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 18, 1995, an attorney's regular collection of covered consumer debts for another is not exempt because it occurs through litigation. Coverage remains distinct from the ordinary elements and defenses of a particular claim. Neither a failed lawsuit nor the presence of a settlement letter automatically creates liability, privilege, good faith, damages or fees. The actual statutory exceptions operate on their stated terms, and no comprehensive litigation exemption is created.

### Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, No. 94-38. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Lanphere_and_Urbaniak_v_Colorado_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### The intended-speech condition requires commercial-speech review

**Holding and operative rule:** When a State otherwise makes covered records available, a rule denying access and prohibiting use because the requester intends truthful, lawful commercial solicitation is subject to the governing commercial-speech standard. The absence of a general entitlement to every government record does not remove this speech-selected condition from First Amendment review; a qualified right to particular court records is left undecided.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justices O'Connor and Scalia dissent from the application and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the challenged line concerns expression as well as access. Colorado prohibits using the covered records and their information to solicit business for pecuniary gain and conditions inspection on a promise against direct solicitation. Identical record requests thus receive different treatment because of the protected communication the requester plans to make. Central Hudson governs truthful, nonmisleading commercial expression concerning lawful activity. Shapero establishes that targeted legal-service mail falls within that protection, while Discovery Network requires justification for a selected commercial category. Colorado's custody of the records does not erase these requirements. Its strongest argument distinguishes access to information from later communication of information already acquired, but the challenged enactment regulates both access and use within an existing disclosure regime. The Court need not decide whether experience and logic independently guarantee access to these particular court materials. Nor does this holding compel disclosure of confidential files, create records, or forbid neutral access conditions. It requires review of the speech condition actually imposed.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corp. v. Public Service Commission, 447 U.S. 557: applies commercial-speech protection to truthful solicitation about lawful services.
- Shapero v. Kentucky Bar Association, 486 U.S. 466: applies protection for truthful targeted mail while distinguishing the separate source-of-information question.
- Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, March 24, 1993: applies review of a speech-selected commercial category notwithstanding governmental control of the facility involved.
- Houchins v. KQED, Inc., 438 U.S. 1: does not establish an absolute governmental privilege to attach this speech restriction to otherwise available records; no general access holding is added.
- Nixon v. Warner Communications, Inc., 435 U.S. 589: does not establish an absolute governmental privilege to attach this speech restriction to otherwise available records; no general access holding is added.
- Press-Enterprise Co. v. Superior Court, 478 U.S. 1: preserves the separate experience-and-logic inquiry for qualified access; its application to these records is not decided.

### Colorado has not demonstrated material advancement in this application

**Holding and operative rule:** For a restriction on truthful, lawful commercial solicitation, the State must demonstrate real harms and material advancement of its substantial interests in the relevant setting. Colorado's generalized privacy, abuse-prevention, and public-confidence justifications do not establish that this categorical use-and-access restriction materially advances those interests as applied to these plaintiffs' proposed direct mail.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justices O'Connor and Scalia dissent from the application and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that substantial interests do not dispense with proof of the connection between those interests and the chosen burden. Edenfield requires real harms and material advancement in the particular professional setting; it does not invariably demand empirical studies. Privacy for persons merely accused of offenses, prevention of solicitation abuse, and confidence in justice are substantial concerns. But Colorado has not shown why denying these otherwise available names and addresses to these speakers materially protects those interests. The lower court inferred meaningful privacy simply from the plaintiffs' persistence in seeking access. That inference does not establish the effect of the selected restriction. The conceded availability of the information through the El Paso County News further bears on that explanation, without creating a rule that every privacy measure must cover every source. Shapero distinguishes targeted mail from coercive personal confrontation. No finding of unlawful or misleading proposed mail answers that distinction here. Supported controls on actual deception, harassment, or protected information remain available.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Edenfield v. Fane, April 26, 1993: applies the real-harm and material-advancement burden without requiring studies in every case.
- Shapero v. Kentucky Bar Association, 486 U.S. 466: applies the distinction between targeted mail and coercive personal solicitation; the Court does not adjudicate unexamined letter-specific content.
- Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, March 24, 1993: preserves the need for a connection between the commercial selection and the harm, without an all-sources-at-once rule.
- Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation, June 13, 1994: applies the requirement of justification responsive to truthful professional expression; actual deception remains regulable.

### The categorical prohibition lacks the required reasonable fit

**Holding and operative rule:** The restriction must reasonably fit the State's substantial interests and may not burden more truthful, lawful commercial speech than that fit permits; the State need not employ the least restrictive means. This categorical prohibition and access assurance lack the required fit as applied, because Colorado has not justified selecting every proposed direct solicitation over neutral access protections or measures addressing actual abuses.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justices O'Connor and Scalia dissent from the application and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the wholesale speech criterion is broader than Colorado has justified. Fox requires a reasonable, proportionate fit rather than the least restrictive conceivable measure. Discovery Network requires the chosen category to correspond to the asserted harm. Colorado distinguishes the point of official disclosure from the later mailing and argues that withholding information imposes only an indirect burden. That distinction deserves consideration, but it cannot establish fit by itself when the statute also prohibits the information's solicitation use and selects otherwise identical requests by intended expression. The record does not explain why neutral redaction, delayed release, opt-out protections, retrieval fees, or direct regulation of deceptive, coercive, and harassing solicitation are inadequate to support a more fitted response. The Court does not mandate any particular replacement system or find that every alternative is required in every case. It rejects this categorical criterion on this record. Confidential records and independently justified neutral limits remain subject to their own rules.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Board of Trustees of State University of New York v. Fox, 492 U.S. 469: applies reasonable fit and preserves rejection of a least-restrictive-means requirement.
- Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, March 24, 1993: applies the relationship between the selected commercial category and the harm; alternatives are relevant without requiring regulation of all sources simultaneously.
- Shapero v. Kentucky Bar Association, 486 U.S. 466: preserves appropriately directed safeguards against misleading or abusive mail instead of an unsupported blanket barrier.
- Edenfield v. Fane, April 26, 1993: retains protection for materially different professional settings and supported regulation of deception or coercion.

#### Precedent Treatment

Central Hudson and Fox remain the governing framework. Discovery Network, Edenfield, and Ibanez supply the selected-category, material-advancement, and truthful-professional-speech principles applied here. Shapero protects targeted mail without alone determining access to government information. Houchins and Nixon do not turn the absence of a general access entitlement into an absolute privilege to burden speech; the Court leaves the separate qualified-access inquiry under Press-Enterprise undecided. No precedent is overruled, and no strict-scrutiny or commercial/political-speech equality rule is adopted.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 18, 1995, the challenged solicitation-use prohibition and access assurance cannot sustain withholding otherwise available information in this application under Central Hudson and Fox. Material advancement and reasonable fit remain distinct requirements; each deficient showing supports the judgment. The ruling does not require disclosure of confidential records, resolve access to every court filing or proceeding, command creation of a list, or invalidate independently justified neutral access rules. Regulation of false, deceptive, coercive, or harassing solicitation remains available. Seven Justices directly join the controlling explanation; no Marks inquiry is needed.

### Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc., No. 93-1121. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Plaut_v_Spendthrift_Farm_Inc_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Section 27A(b) cannot reopen this completed private federal judgment

**Holding and operative rule:** Article III prevents §27A(b) from requiring reinstatement of this private §10(b) action after its dismissal with prejudice became final without appeal before December 19, 1991. Satisfaction of the statute's reinstatement conditions does not overcome that constitutional limit; the denial of reinstatement is affirmed without deciding pending cases, judgments still on direct review, continuing prospective relief, or independently lawful judicial reopening.

**Authority:** Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: seven direct rationale and judgment votes. Stone-Zsela also concurs separately; his broader formulation is not the Court's holding. Justices Stevens and Ginsburg dissent.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Morgan Stanley governs this completed private limitations judgment. That decision accepts Congress's genuine amendment of limitations law but rejects this command to reopen a dismissal already final without appeal. Robertson permits changed law in pending litigation; it does not erase the completed-judgment boundary. McCullough recognizes completed adjudication, and Klein protects the judicial function without prohibiting genuine statutory amendment. The plaintiffs satisfied §27A(b)'s conditions and timely sought reinstatement, so their motion fails on Article III grounds, not statutory ineligibility. Their strongest argument is that Congress restored earlier filing expectations while leaving fraud and defenses for judicial resolution. Morgan Stanley considered that distinction: further merits work cannot make this completed dismissal nonfinal. No altered statutory command, relevant record difference, intervening displacement, or demonstrated unworkability warrants reconsideration here. Wheeling Bridge concerns continuing prospective relief, and Sioux Nation concerns the Government's waiver of its own defense. The Court applies the existing confined rule, leaving broader remedial legislation, other statutory applications, due process, and fraud liability undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994: applies its as-applied Article III rule to the same completed, unappealed private-dismissal setting without enlarging it.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, March 25, 1992: preserves genuine legislative changes governing pending cases; it does not authorize the present reopening of a completed judgment.
- McCullough v. Virginia, 172 U.S. 102: applies the distinction between changing law and disturbing completed adjudication.
- United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128: preserves protection of the judicial function without treating actual legislative amendment as forbidden.
- Pennsylvania v. Wheeling & Belmont Bridge Co., 59 U.S. (18 How.) 421: distinguishes adjustment of the continuing prospective operation of equitable relief.
- United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371: distinguishes sovereign waiver of the Government's own final-judgment defense from compulsory reopening between private parties.
- Chase Securities Corp. v. Donaldson, 325 U.S. 304: leaves its due-process revival rule intact; it does not resolve the Article III question presented.

**Statutory conditions and reservations:** Section 27A(b) addresses a private §10(b) action commenced on or before June 19, 1991, dismissed as time barred after that date, and timely under the law of the jurisdiction, including retroactivity principles, as it existed June 19, 1991. It directs reinstatement on a plaintiff's motion within sixty days after December 19, 1991. These conditions were satisfied here, including the February 11, 1992 motion. Subsection (a), other applications of subsection (b), general severability, constitutional due process, fraud, damages, federal waiver, and independently authorized judicial reopening are not decided. The Court adopts no comprehensive prohibition governing every later legislative effect on every final order.

#### Precedent Treatment

Morgan Stanley controls without enlargement. Robertson retains legislative amendment of pending-case law; McCullough and Klein protect completed adjudication without denying that genuine amendment occurred. Wheeling Bridge and Sioux Nation remain distinct prospective-relief and sovereign-waiver settings. Chase Securities remains a due-process revival authority. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

The existing May 23, 1994 Article III rule remains operative: §27A(b) cannot compel reopening of the completed, unappealed private dismissal presented here. This decision applies that rule rather than establishing a universal prohibition on every legislative effect on every final order. Stone-Zsela's broader formulation and the two dissenters' proposed reconsideration are noncontrolling. Statutory timeliness is satisfied, and neither fraud nor liability is adjudicated.

### Shalala v. Whitecotton, No. 94-372. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Shalala_v_Whitecotton_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### The Table onset route requires the first manifestation of the injury

**Holding and operative rule:** A claimant using the applicable Table's onset route must establish that the first symptom or manifestation of onset of the claimed Table injury occurred within the prescribed period after vaccination; for the DPT encephalopathy claim here, that period is three days. A later manifestation of the same already manifested injury does not satisfy first onset, but no finding that Maggie had that injury before vaccination is made or insulated from review by this construction.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct rationale votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that first onset means the beginning of the claimed injury, not simply its first manifestation after vaccination. The statutory timing requirement would lose its work if any later symptom of the same injury sufficed. Gardner requires respect for an enacted condition while preserving the protection that follows its satisfaction. The Table relieves a qualifying claimant of proving the vaccine's actual causal mechanism; it does not eliminate the qualifying onset requirement. The lower court's comparison with the residual-seizure provision does not overcome the first-onset language. Nor does the separate unrelated-factor defense require the contrary reading: a prior cause need not have produced an earlier manifestation of the Table injury. Significant aggravation remains a separate route for an injury already present. The Court therefore reverses the compensation direction without deciding whether the master's preexisting-injury finding is factually sound. A small head circumference or other prior abnormality alone does not establish the identity, onset, cause, or aggravation of this child's injury.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brown v. Gardner, December 12, 1994: applies respect for distinct statutory conditions without inserting a universal actual-causation requirement into the Table route; its veterans-benefits rule is not transplanted.
- Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc., June 28, 1993: leaves expert-evidence admissibility separate from the Vaccine Act's entitlement predicates; no new admissibility determination is made.

### Preserve significant aggravation and the separate rebuttal stage on remand

**Holding and operative rule:** A qualifying significant-aggravation claim and an otherwise available actual-causation claim remain distinct from first onset. The claimant's statutory entitlement showing by a preponderance precedes the Secretary's separate burden to establish an unrelated factor by a preponderance; the disputed preexisting-injury finding, preserved alternative routes, and consolidated new-evidence proceeding return for lawful consideration without an award, final denial, or hypothetical rebuttal ruling.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct rationale votes on the sequence and bounded remand.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that correcting the onset construction does not decide the family's remaining entitlement. Congress separately provided significant aggravation and actual causation, and the lower courts must consider properly preserved routes under their own requirements. Greenwich Collieries distinguishes persuasion, presumptions, and separately allocated defenses; the Vaccine Act itself supplies the governing allocations here, not the APA. The Secretary's unrelated-factor burden arises only after the necessary claimant showing. Section 13's exclusion of idiopathic, unexplained, unknown, hypothetical, and undocumentable causes from that defense does not dispense with the initial predicate. Conversely, a prior abnormality cannot automatically defeat compensation or shift the Secretary's burden to the claimant. The Court does not decide whether a specifically identified underlying condition may qualify as unrelated although its ultimate origin is unknown. That dispute and the sufficiency of any rebuttal are premature without a qualifying claim. The new-evidence procedural component likewise returns without an invented mootness or Rule 60 determination. The lawful remand preserves factual review and statutory alternatives, not a predetermined medical outcome.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Director, OWCP v. Greenwich Collieries, June 20, 1994: applies only the distinction among persuasion, presumptions, and separately allocated defenses; the Vaccine Act's own burdens govern.
- Brown v. Gardner, December 12, 1994: preserves the distinction between an eligibility condition and additional grounds not supplied by the statute; no compensation follows automatically from removing a legal error.

**Operative qualifications and nonreach:** Section 13(a)(2)(A) excludes any idiopathic, unexplained, unknown, hypothetical, or undocumentable cause, factor, injury, illness, or condition from unrelated factors. That provision is distinct from §14(b)(3)(B), which excludes an encephalopathy from the Table when a preponderance establishes that its cause is infection, toxins, trauma, or metabolic disturbances; those specified Table causes were not asserted by the Secretary in this appeal. No application of either provision is decided. The August 2, 1990 petition uses §2114(a) and (b) as in effect February 8, 1995: the March 10, 1995 revision applies only to petitions filed on or after March 10. The earlier Table and its qualifications both remain applicable. No new definition of significant aggravation, final preexisting-injury finding, actual-causation determination, or new-evidence merits ruling is adopted.

#### Precedent Treatment

Gardner supports keeping distinct statutory predicates and consequences separate. Greenwich Collieries supplies a distinction among burdens, not an imported APA allocation. Daubert remains separate evidentiary law and decides no medical or entitlement issue here. The Court does not resolve the lower Koston-related rebuttal dispute before it becomes necessary.

#### Law After Decision

First onset remains a true temporal predicate under the applicable Table, distinct from significant aggravation and actual causation. A qualifying claimant retains the statutory Table benefit without proving a vaccine mechanism; the Secretary retains the assigned preponderance rebuttal burden subject to the actual unrelated-factor exclusions. This decision corrects the onset construction without deciding medical entitlement or every rebuttal issue. The 1990 petition remains under the Table and qualifications effective February 8, 1995.

### Celotex Corp. v. Edwards, No. 93-1504. — 1995-04-19

Authority: [Record](../records/Celotex_Corp_v_Edwards_merits_1995-04-19.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### The operative June injunction reaches this proposed bond enforcement

**Holding and operative rule:** The June 13, 1991 order expressly continued the §105 restraint and required relief from the issuing bankruptcy court before collection on bonds whose appeals had concluded when the bankruptcy petition was filed. Its coverage reaches the Edwards bond proceeding authorized in May 1992; this conclusion does not depend on an automatic §362 stay, estate ownership of the matured bond, or a finding of contempt.

**Authority:** This portion of Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court is joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes for this portion. Stone-Zsela's join does not extend to the separate actual-relatedness, affirmative interim-authority, or complete collateral-review portions. Justices Stevens and Ginsburg dissent from the judgment on the distinct §157 allocation objection.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the order operative when enforcement was authorized must be considered in full. The Fifth Circuit focused on the October 17 language concerning proceedings involving a debtor and distinguished Northbrook's independent undertaking. The June 13 order expressly addresses the omitted circumstance: a creditor whose appellate process had already concluded when the petition was filed must first seek relief from the continued §105 restraint before proceeding against the bond. The Edwardses filed their enforcement motion before June, but the Texas court authorized execution almost a year after that order. The independent nature of the surety's obligation therefore does not remove the proceeding from the later command's terms. Rule 65.1 permits enforcement of surety liability by motion; it does not itself dissolve an applicable injunction. The Court neither changes October's words retroactively nor decides a personal-notice or contempt question. Northbrook's undertaking and Celotex's tort debt remain distinct, and the validity of the restraint is a separate inquiry.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Rule 65.1 retains its motion procedure and surety-jurisdiction function; it does not itself answer the effect of another court's applicable injunction.
- United States v. United Mine Workers, 330 U.S. 258: preserves the distinction between the operative order and objections to its validity; no sanction or culpability finding is imported.

### Immediate execution on this bond is related to the Chapter 11 case

**Holding and operative rule:** Whether the Edwardses may immediately execute against Northbrook on this supersedeas bond is at least related to Celotex's Chapter 11 case under §1334(b). The bond, retained insurance-settlement collateral, and reorganization connection establish actual nonexclusive subject-matter relatedness; this holding does not establish exact depletion, final core authority, the outer limits of relatedness, or an independent jurisdictional grant under §105.

**Authority:** This portion of Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court is joined by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: six direct full-rationale votes, including the author. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela does not join this stronger resolution and concurs in the judgment on his separate colorable-jurisdiction ground. Stevens and Ginsburg's acceptance of district-court relatedness is not a join in this complete rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the immediate-execution proceeding is related to this bankruptcy. Section 1334(b) reaches related civil proceedings and expressly makes that jurisdiction nonexclusive; it is not limited to actions against the debtor or estate-owned property. Northbrook's independent bond obligation coexists with the retained insurance-settlement proceeds securing it and the administration and prospects of Celotex's reorganization. Those supplied connections establish the statutory relation. The Fifth Circuit's concern about the unproduced particular contract and concrete depletion does not require treating that relation as merely possible. It limits assertions of automatic offset, quantified loss, successful avoidance, and the eventual justification for continued restraint. The Court makes none of those findings. Bancorp preserves the distinction between procedural power and unpresented underlying merits; it does not itself decide relatedness. Section 105 supplies no jurisdiction that §1334 lacks. Final core classification, consent, and the ultimate injunction merits remain distinct questions. The Court decides this proceeding's relation without adopting a comprehensive outer-boundary test for every economically consequential third-party suit.

**Precedent treatment:**

- U.S. Bancorp Mortgage Co. v. Bonner Mall Partnership, November 8, 1994: preserves the distinction between procedural power and unpresented underlying merits; its holding does not independently determine bankruptcy relatedness.

### Section 157 permits this interim restraint without a final core determination

**Holding and operative rule:** In this related proceeding, §157(c)(1)'s restriction on a bankruptcy judge's final noncore adjudication does not deprive that judge of authority to enter this interlocutory restraint preserving the matter for proper determination. That sufficient interim authority defeats the asserted collateral-displacement ground without deciding final core status, all-parties consent, every equitable predicate, or the ultimate propriety and duration of the §105 injunction.

**Authority:** This portion of Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court is joined by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: six direct full-rationale votes, including the author. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela does not join this stronger resolution and concurs in the judgment on his separate colorable-jurisdiction ground. Stevens and Ginsburg's acceptance of district-court relatedness is not a join in this complete rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the bankruptcy judge has sufficient interim adjudicative authority for the collateral posture presented. Section 157 separates referral, core determination, and noncore final-order procedure. For a noncore related matter, proposed findings ordinarily go to the district court for final judgment and review of timely, specific objections; all-parties consent provides a separate route. Neither final core status nor consent is established here. The dissent treats that final-order allocation as disabling this judge from restraining the Article III bond court. The Court rejects that objection to interim preservation of this related matter. The restraint does not conduct a new tort trial or finally award the bond. United Mine Workers distinguishes preservation while authority is contested from ultimate entitlement to relief; its analogy does not itself interpret §157. The statutory distinction between interim and final adjudication supplies the present conclusion. A core label or §105 alone cannot override statutory limits. The separate personal-injury trial and liquidation restrictions and the ultimate injunction merits remain intact.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. United Mine Workers, 330 U.S. 258: extends its interim-preservation reasoning to this substantial bankruptcy authority question without treating arguable authority as final legality.
- Connecticut National Bank v. Germain, 503 U.S. 249: preserves distinct statutory functions and qualifying review routes; it does not independently validate the injunction or decide §157 classification.
- Swint v. Chambers County Commission, March 1, 1995: preserves attention to the actual authorized judicial act; its appellate holding is not a grant of bankruptcy injunction power.

### This still-operative restraint must be challenged through orderly relief and review

**Holding and operative rule:** The actual §1334(b) relation, sufficient interim §157 authority, substantial support for this applicable restraint, and concrete relief/review mechanism require a challenge through the issuing proceeding and authorized review rather than contrary execution elsewhere while the restraint remains effective. The holding preserves the prior authorities' jurisdictional and frustrated-review reservations and does not validate the injunction's ultimate merits or any contempt sanction.

**Authority:** This portion of Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court is joined by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: six direct full-rationale votes, including the author. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela does not join this stronger resolution and concurs in the judgment on his separate colorable-jurisdiction ground. Stevens and Ginsburg's acceptance of district-court relatedness is not a join in this complete rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Texas enforcement proceeding cannot substitute for proper challenge to this restraint. The established relatedness and sufficient interim authority support that conclusion together; a bare assertion of jurisdiction would not suffice. United Mine Workers requires obedience to unreversed orders issued with jurisdiction and permits interim preservation of substantial jurisdictional questions, excluding frivolous and insubstantial claims. Walker distinguishes transparent invalidity or a frivolous pretense and reserves timely challenge frustrated by delay. Neither supplies a universal exception or makes every substantial objection a license to disregard an order. The June motion route places the continuation burden on Celotex and permits statutory review; no frustrated timely challenge is established. Concurrent power over the bond does not confer appellate authority over the restraint. This is an application of orderly-review reasoning to a civil collection proceeding, not a contempt prosecution. Under United Mine Workers, civil remedial relief falls with an erroneously issued injunction; criminal punishment may stand differently. No such post-invalidation relief or contempt judgment is presented.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. United Mine Workers, 330 U.S. 258: applies orderly relief and review with substantial-jurisdiction and duration limits; preserves the different consequences for criminal punishment and civil remedies after invalidation.
- Walker v. City of Birmingham, 388 U.S. 307: applies orderly-challenge reasoning while preserving its transparent-invalidity, frivolous-pretense, and frustrated-timely-review reservations; no announcement of knowing defiance, notice finding, or sanction is imported.
- Swint v. Chambers County Commission, March 1, 1995: preserves the requirement of an actual authorized review route; ordinary power over one proceeding does not confer appellate power over another court's order.

### The execution authorization must yield while the creditors retain qualified relief and review routes

**Holding and operative rule:** The contrary execution authorization must be set aside while the §105 restraint remains effective; the creditors may seek relief in the issuing bankruptcy proceeding, where Celotex bears the burden to justify continuation, and use an authorized review route. Section 158 preserves its finality and leave requirements, Germain preserves a separately qualifying §1292 route, and the decision neither dissolves the injunction nor orders permanent restraint or payment of the bond.

**Authority:** This portion of Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court is joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes for this portion. Stone-Zsela's join does not extend to the separate actual-relatedness, affirmative interim-authority, or complete collateral-review portions. Justices Stevens and Ginsburg dissent from the judgment on the distinct §157 allocation objection.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that reversal preserves proper adjudication of the creditors' remaining objections and their independent bond entitlement. The June order directs successful judgment creditors to request relief and places the continuation burden on the debtor. Final §157 classification and procedure, supporting evidence, ultimate statutory authority, and duration remain open; the Court's actual-relatedness holding is not erased. Section 158(a) directs final-order appeals, and interlocutory appeals with leave, to the district court where the bankruptcy judge serves, here the Middle District of Florida. Qualifying final appellate decisions receive Eleventh Circuit review under §158(d). Germain preserves a separately available §1292 route without removing its conditions or making every interlocutory order immediately appealable. Withdrawal of reference under §157(d) has distinct predicates. The Court sets aside the contrary execution authorization without changing the tort judgment, awarding the bond, ordering recoupment, or deciding whether the injunction should ultimately survive. The issuing court need not await every possible appeal before modifying or dissolving its order.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Connecticut National Bank v. Germain, 503 U.S. 249: applies coexistence of §158(d) and an independently qualifying §1292 route, preserving each route's conditions.
- United States v. United Mine Workers, 330 U.S. 258: preserves the order's force only while operative and the availability of appropriate appellate or other relief.
- Walker v. City of Birmingham, 388 U.S. 307: retains the significance of meaningful access to orderly relief; no unconditional immediate-appeal right is inferred.

#### Precedent Treatment

United Mine Workers supplies distinct interim-preservation and unreversed-order principles, limited by substantial jurisdiction and its civil/criminal remedial distinction. Walker supplies qualified orderly-review reasoning while reserving transparent invalidity, frivolous pretense, and frustrated timely challenge; its contempt facts are not imported. The Court applies those principles to this still-operative civil bankruptcy restraint without deciding its ultimate merits. Germain preserves overlapping statutory review at its actual scope; Swint and Bancorp retain their different procedural holdings and do not grant bankruptcy injunction power. No precedent is overruled and no §1481 legislative-history rule is adopted.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 19, 1995, immediate execution on this bond is actually related to Celotex's Chapter 11 case under §1334(b), and §157(c)(1)'s final-order limitation does not defeat the sufficient interim authority identified for this collateral posture. Six Justices directly join those holdings and the complete qualified collateral-review rationale. Stone-Zsela supplies a seventh reversal vote on a narrower colorable-jurisdiction ground and joins the June-coverage and execution/review-remedy portions. Stevens and Ginsburg accept district-court relatedness while dissenting from the interim-authority and judgment conclusions; their assent does not add full-rationale joins. The Court does not equate relatedness with final core adjudication, make §105 a jurisdiction grant, treat a core label as dispositive, or accept management difficulty alone. Final classification, consent, ultimate statutory and equitable validity, evidence for continuation, and duration remain distinct. No Marks synthesis is needed.

### Statutory distinctions and preserved routes

Section 1334(b) grants original but nonexclusive district-court jurisdiction over civil proceedings arising under title 11, arising in a title 11 case, or related to one, notwithstanding an Act of Congress conferring exclusive jurisdiction on a court other than the district courts. It differs from §1334(a)'s original and exclusive jurisdiction over cases under title 11 and the then-applicable §1334(d)'s exclusive jurisdiction, in the district where the case begins or is pending, over the debtor's property wherever located as of commencement and property of the estate. The 1994 amendments do not apply to this bankruptcy commenced in 1990; the later property designation (e), new jury provision, and changed appellate-panel/election provisions supply no rule here.

Section 157(a) permits a district court to refer title 11 cases and proceedings arising under title 11, arising in such a case, or related to one to its bankruptcy judges. Under §157(b)(1), referred core proceedings arising under title 11 or in a title 11 case may be heard and determined, with appropriate orders subject to §158 review. The bankruptcy court called its bond-property determination core under §157(b)(2)(A), (G), and (O); that is its classification, not this Court's final adoption. Those clauses concern estate administration; motions to terminate, annul, or modify the automatic stay; and other proceedings affecting liquidation of estate assets or adjustment of debtor-creditor or equity-security-holder relationships, except personal-injury tort and wrongful-death claims. Section 157(b)(3) places the classification question before the bankruptcy judge on the judge's own motion or timely party motion and forbids a noncore classification solely because state law may affect the outcome. It does not make the classification unreviewable.

For a noncore but related proceeding, §157(c)(1) permits hearing and proposed findings and conclusions submitted to the district court; the final order belongs to the district judge after consideration and de novo review of the matters timely and specifically objected to. De novo review is not automatically required for every unobjected proposition. Section 157(c)(2) separately permits district-court referral for hearing, determination, and orders with all parties' consent, subject to §158 review. The record establishes neither consent nor an affirmative refusal; neither is presumed. Section 157(b)(5) requires the district court to order personal-injury tort and wrongful-death claims tried in the district where the bankruptcy is pending or where the claim arose, as the district court where the bankruptcy is pending determines. Section 157(b)(2)(B) includes allowance or disallowance of claims or estate exemptions and estimation for plan confirmation in its core category, but excludes liquidation or estimation of contingent or unliquidated personal-injury tort or wrongful-death claims for distribution. These distinct limits remain intact. No new tort trial or liquidation is authorized here.

Section 105(a) permits orders, process, or judgments necessary or appropriate to carry out title 11. A provision allowing a party in interest to raise an issue does not preclude the court from acting on its own when necessary or appropriate to enforce or implement orders or rules or prevent abuse of process. This is remedial authority, not an independent subject-matter jurisdiction grant or an override of §157. The June order distinguishes bonds during an ongoing appellate process, which that court regarded as estate property subject to §362, from bonds after a creditor succeeds through an authorized completed appeal and bonds whose appeals ended by the petition date. Its latter two categories require relief from the continued §105 restraint. The Court's coverage holding does not adopt every premise of the first category, decide §362's scope, or find that every completed bond remains estate property.

The issuing bankruptcy court may entertain a request to vacate or modify the restraint; its June order places the burden on Celotex to establish that continuation is warranted. The Court has settled the supplied §1334(b) relation and sufficient interim authority for this collateral posture. Its reservation of direct challenge does not reopen those conclusions as wholly undecided. Remaining lawful coverage questions, final §157 allocation and procedure, ultimate statutory injunction power, particular evidence, and duration remain available through proper proceedings. The lower court's possible avoidance, collateral-contract, and reorganization inquiries are not completed adjudications of avoidance recovery or exact estate loss on this bond.

Under the applicable §158(a), final bankruptcy orders go to the district court for the district where the bankruptcy judge serves, and interlocutory review requires leave: here the Middle District of Florida. Section 158(d) permits Eleventh Circuit review of qualifying final appellate decisions, not immediate circuit review of every interlocutory action. No bankruptcy appellate panel with the necessary institutional and consent predicates is established. Germain preserves a separately qualifying §1292 route from a district court's bankruptcy appellate order. Section 1292(b), when invoked, requires written district-court findings that a controlling question of law presents substantial ground for difference of opinion and that immediate appeal may materially advance termination, application within ten days, and circuit permission; the application itself does not stay proceedings. No finding that these predicates have already been met is made.

Section 157(d) withdrawal of reference is separate. The district court may withdraw for cause on its own motion or timely party motion; withdrawal on timely motion is required when resolution requires the specified consideration of both title 11 and other federal laws regulating organizations or activities affecting interstate commerce. No withdrawal motion or qualifying finding is invented. Ordinary review and withdrawal are not interchangeable.

### Prior-authority qualifications

United Mine Workers permits temporary preservation of a substantial jurisdictional question and expressly excludes a frivolous and insubstantial jurisdictional claim. Its distinct unreversed-order formulation concerns a court with jurisdiction over subject matter and person. Its orders remain operative until expiration or appropriate relief. After invalidation, remedial civil relief falls with an erroneously issued injunction, and still more clearly with an order beyond jurisdiction; criminal punishment may have a different survival rule, subject to independent procedural and remedial objections. Compensation, coercion, and punishment are not interchangeable. This case imposes none of them and finds neither contempt nor knowing personal violation.

Walker preserves its reservation for transparent invalidity or only a frivolous pretense to validity and its different constitutional posture for timely challenge met by delay or frustration. It does not define a universal exception or hold that any objection automatically licenses disregard. No such frustrated timely challenge is established here. Its treatment of Green does not create a general ban on jurisdictional objections. Its clear-notice, announced-defiance, two-day timing, and Alabama expedited-review facts are not imported. The present explicit motion, debtor-burden, and statutory review mechanisms supply the concrete basis for orderly challenge; no unconditional immediate appellate remedy is presumed.

### McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, No. 93-986 (current petitioner: executor of Margaret McIntyre's estate). — 1995-04-19

Authority: [Record](../records/McIntyre_v_Ohio_Elections_Commission_merits_1995-04-19.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Compelled identification on these political leaflets requires exacting scrutiny

**Holding and operative rule:** A content-defined requirement that an individual identify herself on ordinary political issue leaflets burdens core political expression, including the choice to speak anonymously. The State must satisfy exacting scrutiny by showing narrow tailoring to an overriding state interest; viewpoint neutrality or the election setting alone does not lower that standard.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the author's choice to omit her identity is protected as part of political expression. Talley protects anonymous handbills and recognizes the place of anonymous publication in public discussion. Ohio's requirement selects publications by their electoral subject and conditions their circulation on identification. It is therefore a content-based burden on the individual's communication, not simply a neutral rule for administering a ballot. Burson preserves demanding scrutiny for campaign-specific restrictions even though its different polling-place judgment had no shared validity rationale. Ohio invokes the modest size of the identification obligation and its election-related purpose. Neither answers whether the State may impose the burden on this ordinary issue leaflet. The Court applies exacting scrutiny requiring narrow tailoring to an overriding interest. It does not decide that anonymity is always benign or require proof that this speaker faced retaliation. Candidate finance reports, organizational attribution, coordinated spending, and fraudulent impersonation present distinct questions left outside this holding.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies protection of anonymous handbills to this individual political leaflet; the Court does not establish immunity for fraudulent communications.
- Burson v. Freeman, May 26, 1992: applies the independently majority-supported scrutiny distinction; its fractured polling-place validity judgment does not settle this leaflet requirement.
- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780: distinguishes the election-administration setting; it does not reduce scrutiny for every election-related speech restriction.

### The asserted voter-information interest does not justify this identification demand

**Holding and operative rule:** A general interest in helping voters evaluate a source does not justify requiring this ordinary individual to place her name and residence or business address on her independently produced, truthful political leaflet. The Court leaves candidate finance, campaign committees, organizational spending, and other materially distinct disclosure settings for separate analysis.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that voters' interest in source information does not justify this direct condition on an ordinary citizen's political pamphlet. Talley protects the decision to publish without personal attribution; an author need not establish that identity has no possible informational value. Ohio relies on Buckley's campaign-finance disclosure and Bellotti's discussion of identifying corporate advertising. Those sources concern different mechanisms and interests. A report identifying financial participation in a campaign is not the same requirement as putting a private individual's name and address on every issue leaflet she distributes. Corporate spending likewise supplies no automatic rule for this record, which establishes neither a campaign organization nor coordinated financing. The Court does not hold that source information is useless or that all disclosure is forbidden. It holds that the general informational rationale is inadequate for the burden imposed here. Ohio's committee alternatives and personal-correspondence exception do not transform Margaret McIntyre's independently produced leaflets into those different statutory categories or justify this sanction.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies protection for the ordinary author's choice of anonymous publication.
- Buckley v. Valeo, 424 U.S. 1: distinguishes campaign-finance reporting and its specific interests; those disclosure holdings remain undisturbed.
- First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti, 435 U.S. 765: distinguishes corporate-advertising source information; its observation does not resolve this private individual's leaflet.

### Fraud prevention does not justify this application to truthful independent advocacy

**Holding and operative rule:** Although preventing electoral fraud is a weighty interest, Ohio may not impose this categorical identification requirement on truthful, independently produced individual issue leaflets without the necessary narrow fit to that interest. The enforcement order and $100 fine must be set aside; properly tailored fraud, impersonation, and materially different disclosure rules remain available.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Ohio's fraud-prevention interest does not supply the missing fit. Talley protects anonymous expression without preventing laws directed at false or fraudulent communications. Ohio's identification requirement reaches ordinary truthful advocacy regardless of any finding of deception, false attribution, or corrupt financing. The record contains no adjudicated fraud in McIntyre's leaflet. The State argues that identification helps investigators find wrongdoers and deters unlawful claims before they affect an election. That practical benefit does not explain why every individual issue pamphlet must carry a name and address when existing laws address fraud and false attribution more directly. The personal-correspondence exception and permission to exempt impractical small items do not cure this application to printed leaflets; no implementing exemption rule resolves the fine presented. The Court neither assumes that anonymity guarantees honest speech nor invalidates all disclosure provisions. It sets aside this sanction, preserves properly tailored enforcement against actual abuse, and leaves organizational and financial-reporting requirements outside the judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies anonymous-publication protection while distinguishing measures directed at fraud.
- Buckley v. Valeo, 424 U.S. 1: leaves candidate-finance disclosure and corruption-related interests intact rather than treating them as dispositive of this ordinary leaflet.
- First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti, 435 U.S. 765: leaves materially different corporate source-disclosure questions outside the individual sanction decided here.

#### Precedent Treatment

Talley's anonymous-handbill protection applies to this individual's political issue leaflet. Burson's majority-supported scrutiny distinction remains separate from its fractured validity judgment concerning polling-place environs. Anderson's election-administration framework does not decide this direct speech burden. Buckley's campaign-finance disclosure holdings and Bellotti's corporate-advertising discussion concern materially different interests and mechanisms and remain undisturbed. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 19, 1995, the State may not enforce this name-and-residence-or-business-address requirement against the truthful, independently produced individual political leaflets at issue. Seven Justices directly adopt exacting scrutiny and the inadequate information and fraud-prevention justifications; Thomas supplies a separate eighth judgment vote. No Marks synthesis is needed. The decision leaves candidate and committee disclosures, coordinated expenditures, large financial contributions, organizational spending, fraudulent impersonation, and appropriately tailored fraud regulation outside the adjudicated application. It does not create an automatic personal-correspondence or small-item exemption, decide an unproduced exemption rule, or adjudicate subsection (B).

### Kyles v. Whitley, No. 93-7927. — 1995-04-19

Authority: [Record](../records/Kyles_v_Whitley_merits_1995-04-19.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Collective suppression undermines confidence in this conviction

**Controlling proposition:** Suppressed favorable evidence is material when its collective lawful uses, considered in the whole record and in the defense’s preparation and presentation, create a reasonable probability of a different result sufficient to undermine confidence in the verdict. No item must independently satisfy that standard, and the defendant need not show acquittal more likely than conviction or that the remaining evidence is legally insufficient; the established suppression here meets the standard.

**Authority:** Justice Souter’s opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer: six direct votes for the rule, application and conviction-level relief. The dissent accepts collective assessment but disputes materiality on this record.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the undisclosed evidence changes the relationship among the prosecution’s apparently reinforcing proofs. Brady requires disclosure of material favorable evidence, and Bagley supplies a probability sufficient to undermine confidence rather than a demand for probable acquittal. Earlier identification accounts permit substantial impeachment; Beanie’s differing accounts support corroboration and examination of the investigation; and the garbage material bears on the provenance of tangible evidence. These uses reinforce one another. They do not all depend on calling Beanie or accepting every statement as true. The remaining identifications, ammunition, holster, pet food and credibility objections remain significant. But counting that evidence as sufficient does not answer how the defense could have challenged its apparent independence after disclosure. The plate list contributes only within its disputed completeness. The Court finds no planting, perjury or factual innocence. It resolves the established cumulative suppression and leaves later evidence and independently unadmitted constitutional claims outside this decision.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83: applies the duty to disclose material favorable evidence to the established suppression.
- United States v. Bagley, 473 U.S. 667: applies reasonable-probability materiality to the combined defense counterfactual, including lawful impeachment and preparation uses.
- Schlup v. Delo, January 23, 1995: distinguishes its gateway showing; it supplies no additional innocence prerequisite for this Brady claim.

### The prosecutor’s responsibility includes the investigating team

**Controlling proposition:** A prosecutor must learn of favorable evidence known to police acting on the government’s behalf in the case and disclose it when Brady requires disclosure; personal ignorance does not remove that evidence from the materiality inquiry. This case-specific responsibility does not impute all knowledge of every government office or establish unrestricted investigative or open-file duties.

**Authority:** Souter’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer: six direct votes. The dissent’s consideration of the police-held material does not supply additional votes for an affirmative organizational rule.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the prosecution cannot avoid its disclosure responsibility by separating the trial lawyer from police who worked on the case. Brady protects the fairness of the trial, and Bagley assesses the material effect of nondisclosure; that protection would fail if the same favorable information counted only when personally recalled by the prosecutor. Here the eyewitness statements, recorded accounts, plate list and investigative memorandum arose within the investigation used to prosecute Kyles. Their lawful defense uses therefore enter the same collective inquiry. The rule does not require every agency to search all government files, and it does not convert every nondisclosure into a completed constitutional violation. Materiality and the evidence’s favorable character still matter. United States v. Williams addresses a distinct federal grand-jury duty and its specified remedy conditions; its personal-knowledge limitation does not narrow trial Brady responsibility. The Court creates no open-file entitlement and excuses no lack of diligence concerning evidence already known to the defense.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brady and Bagley: apply their trial-disclosure protection to information held by police acting for this prosecution.
- United States v. Williams, May 4, 1992: distinguishes its confined grand-jury disclosure and dismissal rules; those limits remain effective within their own setting.

### A completed material Brady violation requires no second duplicative harmlessness showing

**Controlling proposition:** Once this suppression is found material under Brady and Bagley, a second prejudice or harmlessness test may not defeat relief merely by reassessing whether the same nondisclosure affected the verdict. This holding rests on the prejudice built into the completed Brady violation and establishes no universal harmlessness rule for other habeas claims.

**Authority:** Souter’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer: six direct votes for this claim-specific ground. The dissent finds no material violation and adopts no independent harmlessness ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a second duplicative prejudice inquiry cannot rescue a conviction after material suppression has already undermined confidence in its verdict. Bagley makes the requisite effect part of the constitutional violation itself. The Court has found that effect through the combined disclosure counterfactual, not merely identified an error whose effect remains unexamined. Brecht retains Chapman for its specified preserved protected-silence claim, and O’Neal extends that treatment to its specified personal-intent category; neither decision supplies a general habeas rule displacing Brady’s own materiality requirement. Nor does this decision replace Bagley with Chapman. The State’s surviving evidence has already been considered, and it does not restore confidence after the lawful combined uses are taken into account. Ordinary habeas gates and independently presented claims retain their own rules. For this established violation, the appropriate relief is a conditional new-trial opportunity, not an additional weaker review of the same prejudice or a declaration of innocence.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Bagley: treats materiality as a defining element of the suppression violation, not an initial screen before a second duplicative prejudice test.
- Brecht v. Abrahamson, April 21, 1993, and O’Neal v. McAninch, February 21, 1995: preserve their actual category-specific holdings and distinguish them from claim-defining Brady materiality.

#### Precedent Treatment
