Karo preserves recognized exceptions, including genuinely exigent circumstances; it does not authorize warrantless home surveillance merely because the receiving instrument remains outside. Nothing in this record establishes consent, exigency, or another exception for the July 25 scan. The Government's exterior-measurement theory addresses whether a search occurred and supplies no separate justification after that question is resolved. Nor does the limited detail of the output establish an exception for searches considered less revealing. The July 30 warrant authorized a later physical entry; its issuance cannot supply advance judicial authority for an earlier completed search. This conclusion concerns the scan's justification. It neither determines the validity of that later warrant nor requires exclusion of the evidence found during its execution. Those distinct questions remain subject to the record and procedural limits governing the remand.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Katz v. United States, 389 U.S. 347: applied; protected surveillance is subject to the warrant requirement and its established exceptions.
- United States v. Karo, 468 U.S. 705, Part III: applied; the warrant presumption for concealed home information retains recognized exceptions, including true exigency, none established here.

### III. Correct the preserved search ruling without reopening unpresented warrant questions

**Holding and operative rule:** The Eighth Circuit's judgment is vacated insofar as its rejection of the preserved suppression claim and affirmance of the conviction rest on the categorical no-search ground, and the case is remanded for lawful disposition of that claim under the corrected search rule. Pinson's failure to challenge affidavit sufficiency on appeal remains operative: the mandate directs no new excision-and-probable-cause hearing, revives no waived or forfeited argument automatically, and permits further warrant, suppression, or reliance questions only to the extent properly available under the existing record and ordinary preservation and remedial law.

**Authority:** Part III of Scalia's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg: seven Justices. The mandate controls directly. The remedial authorities below delimit unresolved possibilities; none supplies an independent finding admitting or excluding the evidence. Kennedy and Breyer prefer affirmance on their no-search ground; their acceptance of these procedural limits if the search holding governs does not add votes for vacatur.

**Controlling explanation:** The circuit rejected the preserved suppression claim because it considered the scan no search. That premise cannot sustain the challenged conviction affirmance. But its footnote expressly records that Pinson did not challenge affidavit sufficiency on appeal, and the circuit did not decide that question. Correcting the search rule does not erase this limit. Karo's separate treatment of an untainted warrant establishes a possible legal distinction, not a finding about Pinson's affidavit. Olano distinguishes intentional waiver from forfeiture and imposes additional conditions before forfeited error may be corrected. A preserved search question does not establish those conditions for another argument. Leon requires supported objective reliance, subject to its exclusions, rather than automatic validation by a later magistrate. Evans concerns qualifying court-employee clerical error and reasonable reliance; deliberate police scanning is not that error. The lower court must determine what remaining issues are lawfully available. This Court orders neither suppression nor a particular ultimate conviction consequence and does not reopen the separate sentencing question.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Karo, 468 U.S. 705, Part IV: retained as the distinct possibility of sufficient untainted warrant information; no determination of Pinson's affidavit or entitlement to a new hearing follows.
- United States v. Olano, decided April 26, 1993: retained; intentional waiver differs from forfeiture. If Rule 52(b) is actually invoked, error, plainness, ordinarily outcome-affecting prejudice, and the separate discretionary inquiry concerning fairness, integrity, or public reputation of judicial proceedings must be satisfied as applicable. This mandate does not establish any predicate or revive an argument automatically.
- United States v. Leon, 468 U.S. 897: left available only if procedurally open and factually supported. Objective reliance affords no protection for knowing or reckless deception of the magistrate, abandonment of judicial neutrality, an affidavit so lacking probable-cause indicia that reliance is wholly unreasonable, or facial warrant deficiency precluding reasonable reliance. The inquiry includes information-obtaining and supplying officers and assumes execution within the warrant's scope; no final reliance finding is made.
- Arizona v. Evans, decided March 1, 1995: confined to qualifying non-law-enforcement court-employee clerical error plus objectively reasonable reliance. Reserved police or mixed institutional errors supply no technology exception for this deliberate scan, and no final exclusion ruling follows here.

#### Precedent Treatment

Katz remains the basis for privacy protection without physical penetration.

Karo Part III governs otherwise unavailable home information and retains recognized warrant exceptions; Part IV remains a separate untainted-warrant inquiry whose factual and procedural predicates are not decided here.

Hicks supplies the additional-examination analogy and rejects an exemption for supposedly unimportant information.

Riley retains its scope for naked-eye observation through actual openings and supplies no missing flight fact.

Dow Chemical retains its outdoor industrial setting and does not authorize this closed-home examination.

Place retains its contraband-information limitation; this sensor's information also concerns lawful activity.

Greenwood retains its purposeful-disposal rationale; escaping heat does not establish comparable abandonment of domestic information.

Dickerson retains the boundary between authorized detection and additional unauthorized probing.

Olano retains distinct waiver, forfeiture, plain-error, prejudice, and discretionary-correction requirements, as stated in Part III.

Leon retains objective reliance and all four exclusions stated in Part III, including the inquiry into information-supplying officers and proper execution.

Evans retains its qualifying court-employee clerical-error and reasonable-reliance rule, with police and mixed institutional questions reserved.

No precedent is overruled. The circuit's categorical no-search rationale cannot sustain the affected disposition.

#### Law After Decision

Lower courts must treat this targeted closed-home thermal examination as a search on its demonstrated informational content. Seven Justices support that bounded rule; six additionally hold that no warrant exception is established for this scan. The rule does not depend on an invented capacity to see through walls or a finding about public availability, altitude, or flight frequency. It creates no general rule for every sensor, every emission, or every technological measurement. The absence of a warrant exception does not itself decide exclusion or the validity of a later warrant. The seven-Justice mandate preserves the appellate nonchallenge to affidavit sufficiency and leaves only lawfully available proceedings open. The Chief's broader formulation and the dissenters' limited no-search theory are separate positions, not additional controlling holdings.

### Hubbard v. United States, No. 94-172; Sixth Circuit No. 91-1775. — 1995-05-15

Authority: [Record](../records/Hubbard_v_United_States_merits_1995-05-15.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### I. The court-entity question is properly before the Court

**Holding and operative rule:** The Court reaches whether the federal court receiving the filings supporting Counts V–VII is a department or agency within offense-date §§6 and 1001. The governing-law inquiry concerns the same challenged convictions and the question presented; it does not authorize review of a separate judgment, enlarge review merely because merits briefs discuss an issue, or require decision of every unargued antecedent question.

**Authority:** Part I of Thomas's opinion. Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer join the complete analysis: eight Justices including the author. Stone-Zsela joins the conclusion that no jurisdictional, preservation, or vehicle bar prevents reaching the question presented; he does not join the additional analysis. The complete procedural explanation therefore has eight votes and the reach conclusion has nine.

**Controlling explanation:** The question before the Court asks whether a federal court is an included entity under §1001. Answering it determines the governing law for the same three convictions challenged here. The Government's account of the petition's narrower focus does not transform this inquiry into review of another judgment. Independent Insurance permits consideration of necessary antecedent governing law with a fair opportunity for adversarial presentation; it does not impose a duty to decide every omitted issue. Yee preserves the difference between a supporting argument and an independent question. Izumi refused to reach a distinct intervention denial, while Caspari permitted its confined subsidiary defense and Madsen required its own supported basis for exceptional review. None makes later briefing sufficient by itself. The entity inquiry is also the premise of the proposed judicial-function exception. The circuit's separate Rule 12 ruling resolved the failure-to-charge objection below; it did not decide this Court's question-presentation issue. The Court reaches coverage without reopening other convictions or unrelated claims.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States National Bank of Oregon v. Independent Insurance Agents of America, Inc., decided June 7, 1993: applied; necessary antecedent governing law may be considered in a live dispute with adversarial fairness, without a duty to raise every omitted issue.
- Yee v. City of Escondido, decided April 1, 1992: applied; new supporting arguments and independently presented questions remain distinct.
- Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp., decided November 30, 1993: distinguished; Hubbard seeks review of the same three convictions, not an additional intervention judgment. Its prudential limit remains intact.
- Caspari v. Bohlen, decided February 23, 1994: preserved at its confined subsidiary-defense scope; it supplies no general briefing exception.
- Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc., decided June 30, 1994: preserved; its exceptional review required its own circumstances, and its nonreach limits are not displaced.
- United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc., decided November 29, 1994: its prevailing-respondent alternative-ground rule is not transferred to this petitioner seeking reversal.

### II. A federal court is not an included department or agency under this statute

**Holding and operative rule:** Under the offense-date §§6 and 1001, a federal court is neither an enumerated executive department nor an agency within the statute's contextual meaning; the department definition's branch-wide contextual alternative does not extend this statute to the court, and the distinct agency definition does not do so either. Counts V–VII therefore lack the asserted court nexus, without deciding whether another Judicial Branch entity could qualify as an agency or whether an independently established nexus to a different included institution would support a different prosecution.

**Authority:** Part II of Thomas's opinion, joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer: six direct votes. Stone-Zsela reaches the same count disposition under his narrower Article III/adjudicative rule but does not join this broader court-entity construction. O’Connor and Souter would retain Bramblett's contrary construction. No combination of these different grounds is needed for the six-Justice holding.

**Controlling explanation:** Congress supplied separate definitions of department and agency, and both must be read with their own qualifications. Rowland gives an enacted default genuine force while permitting the context to do its stated work. Here the broad references to any matter and jurisdiction describe the functions of an included body; they do not independently identify which bodies are included. Rodgers construed authorized agency functions, not away the separate entity requirement. A general antifraud purpose and the inconclusive predecessor history do not establish the branch-wide department meaning here. Nor does the word authority in the agency list turn every body exercising governmental power into an agency in this setting. Section 151 identifies the bankruptcy court as a unit of the district court, without independently answering either Title 18 definition. The knowing factual falsehoods found below cannot supply the missing entity nexus. This construction resolves these court filings without creating a judicial-function exception, classifying the trustee, or deciding the status of every Judicial Branch institution.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Rowland v. California Men's Colony, decided January 12, 1993: applied for an enacted definition operating subject to a genuine contextual qualification; its association-eligibility holding supplies no categorical result under a different statute.
- United States v. Rodgers, 466 U.S. 475: preserved; jurisdiction includes the authorized functions of an included agency. Footnote 4 reserved the judicial-function question and did not decide these filings or eliminate the entity requirement.
- United States v. Bramblett, 348 U.S. 503: its incompatible all-branch construction is expressly overruled in Part III, not avoided as merely an unnecessary judicial passage.
- Brown v. Gardner, decided December 12, 1994: consistent with enforcing enacted conditions without adding a category the statute does not establish.
- Heintz v. Jenkins, decided April 18, 1995: distinguished in statutory setting; rejection of an added FDCPA litigation exemption does not resolve §1001's entity definition or supply an exception here.

### III. Bramblett's incompatible all-branch construction is overruled

**Holding and operative rule:** Bramblett's construction of department in §1001 to include the executive, legislative, and judicial branches is overruled, including the rationale supporting its legislative-branch result. The decision does not adjudicate the independent House Disbursing Office agency or Treasury-nexus theories Bramblett left open, classify every other judicial entity, or alter §287's separate false-claims coverage.

**Authority:** Part III of Thomas's opinion, joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer: six votes for this express precedent treatment. Stone-Zsela supports overruling only to the extent stated in his narrower concurrence and does not join the broader disposition of Bramblett. The six associates do not share a controlling extended stare decisis framework: Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer adopt one account; Scalia and Kennedy another; Thomas joins neither complete account. No Marks synthesis supplies a further rule.

**Controlling explanation:** Bramblett's treatment of department cannot remain the governing interpretation alongside the construction adopted today. It considered §6 and the predecessor statutes; the defect is its expansion of the definition, not failure to notice those materials. Its actual dispute involved representations to the House Disbursing Office, but its reason for coverage treated all three branches alike. Calling the reference to courts unnecessary would leave that incompatible interpretive reason in force. The Court therefore rejects the all-branch construction expressly, including the rationale supporting the legislative result. Bramblett declined to decide whether the office separately qualified as an authority or whether Treasury jurisdiction supplied another nexus. Those questions do not become resolved by today's rejection of its chosen ground. Rodgers's meaning of jurisdiction for an included agency remains intact. Six Justices agree on this construction and express precedent treatment, although their additional explanations for departing from statutory precedent differ. The decision establishes this overruling, not a general standard assembled from those separate explanations.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Bramblett, 348 U.S. 503: expressly overruled as to its incompatible all-branch department construction, including the rationale supporting its legislative result; no new ruling resolves the independent agency or Treasury theories it declined to decide.
- United States v. Rodgers, 466 U.S. 475: retained at its authorized-function jurisdiction holding and express reservation of the judicial-function issue; its citation of Bramblett does not preserve the displaced entity construction.
- Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission, decided December 16, 1991: its retained FELA construction and specialized reliance setting remain intact; the separate writings explain its significance without creating a new majority stare decisis framework here.
- Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, decided January 18, 1995: its retention of Southland and its actual contractual setting remain intact; disagreement about this precedent does not displace that holding.

### IV. Reverse only the affirmance of Counts V–VII and remand to vacate them

**Holding and operative rule:** The Sixth Circuit's affirmance of Counts V, VI, and VII is reversed, and the case is remanded for lawful proceedings to vacate those §1001 convictions on the asserted court-nexus ground. Counts I–IV and VIII–X remain undisturbed by this review; no exact prison, probation, supervised-release, fine, immediate-release, general resentencing, retrial, or new-prosecution command follows, and any ordinary implementation requires lawful proceedings and the actual criminal judgment.

**Authority:** Part IV of Thomas's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven votes for the exact count-specific mandate. Stone-Zsela's narrower coverage and precedent position supplies his vote; he does not join Parts II–III. O’Connor and Souter would affirm these three convictions. The other seven convictions receive no new merits adjudication.

**Controlling explanation:** Count V concerns the account of records supplied to a former trustee, while Counts VI and VII concern denials about the locations of machinery and parts. These findings of factual falsehood are not set aside as unproved or converted into permissible legal argument. The defect is the statutory court nexus supporting these three convictions. Their affirmance must therefore be reversed and the convictions vacated through the implementing remand. This disposition does not reach the separate bankruptcy-fraud and mail-fraud counts or the rejected ineffective-assistance claim. Nor does it require a decision on the Government's administrative-asset-collection characterization, which the circuit left open. The Court does not adopt a function exception as the common basis of its mandate. The descriptions of Count X's sentence differ, and the criminal judgment is necessary before any exact sentencing consequence can be determined. The remand accordingly commands removal of V–VII, not release, a new trial, a specified sentence, or a new prosecution.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Bramblett, 348 U.S. 503: no longer supports these court-based §1001 convictions; the seven mandate votes differ in the broader extent of their precedent treatment as stated in Part III and Stone-Zsela's concurrence.
- United States v. Rodgers, 466 U.S. 475: its jurisdiction holding does not supply a new entity nexus or an independent basis to affirm these convictions.

#### Precedent Treatment

Bramblett's incompatible all-branch department construction is expressly overruled, including the rationale supporting its legislative result. Its undecided agency and Treasury theories are not adjudicated by implication.

Rodgers retains the authorized-function meaning of jurisdiction for an included department or agency and its reservation of the judicial-function issue.

Rowland retains its default-and-context construction and association-eligibility holding; the analogy here does not alter its statutory result.

Independent Insurance retains authority to address necessary governing law with adversarial fairness, without a universal obligation to decide every omitted issue.

Yee retains the distinction between new arguments and independent questions.

Izumi retains its separate-judgment and prudential question-presentation limits.

Caspari retains its confined subsidiary-defense holding.

Madsen retains its supported exceptional review and separate nonreach limits.

X-Citement Video retains its contextual criminal-knowledge holding and distinct prevailing-respondent rule; neither creates a litigation privilege here.

Gardner retains its enforcement of actual statutory conditions without a judicially added prerequisite.

Heintz retains its rejection of a categorical FDCPA attorney-litigation exemption; it supplies no §1001 entity ruling.

Hilton retains its specific FELA construction and reliance setting; its application in separate writings creates no new majority stare decisis test here.

Allied-Bruce retains Southland's state-court rule and the stated contractual qualifications; the separate writings do not displace it.

Crandon retains its statute-specific criminal construction. Stevens's opinion and Scalia's separate reasoning inform different concurrences without becoming a new general rule.

Patterson's special-justification discussion is invoked in separate writings; this decision supplies no majority revision of a general stare decisis standard.

Square D's statutory-precedent force is invoked in Stone-Zsela's concurrence and is not displaced.

Illinois Brick's statutory-precedent force is invoked in Stone-Zsela's concurrence and is not displaced.

Ratzlaf retains residual lenity after ordinary interpretation; the predicate for an independent lenity holding is not selected here.

Granderson retains its confined contextual and residual-lenity rules; disagreement among the Justices does not alone establish ambiguity.

The lower judicial-function decisions are not adopted as an independently controlling exception. Section 287 and the separate statutes governing perjury, contempt, bankruptcy fraud, obstruction, and covered sworn or court statements remain operative on their own terms.

#### Law After Decision

Under the offense-date law, the court is neither a department nor an agency within §§6 and 1001. The department contextual alternative and agency qualification remain real and distinct; no blanket conclusion about every Judicial Branch body or trustee follows. Bramblett's inconsistent all-branch construction no longer governs, including the rationale supporting its legislative result. Six Justices directly support those holdings. No additional entity nexus or outcome under an independent agency theory is established. Rodgers's jurisdiction rule operates only once an included institution is identified. The decision creates neither a right to lie nor an independent judicial-function or private-litigation exception. Other enacted offenses retain their scope. Seven Justices require vacatur of V–VII alone. No extended stare decisis account, generic Marks synthesis, or independently sufficient lenity alternative changes current doctrine.

### Kansas v. Colorado, No. 105, Original. — 1995-05-15

Authority: [Record](../records/Kansas_v_Colorado_original_exceptions_1995-05-15.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### 1. Pumping liability is established without choosing a general proof quantum

**Holding and operative rule:** Colorado’s post-Compact well development, including increased use of existing wells, materially depleted water protected by Article IV-D in usable quantity or availability. The substantial pumping violation is established even by clear and convincing evidence, while the separate WWSP claim fails even by a preponderance; the Court therefore overrules Colorado’s proof-standard exception without choosing the general quantum governing disputed Compact-enforcement facts or fixing a compensable quantity.

**Authority:** Part 1 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the competing standards do not change this liability disposition. Nebraska distinguishes enforcement of an established right from creation of a new allocation, but does not settle every factual burden under this Compact. Colorado v. New Mexico requires high probability in its new-diversion setting, not an unerring forecast. Here the pumping evidence is not a small residual produced by one disputed calculation. The States’ differing models show substantial gross depletion, and the record separately addresses actual reservoir operations, imported return flows and the usable portion of the loss. Those considerations support a material violation even under the more demanding proposed standard. Colorado’s concerns about sovereign error and uncertain estimates therefore do not defeat the finding. They remain reasons not to convert illustrative gross totals into an award. The WWSP claim presents different operational and measurement defects and fails the lesser standard. The Court adopts neither a universal preponderance rule nor a new presumption against Colorado; exact quantity and relief remain for the remedy phase.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: preserves the distinction between enforcement of an established decree right and modification requiring clear and convincing real and substantial injury; it does not replace this Compact’s material-depletion element or settle its general factual quantum.
- Colorado v. New Mexico, 467 U.S. 310 (1984): distinguishes the proposed new-diversion setting while applying its explanation that clear and convincing proof requires high probability rather than certainty; the general Compact standard remains open.

### 2. The protected pumping baseline is actual use, not hypothetical capacity

**Holding and operative rule:** Article IV-D’s protection against material depletion extends to the improved or prolonged functioning of existing works, and Article VI-A(2) preserves Colorado’s administration only subject to the Compact’s other provisions. The supported 15,000-acre-foot historical actual-use benchmark governs this liability accounting rather than later decreed quantities or hypothetical maximum capacity; it is not an unconditional annual pumping cap or a final damages quantity.

**Authority:** Part 2 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a well’s existence before the Compact does not immunize every later expansion of its use. Article IV-D expressly reaches improved or prolonged operation, and Article VI’s qualification prevents state decrees from enlarging the interstate allowance. Texas v. New Mexico requires enforcement of the agreed allocation rather than judicial replacement of it. Both States accept protection for established use, but that agreement does not establish Colorado’s proposed amount. The Master selected the highest accepted negotiation-period estimate, 15,000 acre-feet, rather than the single wet-year 11,000 figure. The shared Hurr foundation is not five independent measurements, and unavailable original calculations remain a limitation. Colorado’s competing power, acreage and decree reconstructions also depend on disputed coverage, nonelectric use and equipment assumptions. The comparative evidence supports the selected benchmark without making official publication conclusive. Pumping above that benchmark must still cause the material loss of usable Compact water required by IV-D. The Court fixes neither a general groundwater limit nor final compensable depletion.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 (1983): applies the duty to enforce the congressionally approved Compact as written; neither state decrees nor preferred allocation policy replaces its express conditions.

### 3. The laches exception fails for lack of established inexcusable delay

**Holding and operative rule:** Assuming laches could apply, Colorado has not established Kansas’s inexcusable lack of diligence and therefore cannot obtain the requested bar to monetary liability for periods before March 1985. The Court leaves laches’s categorical availability in Compact enforcement open, makes no independently sufficient prejudice holding, and reserves the eventual form of relief and properly supported remedy-stage equities.

**Authority:** Part 3 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Colorado has not proved the unreasonable lack of diligence necessary to its asserted defense. Illinois v. Kentucky’s general discussion of state claims does not decide whether laches can ever apply to Compact enforcement. Nebraska likewise shows why specific interstate conduct matters, without making every delay an acquiescence. Kansas knew that wells increased and that gross flow declined, but those facts did not establish knowledge of material depletion in usable Compact water. Drought, reservoir operation, imports and developing regulation complicated that inference. The report’s account of investigation and conflicting information does not show that Kansas possessed a provable violation and inexcusably declined to pursue it. Colorado’s concerns about old records, lost testimony and development reliance cannot substitute for the missing diligence element. The Court need not decide an independent prejudice ground. Texas v. New Mexico rejects good-faith uncertainty as a complete answer to past nonperformance while preserving remedial flexibility. No final damages cutoff or rule excluding every equitable timing consideration follows.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Illinois v. Kentucky, 500 U.S. 380 (1991): distinguishes its concrete prescription and acquiescence setting; its general laches observation does not establish an absolute Compact-enforcement exemption.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: preserves its concrete acquiescence determination without treating knowledge of wells or passage of time as equivalent conduct here.
- Texas v. New Mexico, 482 U.S. 124 (1987): retains enforcement of past Compact obligations despite good-faith uncertainty, while leaving appropriate remedial considerations for the remedy proceeding.

### 4. The separate reservoir bargain supplies no independent pumping credit

**Holding and operative rule:** The 1980 John Martin Operating Plan does not create the asserted independent legal credit against well-pumping liability for benefits Kansas obtained through a separately bargained exchange of release and timing rights. Actual Plan operation and actual imported return flows that reduce the measured loss must remain in the physical accounting; the Court rejects no credit actually created by agreement and awards no final numerical offset.

**Authority:** Part 4 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that performance of the reservoir bargain is not automatically payment for a different Compact violation. Texas v. New Mexico requires attention to the actual undertaking. Colorado surrendered winter river-flow demands, Kansas surrendered its summer entitlement, and both obtained more useful timing through separate accounts. The Plan preserves the parties’ ability to pursue their interests in Administration and court controversies. That bargain does not purchase the independent pumping credit Colorado seeks. The absence of a settlement confirms the agreement’s scope; Colorado’s exception seeks a partial credit, not a complete bar. Its strongest point concerns actual injury: additional water can reduce an actual loss. The Court preserves that point by requiring the hydrologic comparison to reflect the river as operated, including the Plan and actual imported return flows. Imported water remains outside the apportioned source, and no right to future imports follows. Separately purchased benefits cannot become an unagreed legal credit merely because they are valuable. Final causal accounting and supported remedy issues remain open.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Texas v. New Mexico, 482 U.S. 124 (1987): applies enforcement of the actual Compact obligation with remedial flexibility; that flexibility does not create an unbargained credit or establish a present numerical offset.

### 5. The pleaded Trinidad claim requires the Compact’s material depletion

**Holding and operative rule:** The 1967 Trinidad Operating Principles do not establish the asserted independent Compact command making every departure a violation without the material depletion required by Article IV-D. Kansas has not established that depletion by comparing actual operation with conditions without the project, so its pleaded Compact claim is dismissed; independent agreement, third-party-beneficiary and other authorized enforcement theories remain undecided.

**Authority:** Part 5 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the operative source and scope of the asserted obligation control. Texas v. New Mexico respects authorized Compact machinery but does not permit enlargement of the interstate bargain. Article VIII-B permits consistent implementing rules; unanimity and mandatory wording deserve attention, but do not establish that these cooperative project arrangements created the independent Compact prohibition asserted here. Nor does their title alone decide enforceability. Under IV-D the comparison is the river with and without the project, not actual operation against an ideal project complying with every Principle. The offered studies and shortages do not establish material usable-water depletion on that comparison. An assumed inconsistency for purposes of disposition is not an adjudicated breach of every operating term. The contrary argument correctly observes that formal commitments may have legal force. The Court leaves a separate agreement or beneficiary claim, genuine authorized implementation and the broader question of compelling Administration duties to their own sources and proper presentation. It creates neither project immunity nor a rule that all operating commitments are unenforceable.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 (1983): applies respect for the Compact’s actual institutional authority while forbidding judicial alteration of its allocation; it does not foreclose every valid implementation rule or separate undertaking.

### 6. The WWSP depletion claim fails on the particular proof

**Holding and operative rule:** Kansas has not proved even by a preponderance that the reviewed Winter Water Storage Program materially depleted protected usable water, and its depletion exception is overruled. The conclusion rests on the comparison’s operational, nonparallel-error and timing defects, not a finding of no possible depletion, a demand for perfect modeling, or a rule that calibration error larger than the modeled effect always defeats proof.

**Authority:** Part 6 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the particular comparison does not establish the prohibited effect. Daubert distinguishes a method’s fit for its task from automatic acceptance of every inference drawn from it; admissibility does not establish persuasive sufficiency. Common errors can cancel in paired runs, so absolute calibration error alone is no answer to Kansas. These runs nevertheless omit actual Plan operation, contain nonparallel winter-evaporation and storage errors, and create artificial storage buildup and late spills. The concentration of modeled accretions in May and June 1985 leaves their usable timing inadequately established; neither all gains nor all losses may simply be assumed usable. Success in estimating the much larger pumping effect supplies no missing precision for this smaller program. Defects in the opposing evaporation calculation do not prove Kansas’s calculation, and conditional output is no concession accepting its assumptions. Kansas had an extensive opportunity to try liability. The Court orders no further liability trial merely because more science is possible and creates no automatic burden shift, metering duty or spoliation inference.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc., June 28, 1993: preserves reliability and task-specific fit while keeping preliminary admissibility distinct from the persuasive sufficiency of a particular estimate; no mandatory checklist or perfect-model requirement is imposed.

### 7. The adopted usable-water method fits this liability record

**Holding and operative rule:** Article IV-D protects usable quantity and availability, including supported timing and recharge, rather than gross annual flow alone. For this liability record the Court accepts Durbin’s approach with Larson’s modifications—72% summer, 25% winter, a 9.9% pre-Compact recharge component, and monthly/seasonal ceilings of 40,000/140,000 acre-feet in summer and 7,500/40,000 in winter—and rejects Kansas’s exception without prescribing a permanent formula or foreclosing reliable daily analysis.

**Authority:** Part 7 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the calculation must fit both the protected use and the evidence’s temporal resolution. Daubert’s fit principle does not require choosing whichever computation produces the most detailed output. Spronk’s daily Garden City reconstruction combines daily demands with uncertain monthly depletion estimates, and its recharge attribution can charge Colorado for effects of later Kansas pumping. Kansas rightly argues that seasonal averages may understate loss during periods of greatest need. That concern requires examination of the coefficients and caps, not unsupported daily precision. The accepted approach uses the historical pre-Compact period, excluding the extraordinary 1942 flood, with Larson’s modified percentages, recharge component and ceilings stated above. It is not the unmodified Durbin proposal. The Court does not reject the Garden City gage as inherently inadequate; the discussed 20-cubic-feet-per-second power-plant threshold concerns 1970–1973, while the ordinary threshold discussed is five. Dismissal of Colorado’s Kansas-well counterclaim does not erase Kansas pumping’s separate effect on usability. No report estimate becomes a final award.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc., June 28, 1993: applies the importance of fit to the inference sought, without turning its admission rule into a permanent water-accounting formula or choosing the ultimate Compact proof quantum.

### 8. The separate WWSP prior-approval ruling is confirmed on its narrow ground

**Holding and operative rule:** The 1951 Administration policy resolution, the Compact and the cited 1944 and 1962 federal authorizations do not establish the asserted general requirement of prior Administration approval for WWSP. The partial summary judgment is confirmed on that legal ground, while Article IV-D’s material-depletion restriction, Article IV-C’s actual operational qualifications and Article IX’s federal reservation and jointly approved John Martin exceptions remain effective.

**Authority:** Part 8 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the asserted veto requires an enacted or validly exercised source of authority. The 1951 resolution recommends a policy to the governors; it does not itself impose the general approval condition Kansas proposed. Article VIII-B authorizes rules consistent with the Compact, not an unexpressed enlargement of its bargain. The 1944 statute’s provision for state views does not make those views a consent requirement, and the 1962 authorization supplies no such condition. Texas v. New Mexico requires respect for the actual Compact machinery without making administrative division an unlimited obstacle to interstate adjudication. The contrary argument that coordinated storage benefits from prior review does not supply the missing legal command. This conclusion leaves the substantive depletion duty intact and gives federal projects no blanket immunity. The Court adopts no unnecessary laches, estoppel or supersession alternative. Nor does this Administration impasse excuse every future procedural obligation. Confirmation of this unexcepted ruling reaches its necessary ground, not every statement in the earlier report.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 (1983): preserves actual Compact machinery and judicial enforcement despite administrative impasse, without creating either an unexpressed approval veto or a universal exception to administrative procedures.

### 9. Lake McKinney is dismissed for failure to prove unauthorized retention

**Holding and operative rule:** Colorado’s Lake McKinney counterclaim is dismissed because it has not adequately traced protected John Martin releases to unauthorized retention rather than the short retention needed to move water through the pool. Article V-E(2) still requires prompt beneficial use unless the Administration authorizes downstream storage; this record-specific failure of proof supplies no general permission for storage and no universal counterclaim proof rule.

**Authority:** Part 9 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the existence of a downstream pool does not establish the asserted Compact breach. Texas v. New Mexico requires enforcement of the obligation actually agreed, which here includes both prompt beneficial use and the express allowance for Administration-authorized storage. The approximately 3,600-acre-foot pool and its observed level do not by themselves identify the source of the water, the duration of retention or the presence of protected John Martin releases. Short retention necessary to move water through the facility does not establish the unauthorized withholding Colorado alleged. The 1967 episode likewise lacks the required proof. Colorado’s concern that storage could defeat timely use is legally relevant, but cannot replace tracing and duration evidence in this counterclaim. The evidence fails even under the less demanding proposed quantum, so no general counterclaim standard is chosen. The Plan does not erase V-E(2), and dismissal does not legalize every downstream storage practice or determine a materially different future operation.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 (1983): applies enforcement of the enacted obligation with its express qualification; the Court neither deletes authorized downstream storage nor adds a categorical storage permission.

### 10. The Kansas-well counterclaim fails for want of proved Colorado injury

**Holding and operative rule:** Colorado’s Kansas-well counterclaim is dismissed because the asserted additional calls, transit or account effects, and lost surplus have not been shown to injure Colorado’s protected supply. The dismissal does not immunize Kansas development, equate all Kansas groundwater with Compact-covered Colorado-origin water, or remove Kansas pumping from the distinct calculation of water Kansas could actually have used.

**Authority:** Part 10 of Justice O’Connor’s opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for this complete, bounded proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that development in Kansas alone does not prove the injury alleged in this counterclaim. Article III identifies the apportioned source, and Article VI preserves Kansas jurisdiction over its originating waters and waters crossing the Stateline subject to the Compact. Texas v. New Mexico requires the actual interstate obligation to supply the rule. After the 1980 Plan, Kansas draws on its own conservation account; the record does not establish the claimed additional burden on Colorado through that account. For the earlier period, the asserted extra calls lack the necessary attribution, and the alleged loss of surplus remains unquantified. Colorado’s argument identifies possible mechanisms of harm, but the evidence does not establish their asserted effect on its protected supply. No broader immunity follows from that failure. Kansas pumping may still affect recharge and the amount of water Kansas could have used, a different question addressed in the usability analysis. The Court confirms this unexcepted dismissal without a new presumption or final remedial calculation.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 (1983): applies the actual Compact obligation and protected entitlement as the basis for adjudication; failure to prove this claimed injury creates no general immunity for downstream development.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 (1983): applied to enforce the actual congressionally approved bargain and its authorized machinery; judicial enforcement neither rewrites the allocation nor grants an administrative impasse an unlimited veto.
- Texas v. New Mexico, 482 U.S. 124 (1987): applied to past Compact performance and the distinction between an established obligation and the form of relief; good-faith uncertainty alone is no complete defense, and remedial flexibility creates no unagreed credit.
- Colorado v. New Mexico, 467 U.S. 310 (1984): distinguished as a proposed new diversion; its high-probability account of clear and convincing evidence informs the alternative sufficiency conclusion without deciding the general enforcement quantum.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: preserves enforcement of established decree rights without a new-injury requirement, the separate modification burden, and its concrete acquiescence determination. None replaces Article IV-D or decides every disputed factual burden here.
- Illinois v. Kentucky, 500 U.S. 380 (1991): distinguished in its prescription and acquiescence setting; no categorical sovereign-laches rule is extracted from its general language.
- Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc., June 28, 1993: preserves reliability and fit for a particular inference while keeping admission separate from ultimate persuasion; it supplies no universal hydrological method or certainty requirement.

No precedent is overruled. The Compact’s substantive condition, proof of a breach and eventual relief remain separate. No cross-area burden rule displaces the unresolved Compact quantum.

#### Law After Decision

Article IV-D governs both later development and materially depleting increases in the operation of existing works. The 15,000-acre-foot historical allowance and modified usable-water calculation are accepted for this liability record, not imposed as permanent extraction or accounting codes. Colorado’s pumping violation stands under either proposed proof quantum. Laches fails on diligence; the general doctrine’s availability and remedy-stage equities remain open. Separately purchased Plan benefits supply no independent legal credit, while actual operations and imported return flows remain part of causal accounting. The Trinidad and WWSP claims fail on their respective bounded Compact and proof grounds. The narrow prior-approval ruling and two distinct counterclaim dismissals create neither a general approval veto nor immunity from future Compact duties. The Court has not chosen a universal enforcement burden, shifted persuasion because of missing data, or fixed compensation. The original action continues before the Master for the separated remedy phase.

### City of Edmonds v. Oxford House, Inc.; No. 94-23 - 1995-05-15

Authority: [Record](../records/City_of_Edmonds_v_Oxford_House_merits_1995-05-15.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### A household-composition definition is not this occupancy exemption

**Holding and operative rule:** The FHA exemption for reasonable restrictions regarding the maximum number of occupants permitted to occupy a dwelling does not cover Edmonds' family definition, which permits an unlimited number of related residents while limiting unrelated residents to five. The definition regulates household composition, so the absolute exemption does not foreclose the ordinary FHA discrimination and accommodation inquiry.

**Authority:** Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Breyer: six direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the exemption addresses crowding rather than the membership of a household. Section 3607(b)(1) preserves reasonable restrictions on the maximum number of people occupying a dwelling, while section 3604(f) supplies protection against disability discrimination and requires reasonable accommodation within its terms. Edmonds permits any number of people related by genetics, adoption or marriage, yet limits unrelated people to five. That operation identifies who may live together, not how many occupants the dwelling can safely hold. The City's strongest response is that five is a real maximum for the unrelated household. But a numerical feature does not change the subject of this rule: unlimited related occupancy remains permitted. Reading the exception in its enacted setting keeps genuine crowding controls distinct from household definitions. The Court decides only the exemption. It does not invalidate single-family zoning, find discrimination, order a particular accommodation or determine ultimate relief.

**Precedent treatment:**
- No earlier Supreme Court decision is materially extended, limited or overruled. The enacted FHA coverage and occupancy exception supply the controlling distinction.

#### Precedent Treatment

The statutory exemption is applied within its terms; no Supreme Court precedent is overruled. Chevron is not reached because no qualifying agency interpretation is established.

#### Law After Decision

Effective May 15, this unlimited-related-family composition rule is outside the absolute crowding exemption. Genuine reasonable occupancy limits remain protected at their actual scope; FHA liability and accommodation remain for remand.

### Day v. Holahan; No. 94-672 - 1995-05-15

Authority: [Record](../records/Day_v_Holahan_merits_1995-05-15.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Financing component: no Opinion of the Court

The responsive-financing judgment has five votes to affirm invalidation but no five-Justice financing framework. Kennedy's four-Justice plurality regards this adverse, proportional legal trigger as a burden; Stone-Zsela agrees to the judgment on the insufficient programmatic connection shown here and rejects categorical invalidity. Their shared judgment does not establish that either complete framework controls. The lower judgment invalidating this arrangement binds the parties. The reporting, contribution and nonprofit holdings below each have direct majorities.

### The special immediate-notice obligation falls with this trigger

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 10A.20, subdivision 6b's additional immediate affidavit and service obligation cannot be enforced independently of the invalid responsive-financing mechanism on this scheme's demonstrated function and legislative relationship. Under Minnesota section 645.20, the special mechanism is essentially and inseparably connected with the invalid provision such that the legislature cannot be presumed to have enacted it alone; ordinary campaign reports remain enforceable within their separate terms.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, Part II, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas: five direct votes. Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer dissent on this component.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the special notice mechanism falls with the responsive program it implements. Buckley recognizes genuine informational, anticorruption and enforcement interests for disclosure, together with protection against serious associational burdens. Minnesota already requires ordinary expenditure reports. Subdivision 6b adds a twenty-four-hour affidavit and service on the candidates and campaign treasurers in the affected race, allowing the Board to administer the amount-linked response. Section 645.20 generally preserves valid provisions, but excepts those essentially and inseparably connected with invalid provisions when independent enactment cannot be presumed. That exception applies to this specially linked mechanism. The State answers that prompt information may still be useful. General usefulness does not establish an independently intended statutory function for this additional obligation once its material implementing purpose disappears. This is a legislative-relationship conclusion, not a finding that notices are physically impossible. Ordinary reports remain intact. The Court does not reach the preserved independent vagueness and overbreadth theories.

**Precedent treatment:**
- Buckley, 424 U.S. 1: preserves distinct disclosure interests and associational protections; no categorical disclosure invalidation follows.
- Gibson, 372 U.S. 539: applies the need for a substantial relation between information demanded and the legitimate subject; does not eliminate ordinary reports.
- Brown v. Socialist Workers, 459 U.S. 87: recognizes genuine disclosure interests with protection for demonstrated associational harms; no new general exemption is created.

### This $100 intermediary-contribution ceiling lacks sufficient fit

**Holding and operative rule:** Minnesota's $100 annual ceiling on contributions received by the covered political committees and political funds is unconstitutional on the established burden and insufficiently fitted corruption/circumvention justification. Contributions remain distinct from independent expenditures: realistic routes around valid candidate limits may be regulated, but a broadly restrictive ceiling must preserve meaningful collective political association and fit an identified corruption or circumvention mechanism; no permanent dollar minimum follows.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, Part III, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: nine direct votes, at this stated functional application.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that this unusually low, broadly applied intermediary ceiling cannot stand on the showing made. Buckley distinguishes contributions from expenditures and permits contribution regulation fitted to corruption and its appearance while preserving meaningful association. California Medical sustains regulation of transfers to a multicandidate committee that makes candidate contributions; its fractured reasoning does not settle every independent-advocacy committee's case. The State therefore has a legitimate circumvention route to investigate, and the absence of legislative power in a committee is not dispositive. But Minnesota has not fitted this across-the-board ceiling to an identified route of improper dependence or evasion. Between one-quarter and one-third of MCCL-CSPC's contributions exceeded $100 in the relevant cycle, demonstrating a substantial practical pooling burden. A general reference to special-interest money does not justify it. Buckley's historical amount supplies no constitutional floor or permanent safe harbor. The Court leaves higher limits, coordination controls, candidate transfers and demonstrated conduit restrictions open.

**Precedent treatment:**
- Buckley: applies contribution/expenditure distinction, corruption fit and effective collective advocacy; its historical dollar figure is no minimum.
- California Medical, 453 U.S. 182: preserves candidate-transfer anticircumvention regulation; its plurality is not a categorical answer for every committee.
- Citizens Against Rent Control, 454 U.S. 290: applies protection of pooling for collective political expression; does not immunize a candidate-contribution conduit.
- NCPAC, 470 U.S. 480: preserves independent advocacy at its scope; contributions are not silently equated with expenditures.

### MCCL receives protection on its actual nonprofit characteristics

**Holding and operative rule:** Minnesota may not prohibit MCCL's independent expenditures solely because its incidental business activity and absence of a formal policy against significant corporate contributions fall outside section 211B.15, subdivision 15's literal safe harbor. On the undisputed minimal-business-income and insignificant-corporate-funding record, its relevant political-association characteristics support as-applied MCFL protection; material commercial accumulation, economic claimants or corporate/union conduit funding requires a different assessment.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, Part IV, joined by all eight Associates: nine direct votes on this as-applied holding. No general overruling or endorsement beyond Austin's surviving scope is adopted.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the State must examine MCCL's actual constitutionally relevant characteristics rather than mechanically deny protection because it misses a descriptive checklist. MCFL protects independent spending by a voluntary political organization whose resources reflect political support rather than commercial accumulation and whose structure and funding avoid economic claimants and corporate conduits. Austin preserves regulation of materially different corporate treasury wealth; a nonprofit label alone does not establish an exemption. MCCL's incidental business operations generated minimal income related to its political work, and the State did not dispute that corporate contributions were insignificant. Failure to adopt the statutory policy against significant contributions does not itself establish material corporate financing. The State's strongest argument is administrative certainty and prevention of conduits. Objective safe-harbor conditions can provide certainty, but cannot foreclose as-applied protection where the actual organization lacks the regulatory predicate. Changed activity, business revenue, corporate or union financing may justify reassessment. Direct corporate candidate contributions and ordinary business-corporation treasury restrictions are not decided.

**Precedent treatment:**
- MCFL, 479 U.S. 238: applies protection to actual political-resource, structure and funding characteristics; descriptive facts do not foreclose this narrow as-applied claim.
- Austin, 494 U.S. 652: distinguishes materially different commercially accumulated corporate wealth; its surviving corporate-treasury rule is not overruled.

#### Precedent Treatment

Buckley retains separate contribution, expenditure, disclosure and public-financing treatment. California Medical retains its candidate-transfer circumvention holding and fragmented independent-spending limits; no categorical immunity for intermediary contributions is recognized. MCFL supports the narrow actual-characteristics application; Austin is not overruled. NCPAC remains independent-expression authority rather than a universal ban on public financing. Gibson and Brown retain tailored disclosure/association principles. Effective McIntyre's anonymous-individual-leaflet rule is not extended to decide these organizational arrangements.

#### Law After Decision

Effective May 15, these statutory responsive-financing and linked notice arrangements remain unenforceable, the $100 covered-committee/fund ceiling is invalid, and MCCL receives the established as-applied independent-spending protection. No five-Justice responsive-financing framework is established. The reporting, intermediary-contribution fit and functional MCCL applications have direct controlling majorities as stated. Ordinary disclosure, valid contribution/conduit rules, voluntary public financing and Austin's materially different corporate context remain intact.

### Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde; No. 94-3 - 1995-05-15

Authority: [Record](../records/Reynoldsville_Casket_Co_v_Hyde_merits_1995-05-15.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Reliance alone does not permit withholding a governing federal rule

**Holding and operative rule:** A state court must apply a controlling federal constitutional rule to a case still open on direct review and cannot withhold that rule merely because a litigant relied on the invalidated statute. Independently valid procedural or remedial rules may determine relief, but cannot reproduce selective prospectivity under a different label.

**Authority:** Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: seven direct votes; Kennedy and O'Connor concur in the judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Ohio cannot preserve the legal operation of its unconstitutional tolling provision solely for claims accruing before Bendix. Bendix establishes the interstate-commerce defect in that provision. Harper requires a federal rule applied to the original litigants to govern still-open cases within its substantive scope. Beam supplies the distinction between applying the rule and deciding an independently supported remedy. Ohio's reliance ground is tied to the date of Bendix and continues the invalid tolling rule for earlier claims. Calling that treatment a remedy does not create an independent legal basis. Reliance can matter to an actual equitable doctrine with its own lawful prerequisites; this judgment establishes none. Finality, valid procedural bars, immunity, an independently justified equitable rule or a constitutional remedial requirement can present different grounds. Reich concerns withdrawal of an apparent tax remedy and does not guarantee survival of this tort action. The Court remands without deciding whether another valid ground supports relief.

**Precedent treatment:**
- Bendix Autolite, 486 U.S. 888: applies its invalidation of Ohio's interstate-commerce burden; no expansion of that constitutional holding.
- Harper, June 18, 1993: applies equal rule treatment on open direct review; final judgments and independently valid remedies remain distinct.
- Beam, 501 U.S. 529: applies rejection of selective retroactivity and preserves actual procedural/remedial questions.
- Chevron Oil, 404 U.S. 97: cannot support the selective temporal exception Harper displaces; no additional displacement is decided.
- Reich, December 6, 1994: distinguishes its apparent tax-remedy withdrawal; creates no automatic tort-claim preservation.

#### Precedent Treatment

Bendix supplies the constitutional rule; Harper supplies equal application in open cases. Beam's rule/remedy distinction is preserved. Chevron Oil supplies no selective-prospectivity escape. Reich remains confined to its represented tax remedy; no precedent is newly overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Effective May 15, mere reliance on the unconstitutional tolling rule cannot sustain Ohio's temporal exception. Harper is applied without reopening final judgments or foreclosing genuine independently valid procedural/remedial doctrines.

### First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan; No. 94-560. - 1995-05-22

Authority: [Record](../records/First_Options_of_Chicago_Inc_v_Kaplan_merits_1995-05-22.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Antecedent assent and delegation

**Holding and operative rule:** Courts independently determine agreement to arbitrate arbitrability unless ordinary applicable contract law establishes clear and unmistakable delegation. Presenting an objection while denying panel jurisdiction does not itself establish delegation; company submission does not itself establish an owner's personal assent. Where parties actually delegate arbitrability, limited award review governs that decision.

**Authority:** Breyer's Opinion of the Court, Part I; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer on the antecedent-assent rule and application; Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer alone on the additional conditional actual-delegation review rule (Stone-Zsela reserves that proposition) directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that arbitration depends on agreement, including agreement about who decides whether arbitration is required. AT&T Technologies supplies clear and unmistakable delegation before arbitrators are treated as the chosen decisionmakers on arbitrability. Volt supplies fidelity to the actual contractual arrangement. A presumption favoring coverage within an accepted arbitration agreement does not answer this antecedent decisionmaker question. MKI signed the workout document containing arbitration language and submitted its dispute, but that conduct cannot automatically establish the Kaplans' personal agreement. Their appearance to object asks the panel to decline authority rather than clearly entrusting authority to it. Independent review therefore governs their objections. Actual delegation instead entails limited review, a proposition eight Justices join. Broad clauses, incorporated rules and independent nonsignatory-binding theories remain for cases presenting them; MKI's submission is distinct. Stone-Zsela reserves merits-award deference, and the Court permits no rehearing of an agreed arbitration's merits.

**Precedent treatment:**

- AT&T Technologies, Inc. v. Communications Workers, 475 U.S. 643: applies clear-and-unmistakable delegation.
- Volt Information Sciences, Inc. v. Board of Trustees, 489 U.S. 468: applies arbitration's contractual foundation.
- Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, January 18, 1995: federal coverage supplies no missing assent.
- Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc., March 6, 1995: actual integrated contract terms preserve the antecedent-consent inquiry.

### Ordinary appellate review

**Holding and operative rule:** Appellate courts independently review district-court legal conclusions and review factual findings for clear error. Arbitration supplies no additional appellate deference to the district judge's independent arbitrability determination.

**Authority:** Breyer's Opinion of the Court, Part II; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the ordinary division between review of law and review of fact governs this appeal. A district court's independent decision about assent is a judicial decision. Arbitration gives that judge no additional authority to resolve law conclusively for the appellate court. AT&T Technologies distinguishes the decisionmaker question from underlying merits; Volt requires fidelity to the actual agreement. Those principles explain why deference to an agreed arbitrator cannot simply be transferred to a district judge when parties did not delegate arbitrability. The contrary argument treats arbitration-related judicial action as deserving special insulation, but confuses review of an award with review of the district court's own decision. Legal conclusions remain independently reviewable and findings retain clear-error review. The Court affirms the Third Circuit without creating a new vacatur ground for the merits of an actually agreed arbitration.

**Precedent treatment:**

- AT&T Technologies, 475 U.S. 643: preserves decisionmaker/merits distinctions.
- Volt, 489 U.S. 468: actual agreement explains arbitral deference; it insulates no district-court legal conclusion.

#### Precedent Treatment

- AT&T Technologies, Inc. v. Communications Workers, 475 U.S. 643: applies clear-and-unmistakable delegation.
- Volt Information Sciences, Inc. v. Board of Trustees, 489 U.S. 468: applies arbitration's contractual foundation.
- Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, January 18, 1995: federal coverage supplies no missing assent.
- Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc., March 6, 1995: actual integrated contract terms preserve the antecedent-consent inquiry.
- AT&T Technologies, 475 U.S. 643: preserves decisionmaker/merits distinctions.
- Volt, 489 U.S. 468: actual agreement explains arbitral deference; it insulates no district-court legal conclusion.

#### Law After Decision

Courts independently determine agreement to arbitrate arbitrability unless ordinary applicable contract law establishes clear and unmistakable delegation. Presenting an objection while denying panel jurisdiction does not itself establish delegation; company submission does not itself establish an owner's personal assent. Where parties actually delegate arbitrability, limited award review governs that decision. Appellate courts independently review district-court legal conclusions and review factual findings for clear error. Arbitration supplies no additional appellate deference to the district judge's independent arbitrability determination.

### Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois; No. 94-783 (simulation-assigned). - 1995-05-22

Authority: [Record](../records/Kelley_v_Board_of_Trustees_of_the_University_of_Illinois_merits_1995-05-22.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Program-wide participation opportunity

**Holding and operative rule:** Title IX does not guarantee preservation of a particular varsity team or sport-for-sport counterparts. Lawful separate teams remain subject to equal opportunity; effective accommodation may be established through substantial proportionality, a history and continuing responsive expansion for the underrepresented sex, or full and effective accommodation of that sex's interests and abilities, with any one route sufficient and distinct regulatory requirements independently binding.

**Authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, Part I; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that loss of men's swimming does not itself establish denial of equal athletic opportunity. Section 106.41 permits separate teams for competitive-skill selection or contact sports while requiring equal opportunity. For a noncontact team offered only to one sex, the excluded sex must receive tryout opportunities when its athletic opportunities previously were limited. The guidance offers three alternative accommodation routes; proportionality is a safe harbor, not a compulsory quota. Section 1681(b) rejects required preferential treatment based merely on imbalance while permitting relevant statistical evidence. Chevron and Martin support lawful delegated interpretation, not displacement of statutory limits. The lower record establishes that men's participation remained above substantial proportionality after the cut. Plaintiffs identify no counterpart or preservation requirement. This ruling does not decide complete compliance for women or excuse unequal facilities, coaching, equipment, scheduling or any other independently regulated benefit.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837: applies lawful delegated interpretation; authorizes no quota contrary to statute.
- Martin v. Occupational Safety & Health Review Commission, 499 U.S. 144: applies reasonable regulatory interpretation within its terms.

### Independent constitutional scrutiny

**Holding and operative rule:** A public university may consider sex during genuine fiscal or programmatic restructuring to preserve opportunities against demonstrated inequality only when the choice serves an actual important objective and is substantially related to it. Ratios, budget pressure or attempted federal compliance alone are insufficient; stereotypes, pretext and measures materially broader than the demonstrated need are forbidden.

**Authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, Part II; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that attempted federal compliance does not exempt a public university's classification from constitutional scrutiny. Craig supplies the important-objective and substantial-relationship test. Hogan requires a genuine objective connected to actual disadvantage rather than a remedial label or stereotype. This record supplies more than percentages. OCR earlier identified denied opportunities, although it concluded there was no violation upon promised correction; women's participation remained markedly unequal a decade later. The University reviewed nineteen programs during genuine restructuring and preserved swimming places for women with demonstrated interest and ability. Preservation of those real places substantially serves the objective without commanding parity. The reported deficit preceded unexpected bowl income; no final income calculation is reconstructed. Plaintiffs' statistics-only objection fails because preservation is the relevant connection. The rule applies symmetrically to a demonstrably disadvantaged sex and decides no classification based on sexual orientation, gender identity, transgender status or individual team eligibility.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Craig v. Boren, 429 U.S. 190: applies intermediate scrutiny to sex-conscious treatment.
- Mississippi University for Women v. Hogan, 458 U.S. 718: applies genuine-objective and connected-disadvantage requirements.
- Metro Broadcasting, Inc. v. FCC, 497 U.S. 547: its racial-treatment analysis supplies no changed sex standard or federal-compliance immunity.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837: applies lawful delegated interpretation; authorizes no quota contrary to statute.
- Martin v. Occupational Safety & Health Review Commission, 499 U.S. 144: applies reasonable regulatory interpretation within its terms.
- Craig v. Boren, 429 U.S. 190: applies intermediate scrutiny to sex-conscious treatment.
- Mississippi University for Women v. Hogan, 458 U.S. 718: applies genuine-objective and connected-disadvantage requirements.
- Metro Broadcasting, Inc. v. FCC, 497 U.S. 547: its racial-treatment analysis supplies no changed sex standard or federal-compliance immunity.

#### Law After Decision

Title IX does not guarantee preservation of a particular varsity team or sport-for-sport counterparts. Lawful separate teams remain subject to equal opportunity; effective accommodation may be established through substantial proportionality, a history and continuing responsive expansion for the underrepresented sex, or full and effective accommodation of that sex's interests and abilities, with any one route sufficient and distinct regulatory requirements independently binding. A public university may consider sex during genuine fiscal or programmatic restructuring to preserve opportunities against demonstrated inequality only when the choice serves an actual important objective and is substantially related to it. Ratios, budget pressure or attempted federal compliance alone are insufficient; stereotypes, pretext and measures materially broader than the demonstrated need are forbidden.

### U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill; Nos. 93-1456, 93-1828 - 1995-05-22

Authority: [Record](../records/US_Term_Limits_v_Thornton_Bryant_v_Hill_merits_1995-05-22.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### States cannot add congressional qualifications through the ordinary ballot

**Holding and operative rule:** A State may not deny an otherwise constitutionally qualified congressional candidate ordinary ballot access because of prior congressional service when that restriction adds a substantive personal qualification. The exclusive Article I qualifications cannot be supplemented indirectly through the ballot; genuine time, place and manner administration remains distinct.

**Authority:** Stevens's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: six direct votes, in both consolidated dockets.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Arkansas cannot use prior congressional service to disqualify otherwise eligible candidates from the ordinary ballot. Powell establishes the fixed character of congressional qualifications and protects electors' choice of constitutionally eligible representatives against additional substantive exclusions. The national offices created by Article I do not become state offices subject to a reserved power to alter their qualifications. The Elections Clause allows administration of the manner of elections; it does not authorize a service-based disability on who may represent the people. Arkansas answers that a candidate may still seek write-in votes and that Powell concerned Congress rather than a State.
