But the State deliberately excludes the candidate from the ordinary ballot for a personal eligibility characteristic, achieving indirectly the additional qualification it lacks authority to impose directly. Storer preserves genuine election regulation rather than such additions. The Court leaves neutral filing, petition, support and ballot-management requirements, and Arkansas's separate state-office provisions, outside this holding.

**Precedent treatment:**
- Powell, 395 U.S. 486: applies fixed congressional qualifications and elector choice to the State's service-based restriction; Congress's own exclusion was its original setting.
- Storer, 415 U.S. 724: distinguishes genuine electoral administration from added substantive qualifications; ordinary neutral election rules are preserved.
- Gregory, 501 U.S. 452: distinguishes state control of its own officers from qualification of federal representatives; its actual state-officer protection remains intact.

#### Precedent Treatment

Powell's fixed national qualifications are applied to substantive state additions; Storer's genuine election-administration category remains. Gregory concerns state officers and supplies no reserved authority over qualifications for Congress. Montana and Lopez remain at their own apportionment and national/state allocation scopes; neither supplies a separate qualifications holding.

#### Law After Decision

Effective May 22, substantive state additions to congressional qualifications, including this service-based ordinary-ballot restriction, are impermissible. Genuine neutral election administration remains available. No general judicial authority to require competitive elections or redesign Congress is recognized.

### Wilson v. Arkansas; No. 94-5707. - 1995-05-22

Authority: [Record](../records/Wilson_v_Arkansas_merits_1995-05-22.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Announcement and reasonable home entry

**Holding and operative rule:** Common-law announcement of identity and authority forms part of Fourth Amendment reasonableness when officers enter a dwelling. Supported countervailing circumstances, including danger, futility, escape or threatened destruction of evidence, may justify unannounced entry; actual justification and remedy remain for the state court.

**Authority:** Thomas's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a lawful warrant does not answer every question about how officers enter a home. Announcement protects occupants, officers, property and the opportunity to comply peacefully with lawful authority. Miller supplies the common-law background; Sabbath illustrates federal statutory entry requirements without making every statutory detail constitutional. Ker supplies constitutional scrutiny of state entry and circumstance-dependent excuses. Arkansas therefore erred by treating announcement as constitutionally irrelevant. Its concern for effective law enforcement does not require categorical exclusion of an established entry protection: the reasonableness inquiry accommodates supported danger, futility, escape and threatened evidence destruction. The Court does not decide whether the entry here had a sufficient justification, what exact quantum of suspicion each exception requires, or whether every violation requires suppression. Those application and remedy questions remain separate and must be considered on the actual record on remand.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Miller v. United States, 357 U.S. 301: supplies common-law announcement background; federal statutory particulars are not all constitutionalized.
- Sabbath v. United States, 391 U.S. 585: supplies federal statutory entry analysis without deciding this constitutional application.
- Ker v. California, 374 U.S. 23: applies state-entry reasonableness with circumstance-dependent excuses.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Miller v. United States, 357 U.S. 301: supplies common-law announcement background; federal statutory particulars are not all constitutionalized.
- Sabbath v. United States, 391 U.S. 585: supplies federal statutory entry analysis without deciding this constitutional application.
- Ker v. California, 374 U.S. 23: applies state-entry reasonableness with circumstance-dependent excuses.

#### Law After Decision

Common-law announcement of identity and authority forms part of Fourth Amendment reasonableness when officers enter a dwelling. Supported countervailing circumstances, including danger, futility, escape or threatened destruction of evidence, may justify unannounced entry; actual justification and remedy remain for the state court.

### Garlotte v. Fordice, No. 94-6790 - 1995-05-30

Authority: [Record](../records/Garlotte_v_Fordice_merits_1995-05-30.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Custody under a continuous consecutive series

**Holding and operative rule:** A prisoner serving a continuous unexpired series of consecutive sentences remains in custody under the earlier conviction when invalidating it would advance release from the aggregate term, although its first segment has been served. This establishes the custody threshold, leaving the conviction’s validity and remaining procedural and merits conditions for review.

**Authority:** Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer; eight Justices directly support this rule.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the order of consecutive sentences does not determine whether present imprisonment permits review. Peyton treats such sentences as an aggregate and allows review before a challenged future segment begins. Its central rule rejects isolation of one segment from the continuous custody that the judgments together produce. The same rule applies when the challenged segment came first: Garlotte remains imprisoned under the consecutive series, and invalidating the marijuana conviction would advance his release date. Maleng excludes reliance on the collateral consequences of a wholly expired independent sentence. It does not require severing this unexpired aggregate at the point its first segment was served. The State’s sentence-specific approach would make jurisdiction turn on sequence even though present confinement and the effect of relief remain the same. The Court decides only custody jurisdiction. It neither finds the marijuana conviction invalid nor awards relief without the procedural and merits review that remains necessary.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Peyton v. Rowe, 391 U.S. 54: extends its aggregate treatment from a future segment to a completed first segment of a still-unexpired consecutive series when invalidation advances release.
- Maleng v. Cook, 490 U.S. 488: distinguishes a wholly expired independent sentence and preserves the rule that collateral consequences alone do not supply custody under that conviction.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Peyton v. Rowe, 391 U.S. 54: extends its aggregate treatment from a future segment to a completed first segment of a still-unexpired consecutive series when invalidation advances release.
- Maleng v. Cook, 490 U.S. 488: distinguishes a wholly expired independent sentence and preserves the rule that collateral consequences alone do not supply custody under that conviction.

#### Law After Decision

The first sentence in an unexpired continuous consecutive series is not jurisdictionally finished when its conviction still affects duration of present custody. Maleng’s rule for an independently expired sentence remains intact. No jurisdiction rests here solely on collateral consequences after all custody ends. Ordinary habeas procedural and merits requirements remain distinct from this custody determination.

### Nebraska v. Wyoming; No. 108, Original. - 1995-05-30

Authority: [Record](../records/Nebraska_v_Wyoming_original_exceptions_1995-05-30.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Settled allocation and discrete defenses

**Holding and operative rule:** The decree's 75/25 natural-flow allocation cannot be replaced through these amendments by a mass beneficial-use ceiling. Reject the First Counterclaim and First Cross-Claim insofar as they seek that ceiling, while preserving discrete geographic, priority, unlawful-call and relevant waste defenses not dependent on it.

**Authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that retained jurisdiction does not give a State another opportunity to replace an adjudicated allocation. Nebraska's 1945 decision supplies the percentage allocation and its geography and priorities; the 1953 decree preserves those terms as modified. The April 20, 1993 decision distinguishes enforcement of an established right from modification requiring clear and convincing evidence of real and substantial injury. Wyoming's proposed mass beneficial-use ceiling substitutes a different measure for the settled percentage. A desire for quantified certainty does not itself establish the basis for that change. But rejecting the formula does not establish that Nebraska may make calls outside the decree, bypass priorities or waste water without legal consequence. Those discrete enforcement and defensive matters do not logically depend on the new ceiling and remain available in their proper scope. No alleged violation, waste quantity or final restriction is decided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: preserves allocation, geography and priority terms.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 345 U.S. 981: preserves the decree as modified in 1953.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury. Its Inland Lakes acquiescence alternative is not generalized here.

### Groundwater and equitable connection

**Holding and operative rule:** Specific upstream groundwater development and alleged depletion threatening the decree allocation may be pleaded. Nebraska's own pumping creates no categorical unclean-hands bar without relevant conduct and connection to the asserted harm; supported defenses and applicable eventual proof requirements remain.

**Authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that alleged groundwater effects may be examined without deciding that any well violates the decree. The April 20, 1993 decision supplies the distinction between existing-right enforcement and new allocation, including the latter's clear-and-convincing substantial-injury burden. Kansas v. Colorado supplies proof-sensitive treatment of pumping and concrete equitable defenses, but decides neither these States' hydrology nor a universal enforcement quantum. Nebraska identifies upstream development and alleged effects on protected natural flow. These allegations warrant inquiry; they do not invite general supervision of every well. Wyoming's strongest objection invokes Nebraska's own pumping. Similar activity alone does not establish legally relevant misconduct, injury to Wyoming or connection to this claim. Wyoming remains free to prove a properly connected defense. Leave therefore neither establishes interstate injury nor immunizes Nebraska's conduct. Actual injury, causation and any permissible restriction remain for evidence before the Master.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.
- Kansas v. Colorado, May 15, 1995: applies proof-sensitive pumping and defense analysis without importing Compact elements or factual conclusions.

### Downstream and habitat evidence

**Holding and operative rule:** Downstream nonirrigation and habitat effects may be considered insofar as relevant to injury and lawful modification in admitted decree claims. Relevance creates no independent instream entitlement, habitat-flow quantity, free-standing environmental right or automatic priority; Count IV remains denied without prejudice.

**Authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that evidence of injury and entitlement to a new allocation are different questions. Nebraska's 1945 decision supplies the allocation's setting, and the April 20, 1993 decision supplies the proof required for enforcement or modification. Downstream conditions may demonstrate the consequences of threatened reduction of protected water. Excluding all nonirrigation consequences would remove potentially relevant evidence merely because the decree apportioned irrigation water. Nebraska's contrary risk is equally real: evidence relevant to injury cannot confer a new habitat right. The admitted claims and applicable modification burden supply the limit. Count IV's wider seasonal theory is not admitted, and no wildlife-specific quantity is adjudicated. The Master may examine relevant effects within the authorized controversy. Actual injury, changed conditions and entitlement remain for proof before a different allocation or operating obligation can issue; no independent public environmental right is established.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: supplies the allocation setting without independent habitat rights.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.

### Horse Creek and supporting conditions

**Holding and operative rule:** Horse Creek's location below Tri-State Dam does not alone bar a specific changed-condition allegation threatening the decree's balance. A material decree connection and eventual modification proof remain required; no tributary-wide apportionment or finding of depletion follows.

**Authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the pivotal reach does not exclude every supporting downstream condition from the retained proceeding. Nebraska's 1945 decision supplies the original allocation and its setting. The April 20, 1993 decision preserves warranted modification while demanding clear and convincing real and substantial injury. Nebraska alleges Horse Creek changes affect supply conditions and thereby threaten the existing balance. Entry below Tri-State Dam matters to that causal inquiry but does not alone resolve it. Wyoming's strongest objection is that an indirect relationship could become a device for a basin-wide allocation. The answer is a proved material connection and the governing modification burden, not automatic admission of every remote dispute. The bounded allegation may be tested; its geographic label neither proves nor defeats it. No entitlement to Horse Creek water, cause or magnitude of depletion, or new allocation among tributaries is decided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: applies the allocation setting and preserves geographic limits.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.

### Federal administration as decree predicate

**Holding and operative rule:** The Fourth Cross-Claim may proceed only for alleged changed federal storage administration materially affecting the interstate decree's operation or supporting conditions. Both United States and Nebraska exceptions are overruled within that scope; independent contractual compliance remains outside the original proceeding and in its lawful separate channel.

**Authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, federal-administration portion; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the object of this claim is the interstate decree, not enforcement of every storage contract. The original Nebraska decree and retained jurisdiction supply the allocation under examination. Texas v. New Mexico supplies meaningful enforcement of interstate obligations within their terms, while the April 20, 1993 Nebraska decision separates enforcement from modification. Wyoming alleges changed federal administration affecting conditions supporting the allocation. Storage water's exclusion from direct apportionment does not make those alleged effects irrelevant. The strongest objection points to Goshen litigation and original-jurisdiction limits. Those objections bar independent contractual compliance here but do not require leaving an alleged decree predicate unexamined. The Master must maintain that distinction. Admission establishes no federal breach, general immunity waiver, damages action or absent party's contract rights. It displaces no separate litigation and requires proof before decree relief is available.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589, and 345 U.S. 981: preserve allocation and retained jurisdiction without allocating storage water.
- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554: applies interstate enforcement within operative scope, not general contract jurisdiction.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.

### Exact leave and next stage

**Holding and operative rule:** Permit Nebraska Counts I-III and Wyoming Second-Fourth Counterclaims and Second-Fifth Cross-Claims within the preceding limits; deny Nebraska Count IV without prejudice and reject only impermissible ceiling portions of First Counterclaim and First Cross-Claim. Return Glendo, Laramie and carriage-loss theories to the Master for applicable proof; leave establishes no injury, breach, water award, priority, damages or injunction.

**Authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an express pleading disposition defines the next stage without deciding its outcome. The April 20, 1993 decision supplies different demands for enforcing an established right and modifying an allocation. Its use of Rule 56 under Rule 17.2 is a guide to summary disposition, not a rule turning amendments into proof. This event grants bounded leave, not summary judgment on new allegations. The report's unexcepted Glendo, Laramie and carriage-loss recommendations receive an express procedural disposition alongside contested claims. Absence of an exception and the amendment label prove no substantive prerequisite. Nebraska Counts I-III and specified Wyoming pleadings may proceed under applicable conditions; Count IV's broader seasonal theory remains withheld without prejudice. No numerical Glendo release or revised carriage-loss formula is reconstructed. The Master must receive evidence within admitted scope, preserving factual, legal and remedial defenses; the original action remains open.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury. Rule56 remains a limited guide; leave is not proof.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: preserves allocation, geography and priority terms.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 345 U.S. 981: preserves the decree as modified in 1953.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury. Its Inland Lakes acquiescence alternative is not generalized here.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.
- Kansas v. Colorado, May 15, 1995: applies proof-sensitive pumping and defense analysis without importing Compact elements or factual conclusions.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: supplies the allocation setting without independent habitat rights.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: applies the allocation setting and preserves geographic limits.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589, and 345 U.S. 981: preserve allocation and retained jurisdiction without allocating storage water.
- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554: applies interstate enforcement within operative scope, not general contract jurisdiction.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury. Rule56 remains a limited guide; leave is not proof.

#### Law After Decision

The decree's 75/25 natural-flow allocation cannot be replaced through these amendments by a mass beneficial-use ceiling. Reject the First Counterclaim and First Cross-Claim insofar as they seek that ceiling, while preserving discrete geographic, priority, unlawful-call and relevant waste defenses not dependent on it. Specific upstream groundwater development and alleged depletion threatening the decree allocation may be pleaded. Nebraska's own pumping creates no categorical unclean-hands bar without relevant conduct and connection to the asserted harm; supported defenses and applicable eventual proof requirements remain. Downstream nonirrigation and habitat effects may be considered insofar as relevant to injury and lawful modification in admitted decree claims. Relevance creates no independent instream entitlement, habitat-flow quantity, free-standing environmental right or automatic priority; Count IV remains denied without prejudice. Horse Creek's location below Tri-State Dam does not alone bar a specific changed-condition allegation threatening the decree's balance. A material decree connection and eventual modification proof remain required; no tributary-wide apportionment or finding of depletion follows. The Fourth Cross-Claim may proceed only for alleged changed federal storage administration materially affecting the interstate decree's operation or supporting conditions. Both United States and Nebraska exceptions are overruled within that scope; independent contractual compliance remains outside the original proceeding and in its lawful separate channel. Permit Nebraska Counts I-III and Wyoming Second-Fourth Counterclaims and Second-Fifth Cross-Claims within the preceding limits; deny Nebraska Count IV without prejudice and reject only impermissible ceiling portions of First Counterclaim and First Cross-Claim. Return Glendo, Laramie and carriage-loss theories to the Master for applicable proof; leave establishes no injury, breach, water award, priority, damages or injunction.

### North Star Steel Co. v. Thomas / Crown Cork & Seal Co., Inc. v. United Steelworkers of America, AFL-CIO-CLC, Nos. 94-834 and 94-835 - 1995-05-30

Authority: [Record](../records/North_Star_Steel_v_Thomas_and_Crown_Cork_merits_1995-05-30.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Source and application of the WARN limitations period

**Holding and operative rule:** For these WARN claims, courts must borrow an appropriate analogous state limitations period because NLRA § 10(b) is neither a clearly closer analogy nor shown significantly more suitable through federal policy and litigation practicalities. Both claims are timely under the state candidates presented; the Court need not select an exact statute or decide the WARN merits.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices directly support the rule and application. Scalia joins only the judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that WARN’s notice and compensation action follows ordinary state borrowing. DelCostello supplies the default and a narrow exception requiring both a clearly closer federal analogy and significantly greater suitability in light of federal policy and litigation practicalities. Reed confirms that employment-related litigation does not by that label satisfy the exception. WARN protects a substantive right independent of a direct attack on a collective-bargaining settlement; Fort Halifax explains why such employment protections need not be equated with bargaining machinery. The employers’ desire for nationwide certainty and rapid resolution is understandable, but those ordinary effects of borrowing do not demonstrate that the available state periods frustrate this right. The presented state candidates permit both suits, so choosing among them would not affect these judgments. Courts still must select an appropriate analogy when that selection matters. The Court decides no liability, damages, accrual, tolling, arbitration requirement, or statutory merits exception.

**Precedent treatment:**

- DelCostello v. Teamsters, 462 U.S. 151: preserves the ordinary state-borrowing rule and confines its federal exception to a genuinely closer analogy and significantly greater federal suitability; its hybrid labor-settlement application is distinguished.
- Reed v. United Transportation Union, 488 U.S. 319: applies its restrictive account of the exception; a labor setting alone remains insufficient.
- Fort Halifax Packing Co. v. Coyne, 482 U.S. 1: applies its separation of independent substantive employment protections from bargaining mechanisms; no arbitration question is decided.

#### Precedent Treatment

- DelCostello v. Teamsters, 462 U.S. 151: preserves the ordinary state-borrowing rule and confines its federal exception to a genuinely closer analogy and significantly greater federal suitability; its hybrid labor-settlement application is distinguished.
- Reed v. United Transportation Union, 488 U.S. 319: applies its restrictive account of the exception; a labor setting alone remains insufficient.
- Fort Halifax Packing Co. v. Coyne, 482 U.S. 1: applies its separation of independent substantive employment protections from bargaining mechanisms; no arbitration question is decided.

#### Law After Decision

An appropriate analogous state limitations period governs these WARN claims. Both suits are timely under the state candidates presented. The narrow federal-borrowing exception remains available when its actual requirements are met; no universal state period or guarantee that every state period is adequate is created. Section 1658 does not furnish a period for this 1988 cause. Arbitration, exhaustion, statutory deferral and any associated tolling are separate issues outside this review; no obligation or suspension rule is created.

### United States v. Wellons, No. 94-496 - 1995-05-30

Authority: [Record](../records/United_States_v_Wellons_merits_1995-05-30.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Vehicle privacy and the rental agreement

**Holding and operative rule:** Absence from a rental agreement does not by itself defeat a driver’s Fourth Amendment privacy claim when he possesses and controls the vehicle with the authorized renter’s permission and without theft, fraud or comparable wrongful possession. Courts must evaluate the actual legitimate relationship, including the right to exclude, the owner’s retained rights and any effective termination of possession; renter permission alone does not establish privacy.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela’s Opinion of the Court, joined in this proposition by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; all nine directly adopt it.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a private driver restriction is relevant but does not conclusively decide constitutional privacy. Rakas requires the defendant’s own legitimate interest; Rawlings requires attention to the actual circumstances rather than an automatic property formula. Olson shows that invitation and recognized use can support personal privacy without title. The inquiry here therefore considers the authorized renter’s permission, legitimate possession, practical control, ability to exclude and the owner’s retained rights. Padilla likewise requires individualized rights rather than a proxy claim. The rental company’s request for impoundment may bear on whether legitimate possession continued at search time; the Court does not disregard that fact or decide its legal effect without findings. The Government cannot replace this inquiry with the contract’s name list alone. Nor may a driver prevail merely by showing permission if theft, fraud, comparable wrongful possession or effective repossession defeats the claimed relationship. The remand determines privacy; it does not establish it.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Rakas v. Illinois, 439 U.S. 128: applies its requirement of the claimant’s own legitimate interest, rejecting proxy rights while refusing a contract-name shortcut.
- Rawlings v. Kentucky, 448 U.S. 98: applies the circumstance-specific privacy inquiry; possession or property alone is not conclusive.
- Minnesota v. Olson, 495 U.S. 91: extends its recognition that an invited user can have personal privacy without title to this bounded inquiry; it supplies no automatic rental-driver privacy.
- United States v. Padilla, May 3, 1993: applies the individualized search/seizure inquiry; no automatic derivative rights are created.

### Independent privacy in closed personal luggage

**Holding and operative rule:** A person’s lack of vehicle privacy does not by itself extinguish a legitimate expectation of privacy in that person’s closed luggage inside the vehicle. Ownership or lawful control and measures preserving privacy must be established independently, and a valid warrant exception reaching the container may still authorize its search.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela’s Opinion of the Court, joined on this separate proposition by all eight Associate Justices; nine directly adopt it.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the vehicle does not absorb every constitutional interest in an object placed inside it. Chadwick recognizes personal privacy in closed luggage as distinct from the characteristics of its surrounding vehicle. Rawlings requires the claimant to demonstrate his own interest, so a bare assertion about every bag cannot substitute for ownership, lawful control and circumstances preserving privacy. The Fourth Circuit’s categorical derivative rule eliminated this separate inquiry solely because Wellons was absent from the rental agreement. That ground cannot resolve a personal container claim. Acevedo nevertheless permits an automobile probable-cause search to reach containers within its lawful scope, and Sanders’s contrary automobile warrant restriction is not restored. Privacy establishes the personal interest whose invasion may be challenged, not the unlawfulness of every search. On remand the courts must determine each claimed container’s status, any disclaimer, and the actual search authority; the Court makes no ownership finding or container-specific probable-cause determination.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Chadwick, 433 U.S. 1: preserves personal closed-luggage privacy as distinct from vehicle mobility, subject to the later applicable automobile rule.
- Rawlings v. Kentucky, 448 U.S. 98: applies the requirement of a demonstrated personal privacy interest; ownership or lawful control and privacy measures require assessment.
- California v. Acevedo, 500 U.S. 565: preserves automobile probable-cause authority to search containers within its scope; personal container privacy does not impose a categorical warrant requirement.
- Arkansas v. Sanders, 442 U.S. 753: retains container privacy only consistently with Acevedo’s displacement of its contrary automobile warrant rule; that displaced rule is not revived.

### Remedy after rejection of the categorical grounds

**Holding and operative rule:** When the reviewed categorical privacy grounds are erroneous but personal interests and preserved search justifications remain unresolved, the judgment must be vacated and remanded for those determinations before a suppression consequence is imposed. A recognized interest does not foreclose lawful probable-cause search, consent, standardized noninvestigatory inventory after lawful custody, or another preserved warrant exception.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela’s Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight Associate Justices; nine directly adopt this case’s remedial holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that correcting the privacy rules does not establish the final suppression result. Acevedo permits searches supported by lawful automobile probable cause within their actual scope; Padilla separates the individual protected interest from relief requiring additional determinations. The rental company’s request is not itself a finding of lawful impoundment or consent to open every personal bag. The dog alert is an event in the record, not proof that the preceding detention and sniff were lawful or that probable cause extended without limit. Inventory authority requires lawful custody and standardized noninvestigatory procedures, and consent requires lawful consent within its scope. These questions were not resolved by the categorical ground supporting the conviction affirmance. The lower courts must address them on supported findings, together with luggage ownership and any applicable ordinary harmlessness. Vacatur and remand therefore correct the legal premise while preserving lawful admission grounds. The Court orders neither automatic exclusion nor resentencing.

**Precedent treatment:**

- California v. Acevedo, 500 U.S. 565: applies the distinction between an interest and a lawful probable-cause search reaching a container; no automatic suppression follows recognition of privacy.
- United States v. Padilla, May 3, 1993: applies its individual-interest remand discipline without deciding unproved exception predicates.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Rakas v. Illinois, 439 U.S. 128: applies its requirement of the claimant’s own legitimate interest, rejecting proxy rights while refusing a contract-name shortcut.
- Rawlings v. Kentucky, 448 U.S. 98: applies the circumstance-specific privacy inquiry; possession or property alone is not conclusive.
- Minnesota v. Olson, 495 U.S. 91: extends its recognition that an invited user can have personal privacy without title to this bounded inquiry; it supplies no automatic rental-driver privacy.
- United States v. Padilla, May 3, 1993: applies the individualized search/seizure inquiry; no automatic derivative rights are created.

- United States v. Chadwick, 433 U.S. 1: preserves personal closed-luggage privacy as distinct from vehicle mobility, subject to the later applicable automobile rule.
- Rawlings v. Kentucky, 448 U.S. 98: applies the requirement of a demonstrated personal privacy interest; ownership or lawful control and privacy measures require assessment.
- California v. Acevedo, 500 U.S. 565: preserves automobile probable-cause authority to search containers within its scope; personal container privacy does not impose a categorical warrant requirement.
- Arkansas v. Sanders, 442 U.S. 753: retains container privacy only consistently with Acevedo’s displacement of its contrary automobile warrant rule; that displaced rule is not revived.

- California v. Acevedo, 500 U.S. 565: applies the distinction between an interest and a lawful probable-cause search reaching a container; no automatic suppression follows recognition of privacy.
- United States v. Padilla, May 3, 1993: applies its individual-interest remand discipline without deciding unproved exception predicates.

#### Law After Decision

A rental agreement’s name list does not alone settle a driver’s personal Fourth Amendment interest. Permission, legitimate possession and practical control must be assessed, including the owner’s retained rights and any actual termination of possession. A driver lacking vehicle privacy may still have an independent interest in his own closed luggage. A recognized interest does not defeat a valid search exception. No thief, fraudulent possessor or person remaining after effective repossession obtains privacy merely through this decision; permission alone is insufficient when the full circumstances show no legitimate or socially recognized control.

### Reno v. Koray, No. 94-790 - 1995-06-05

Authority: [Record](../records/Reno_v_Koray_merits_1995-06-05.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Official detention and federal conditional release

**Holding and operative rule:** Time under a federal Bail Reform Act conditional-release order is not official detention under § 3585(b), even when the restrictions are severe, so Koray’s release period requires no conditions-based credit inquiry. This rule concerns the presented federal release/detention distinction, preserves qualifying uncredited detention and judicial review, and does not decide state custody or require physical BOP control for every creditable day.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven directly adopt the status rule and its application without an independently sufficient administrative-deference holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the credit provision must be read with the related release and detention scheme. Sections 3142(c) and 3142(e) distinguish conditional release from detention, and § 3621 supplies the imprisonment context. Restrictive conditions do not convert the established legal status of release into detention under the federal scheme. Wilson assigns initial calculation to the BOP while preserving review and forbidding duplicate credit; it does not answer the present definition by itself. Koray argues that institutional restraint can match jail, but the linked categories resolve this federal period without comparing every alleged restriction or treating allegations as findings. Crandon requires ordinary statutory construction before lenity, and that construction leaves no residual ambiguity here. Administrative consistency and administrability can confirm the reading, but neither independently creates it. Thomas Jefferson University’s own-regulation rule and MCI’s statutory limits do not make the program statement conclusive. The Court reverses the conditions-inquiry order, leaving state custody and unpresented notice claims undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Wilson, March 24, 1992: applies BOP initial computation, no duplicate credit and subsequent lawful review; Wilson did not itself define official detention.
- Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala, June 24, 1994: distinguishes qualified controlling weight for an agency’s own-regulation interpretation from a program statement construing a statute; its own-regulation rule remains intact.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., June 17, 1994: preserves the statutory-boundary requirement; administrative practice does not independently rewrite the credit provision.
- Crandon v. United States, 494 U.S. 152: applies ordinary text and context before lenity; the Court finds no residual ambiguity in the presented federal-release category.

#### Precedent Treatment

- United States v. Wilson, March 24, 1992: applies BOP initial computation, no duplicate credit and subsequent lawful review; Wilson did not itself define official detention.
- Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala, June 24, 1994: distinguishes qualified controlling weight for an agency’s own-regulation interpretation from a program statement construing a statute; its own-regulation rule remains intact.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., June 17, 1994: preserves the statutory-boundary requirement; administrative practice does not independently rewrite the credit provision.
- Crandon v. United States, 494 U.S. 152: applies ordinary text and context before lenity; the Court finds no residual ambiguity in the presented federal-release category.

#### Law After Decision

For the presented federal Bail Reform Act conditional-release period, statutory release status does not become official detention under § 3585(b) merely because the conditions are severe. The mandatory credit command for qualifying detention remains intact, including detention resulting from the offense sentenced or another charge for which arrest occurred after that offense, when the time has not been credited against another sentence. The decision does not universally require physical BOP control for every creditable pretrial day and does not decide state custody. No independent administrative-deference ground or new notice holding is adopted.

No new trial, acquittal, forfeiture or substantive sentencing ruling is ordered.

The matter returns to that court for RFRA adjudication and any district-court findings required. Existing Church-favorable relief and the purpose/severability remand remain undisturbed. ### Reich v. Collins, No. 93-908. — 1994-12-06

The Georgia Supreme Court's denial of constitutionally adequate backward-looking relief is reversed, and the cause is remanded for a clear and certain lawful remedial opportunity. The Federal Circuit judgment is affirmed. The existing remand for administrative redetermination of Gardner's eligibility continues without the invalid fault-or-accident prerequisite. Ordinary Supreme Court review ends; the administrative claim remains for decision under the lawful conditions.

The Fourth Circuit judgment and prospective declaration remain in force at the stated emergency-treatment scope. The conceded original-notice failure, years-late revised billing, prohibited aggregation and liquidated-damages character are not reopened. There are 106 distinct matters after the seven additional unscheduled carryovers are included. No petition-stage event or application appears among the 99 supplied scheduled events; carried applications remain open at their stated stages.

Dates below are the supplied inventory's planning dates, confirmed against Section I of each brief. They are not newly adjudicated Court actions or a certification of historical grants. The fifteen `+` matters use the supplied simulation-assigned dockets; their established merits posture is carried from Standing State without inventing grant dates or petition polls. Consolidated companions count once. No application or petition-stage event is listed among the 99 supplied events.

**Same-day grouping:** every equal event date is one same-day group, including groups split across chunks: 1995-01-10 (1/2), 1995-02-21 (2/3), 1995-04-19 (4/5), 1995-05-15 (5/6), 1995-06-05 (6/7), 1995-06-14 (7/8), and 1995-06-29 (8/9). The next eligible inventory event is **Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, June 5, 1995, chunk 7**. Koray does not enter that same-date matter; the common pre-June-5 baseline remains in force.

## Published noncontrolling positions

These are published separate positions, with the scope and authority specified in each entry. Earlier sitting-Justice positions remain available in [OT1991](../../OT1991/output/), [OT1992](../../OT1992/output/) and [OT1993](../../OT1993/output/). A successor inherits no predecessor’s view.

### Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., including Walsh; No. 93-1197; 513 U.S. 30 — 1994-11-14

Authority: [Record](../records/Hess_v_Port_Authority_Trans_Hudson_Corp_merits_1994-11-14.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

Justice O'Connor, joined by Justices Scalia and Thomas, dissents. She would treat PATH as an arm of the States because the governors and legislatures retain substantial control over its commissioners, actions, projects and spending. Financial exposure matters, but the protection of a State from federal suit also safeguards its institutional authority. A self-supporting state enterprise need not lose that protection merely because it can pay judgments from its own revenues. Lake Country Estates permits an entity-specific finding of arm status; it does not compel treating PATH’s state controls as equivalent to the more local structure there. Puerto Rico Aqueduct recognizes protection from suit for qualifying arms while leaving the qualification question separate.

The dissent does not read Article XV as an unlimited state guarantee. Its equal, conditional administrative appropriations, recommended by the Authority and approved by both governors, bind each State only up to $100,000 annually while operating revenues are inadequate. The claimed sovereign relationship rests on the combined legal controls, not an invented obligation to pay judgments. Having found arm status, the dissent treats consent as a separate requirement. Feeney’s federal-forum consent did not eliminate its conditions; the district courts found both actions outside the one-year period running from accrual. Hilton’s state-court FELA holding does not revive constructive waiver in federal court. The dissent therefore would affirm these dismissals without deciding liability, damages or a new exception to the consent legislation. This position does not control the Court’s status ruling or remand.

### United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc.; No. 93-723; 513 U.S. 64 — 1994-11-29

Authority: [Record](../records/United_States_v_X_Citement_Video_Inc_merits_1994-11-29.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

Justice Scalia, joined by Justice Thomas, dissents. He reads the placement and structure of knowingly as applying to the handling acts, without extending it to the later conditions identifying the depicted conduct. A presumption of criminal fault helps interpret an otherwise unresolved enactment, but cannot overcome what these Justices regard as a clear allocation of the knowledge term. Their agreement with Staples’s contextual presumption does not require a different answer: the problem here is the enacted placement of an express adverb, not simply a statute’s silence about fault. Constitutional avoidance must operate through a permissible construction, not a judicial replacement of that allocation.

Under their reading, the law reaches a person who handles a depiction without awareness of its pornographic character. That content-related defect, in light of Smith and Ferber, supports preserving the appellate reversal. Their conclusion does not depend on making actual knowledge of the performer’s age an unavoidable constitutional requirement. A different enactment could treat performer age differently where the actor knows the pornographic character; the dissent does not decide that different statute. Osborne’s recklessness came from Ohio’s own law and supplies no judicially created federal default.

The dissent accepts the reported finding that respondents knew the performer was underage. It neither asserts factual innocence nor orders retrial because of an imagined missing finding. The finding does not cure the facial absence of adequate content-related fault in the statute as these Justices read it. Although the two renewed definition objections are legally available defenses of the favorable judgment, the dissent reaches neither because its existing ground is sufficient. It also leaves the indictment and actual-tape claims undecided. These positions are noncontrolling; the Court’s statutory construction and bounded remand govern.

### Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, No. 93-1062. — 1994-12-05

Authority: [Record](../records/Church_of_Scientology_Flag_Service_Organization_Inc_v_City_of_Clearwater_merits_1994-12-05.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in leaving the Church's favorable relief and the purpose-and-severability remand undisturbed, but dissents from reopening the surviving identifying and written-policy requirements. He would affirm the Court of Appeals' judgment on the grounds stated in his opinion. RFRA governs future municipal enforcement even though the ordinance predates it, and the Church's inability to invoke an unenacted statute below does not forfeit its protection. The Church must establish a substantial burden; the City then bears production and persuasion on a compelling interest and the least restrictive means.

He regards the existing record as sufficient to invalidate the broad internal financial, operational, organizational, and recordkeeping demands under RFRA. The private-statement option still requires annual sworn information and supporting records, and criminal sanctions reinforce the burden. The sixty-day refund requirement, triggered by a voluntary refund promise, a written request, and willful nonpayment, also displaces the Church's religiously connected refund process. Fraud prevention is compelling, but targeted fraud law, limited identification, transaction-specific investigation, and accurate statements of promised refund terms address the shown secular harms more narrowly. He adds no constitutional holding against those provisions because RFRA suffices.

In his view, the limited identifying requirements establish no substantial religious burden on this record and perform ordinary civil identification without theological licensing or interruption of solicitation pending judicial review. The contemporaneous written-policy requirement, including its good-faith proviso, survives even assuming a substantial burden: concrete evidence of misleading refund representations supports requiring accurate disclosure of the policy voluntarily chosen, without dictating that policy or deciding spiritual entitlement. He also rejects the independent First Amendment challenges to those bounded surviving requirements, subject to purpose and severability.

He would leave the disputed sectarian-purpose issue for the district court because evidence of official hostility or denominational targeting precludes summary judgment for the City without establishing the final factual answer. Florida severability follows that determination. Materially different future applications require their own RFRA assessment. His position supplies no immunity from ordinary fraud, contract, restitution, tax, reporting, or criminal law, no bar to a targeted subpoena or audit grounded in a concrete secular enforcement need and lawful process, and no authority to decide religious truth. Private donations do not become public financing through truthful-solicitation regulation. These application and constitutional positions are his alone; the Court does not adopt them.

### Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund, No. 93-1151. — 1994-12-06

Authority: [Record](../records/Federal_Election_Commission_v_NRA_Political_Victory_Fund_jurisdictional_dismissal_1994-12-06.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK1.md).

Justice Stevens dissents in an opinion joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela. He reads section 437d(a)(6)'s express power to appeal a civil enforcement action through the Commission's general counsel as carrying that action through discretionary Supreme Court review. Section 518(a) itself uses appeal language broadly; the word therefore cannot decide the case without its statutory context. The Commission's exclusive enforcement role and the enacted grant to its own counsel support an exception under the ordinary-construction approach recognized in Providence Journal. The more explicit funding provisions do not negate that affirmative grant. He would retain the timely proceeding for consideration of the merits. He does not adopt later ratification as an additional ground and expresses no conclusion on the constitutionality of the nonvoting members or the underlying contribution dispute.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela also files a separate dissent on the merits. Having accepted independent litigation authority, he would reverse the separation-of-powers ground and remand for the preserved statutory and enforcement issues the Court of Appeals did not decide. He distinguishes legally operative executive control from advice, observation, communication, or persuasion. Congressional officers cannot receive a vote, veto, quorum power, binding directive, or other legal authority over execution by being called nonvoting or ex officio. On the arrangement presented, the appointed Commissioners alone exercise the enforcement powers; the nonvoting officers have no identified authority to authorize, block, suspend, or direct Commission action. The operating account reported below also excludes chairmanship, meeting calls or adjournment, and quorum participation; those operating descriptions are not additional words of the statute.

Buckley concerns officers exercising significant governmental authority; Bowsher concerns executive authority lodged in an officer controlled by Congress; and the Airports Authority decision concerns a legally effective veto over operational decisions. In his view, potential influence without comparable legal power does not establish the constitutional defect those decisions forbid. An advisory label concealing approval, disapproval, delay, compulsory direction, decisive participation, or another legal consequence would present a different case. Because he finds no defect here, he would undertake no severability or structural-ratification inquiry. His proposed remand would not automatically revive the district court's enforcement judgment while other appellate issues remain. No other Justice joins these merits views, and they establish no rule of the Court.

### Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, No. 93-1712 — 1995-02-13

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part; no joins.** The Chief agrees that Casey governs the access challenge and that the actual affected class, the additional burden against existing access conditions, and interacting requirements must be assessed under the complete six-step inquiry. He joins the access remand and prompt consideration of interim protection. He would, however, vacate the entire Eighth Circuit judgment and return each challenged provision for application and legally supported construction, rather than affirm the definition and associated penalty dispositions now.

For the Chief, a practically usable route must permit prompt action in a physician's good-faith clinical judgment when compliance creates a material risk of death, serious physical injury, substantial irreversible impairment or another grave medically established danger to health. Those examples do not narrow Casey's broader necessary-care protection, including relevant physical and mental health. Treatment cannot await approach to death or delay incompatible with the emergency. A protective construction must be fairly supported by the enacted words and controlling state law; the courts must establish the actual culpability requirements rather than presume them. If the text cannot bear the necessary construction, the defective requirement may not be enforced. He would obtain that assessment on remand. He creates neither immunity for negligent practice nor an emergency based on unsupported preference, and would not impose a categorical injunction without the necessary findings. His additional remand position does not control the affirmed components.

**Justice Kennedy, concurring in part and dissenting in part; no joins.** Kennedy joins the independent definition and limited penalty holdings. He distinguishes the intelligibility of professional criteria from a ruling that every possible restriction on necessary care is constitutional. He would also affirm the access judgment under the reasonable-regulation position he stated in Casey, retaining protected choice and meaningful clinical safeguards but rejecting the general residual lesser-burden substantiation rule. Telephone disclosure, authorized agents, actual medical judgment and certification during the procedure visit distinguish the enacted duties from a mandatory second trip. On his approach, truthful information and a finite interval bear a reasonable relationship to informed decisionmaking, without an established third-party veto or concrete denial of necessary care on the preserved access claim. His view supplies no alternative rule for lower courts to substitute for the Court's controlling framework. He would deny restoration of the injunction and does not join the remand merely because lower courts retain ordinary remedial authority under the Court's mandate.

**Justice Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justice Thomas.** Scalia and Thomas join the definition and limited penalty portions because their grounds arise from the actual purposeful-termination words and specified professional criterion, independently of a judicially supplied fault term. They do not adopt a universal scienter cure, a bad-faith-only safe harbor or a rule that statutory silence invariably creates strict liability. They would affirm the access dismissal on their continuing constitutional objection to Casey's heightened abortion-access framework and the ordinary reasonable-regulation ground they supported there. At the actual telephone-and-agent scope, they regard consent information and the interval as permissible legislative regulation rather than a basis for the Court's residual scrutiny. This remains a dissent from governing law, not a finding that the Eighth Circuit performed the six-step inquiry. Their votes neither endorse misleading information or ideological assent nor permit invented criminal elements. They oppose the access remand and requested restoration of interim relief.
